# Environment and Ecology Flashcards

> One flashcard per examinable item in the repository Environment and Ecology owners (basic + advanced), plus a concept card per owner file. IDs: ENV-1001 onward by agent block.
> **Verification status:** built from the repository owners with claims checked against Wikipedia and other pages that loaded while building; confirmed items carry a ✅ source tag; unverified details are marked ⚠️. Owner-vs-source conflicts are flagged inside the cards. Time-sensitive facts carry as-of dates from the owner.

---

## ENVIRONMENT AND ECOLOGY — Part 1: Ecosystems, Biodiversity and Protected Areas

### GROUP — Ecosystem Structure and Function

### Flashcard ENV-1001 — Ecosystem: definition and coinage
**Front:** What is an ecosystem, and who coined the term?
**Definition (learn):** A self-regulating functional unit of interacting biotic communities and their abiotic (physical and chemical) environment that exchange energy and matter. The term was coined by the British ecologist A.G. Tansley in 1935.
**Breakdown:**
- Biotic part: producers, consumers (herbivores, carnivores, omnivores), decomposers and detritivores
- Abiotic part: climatic factors (temperature, light, rainfall) and edaphic factors (soil, minerals, topography); at biosphere scale, lithosphere, atmosphere and hydrosphere
- A functional unit is read through both structure (who is present) and function (what they do)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Tansley, 1935 (✅ owner 01 basic). Core proposition: energy flows one way, matter cycles (✅ owner 01 basic).
**Links:** ENV-1002 (components), ENV-1003 (energy vs matter), ENV-1023 (core theme)
**Trap:** An ecosystem is not only a list of species or a place on a map. The energy and matter exchange with the abiotic environment is part of the definition.
**Mains hook:** Any ecosystem question should begin with the structure-function pair. Qualified verdict: ecosystem health has no single agreed metric, so name the indicator used.
**Back:** *"Tansley 1935: living things plus their non-living surroundings, one working unit."*

### Flashcard ENV-1002 — Biotic and abiotic components
**Front:** Name the two components of an ecosystem and give one example of each.
**Definition (learn):** The biotic component is the living part; the abiotic component is the non-living physical and chemical part that the living part depends on.
**Breakdown:**
- Biotic: producers (plants, chemosynthetic bacteria), consumers, decomposers (bacteria, fungi)
- Abiotic: sunlight, temperature, rainfall, soil nutrients, water chemistry
- Standard framing: biotic subsystems are plants, animals (including humans) and micro-organisms
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner definitions (✅ owner 01 basic; NCERT Class XII Biology grounding per owner).
**Links:** ENV-1001, ENV-1009 (producers), ENV-1011 (decomposers)
**Trap:** Soil is abiotic, but soil organisms are biotic. Do not classify a soil fungus as abiotic.
**Mains hook:** Degradation of abiotic conditions (soil, water) can alter biotic function long before species loss is visible.
**Back:** *"Living = biotic; soil, climate, water = abiotic; the two are one system."*

### Flashcard ENV-1003 — Energy flows one way, matter cycles
**Front:** Why does energy flow one-way in an ecosystem while nutrients cycle?
**Definition (learn):** Energy enters as sunlight, is fixed by producers, passes along trophic levels and is progressively lost as metabolic heat; it cannot be recycled. Matter (nutrients) is returned to the abiotic pool by decomposers and reused.
**Breakdown:**
- Energy: producers fix it (only entry point), consumers transfer part, heat dissipates (second law of thermodynamics)
- Matter: decomposers release nutrients into soil, water or air; producers reabsorb them
- Consequence: pyramid of energy is always upright (see ENV-1035)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Core proposition (✅ owner 01 basic); second-law link (✅ owner 02 basic).
**Links:** ENV-1009, ENV-1014, ENV-1035
**Trap:** "Energy cycles like nutrients" is wrong. Only matter cycles; energy dissipates.
**Mains hook:** Argue that ecosystems need continuous solar input and that nutrient loops, not energy loops, are where human activity causes recycling failure (eutrophication, soil depletion).
**Back:** *"Energy goes in and leaves as heat; matter goes round and round."*

### Flashcard ENV-1004 — Habitat versus niche
**Front:** Distinguish habitat from niche.
**Definition (learn):** Habitat is the physical address where a species lives. Niche is its functional role: resource use, feeding and interactions, its "job" in the community.
**Breakdown:**
- Habitat: place (forest floor, mangrove, grassland)
- Niche: role (what it eats, how it breeds, what it competes for)
- Two species can share a habitat but occupy different niches
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic).
**Links:** ENV-1005 (three niche components), ENV-1019 (competitive exclusion)
**Trap:** Habitat and niche are not synonyms. Calling a species' habitat its niche loses the functional dimension examiners test.
**Mains hook:** Habitat loss matters most when it removes a niche that no alternative habitat can supply.
**Back:** *"Habitat is the address, niche is the occupation."*

### Flashcard ENV-1005 — Three components of a niche
**Front:** Into what three components is a niche decomposed in biogeography?
**Definition (learn):** The niche is broken into the habitat niche (where it lives), the trophic or food niche (how it feeds) and the reproductive niche (how it breeds).
**Breakdown:**
- Habitat niche: physical space used
- Trophic (food) niche: diet and feeding position
- Reproductive niche: breeding site, timing and behaviour
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Frequently examined three-part classification (✅ owner 01 basic).
**Links:** ENV-1004, ENV-1019
**Trap:** Do not confuse "trophic" with "tropical"; trophic refers to feeding level.
**Mains hook:** Two species may overlap in one niche dimension and still coexist because they differ in another (qualified: real coexistence also depends on resource timing).
**Back:** *"Where, what and when: habitat, trophic, reproductive."*

### Flashcard ENV-1006 — Ecotone and edge effect
**Front:** What is an ecotone, and what is the edge effect?
**Definition (learn):** An ecotone is a transition zone between two adjoining ecosystems (e.g., mangrove edge, grassland-forest margin), of variable width. The edge effect is the greater species density and variety found at such junctions compared with either neighbouring community.
**Breakdown:**
- Ecotone: a zone, not a line; width varies
- Competition zone: species of both communities meet and compete for resources
- Edge effect: higher richness than either bordering community because resources overlap
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic; ✅ owner 01 adv on overlap explanation).
**Links:** ENV-1004, ENV-1012
**Trap:** An ecotone is not simply a boundary line on a map, and the edge effect does not mean the edge is always healthier; edges can also be exposed to degradation.
**Mains hook:** Fragmented protected areas create many edges, which can increase edge-effect degradation of the core (qualified: depends on species and edge type).
**Back:** *"Ecotone is a zone of tension; the edge effect is a zone of richness."*

### Flashcard ENV-1007 — Biomass measured as net dry weight
**Front:** How is biomass measured in ecology?
**Definition (learn):** Biomass is the net dry weight of organic material, not fresh or wet weight. It is the material base that feeds the food chain.
**Breakdown:**
- Measured as dry organic matter, which removes water content variability
- Used as the basis for pyramids of biomass
- Standing biomass is a stock; productivity is a rate (distinct measures)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic; ✅ owner 02 basic on pyramid basis).
**Links:** ENV-1013 (productivity), ENV-1037 (biomass pyramid)
**Trap:** Biomass is not "live weight of organisms." Wet weight is the wrong basis for a biomass pyramid.
**Mains hook:** A small standing biomass can support a high turnover (phytoplankton), so biomass alone does not measure ecosystem productivity.
**Back:** *"Biomass is dry, net, organic: the food-chain stock."*

### Flashcard ENV-1008 — Life zones (altitudinal zonation)
**Front:** What is a life zone?
**Definition (learn):** A life zone is an altitudinal band in which plants and animals form distinctive communities, each shaped by its own temperature and precipitation regime.
**Breakdown:**
- Zones change with altitude on mountains
- Each zone has its own assemblage of vegetation and fauna
- Mountain biogeography in India (Himalaya, Western Ghats) shows clear zonation
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic). Himalayan altitudinal band use: see ENV-1085.
**Links:** ENV-1006, ENV-1063
**Trap:** Latitude zonation and altitudinal zonation are different gradients; a life zone is the altitudinal kind.
**Mains hook:** Warming shifts altitudinal bands upward, squeezing high-altitude species (qualified: a projection, not a settled measurement for every site).
**Back:** *"Climb a mountain, change the community: that band is a life zone."*

### Flashcard ENV-1009 — Producers and the energy entry point
**Front:** Which organisms are the sole entry point of energy into an ecosystem, and do they need sunlight?
**Definition (learn):** Producers (autotrophs) convert solar or chemical energy into food energy. Photosynthesis is the main pathway; chemosynthesis is used by some bacteria without sunlight.
**Breakdown:**
- Photosynthesis: green plants, algae, cyanobacteria
- Chemosynthesis: bacteria at hydrothermal vents use chemical energy
- Producers feed herbivores, which feed carnivores
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic).
**Links:** ENV-1003, ENV-1010 (food web)
**Trap:** Producers are not always green plants; chemosynthetic bacteria are producers without sunlight.
**Mains hook:** Loss of primary producers (e.g., phytoplankton decline) breaks the energy entry point for the whole food web (qualified: local effects vary).
**Back:** *"Autotrophs fix the first energy; no producers, no food web."*

### Flashcard ENV-1010 — Food chain versus food web
**Front:** Distinguish a food chain from a food web and state which is more stable.
**Definition (learn):** A food chain is a single linear sequence of who eats whom. A food web is the network of interconnected food chains in an ecosystem.
**Breakdown:**
- Food chain: linear, single path
- Food web: many interlinked paths
- Resilience: a web can absorb loss of one link through alternative links
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic). Chains rarely exceed about 4-5 links (✅ owner 01 adv; ecological efficiency basis).
**Links:** ENV-1009, ENV-1014, ENV-1015
**Trap:** The two terms are not interchangeable. A web is not a longer chain.
**Mains hook:** Simplified plantations or monocultures have short, thin webs; diversity buys resilience (qualified: resilience is recovery capacity, not immunity).
**Back:** *"A chain is one thread; a web holds if one thread snaps."*

### Flashcard ENV-1011 — Decomposers and detritivores
**Front:** Who are the decomposers and detritivores, and what does each do?
**Definition (learn):** Decomposers (mainly bacteria and fungi) chemically break down dead organic matter, releasing nutrients. Detritivores (e.g., earthworms, dung beetles) physically fragment detritus before microbial action.
**Breakdown:**
- Decomposers: bacteria and fungi, heterotrophic
- Detritivores: earthworms, dung beetles and similar animals
- Result: nutrients return to soil, water or air for producers
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic). Detritivore fragmentation step (✅ owner 01 basic).
**Links:** ENV-1003, ENV-1002
**Trap:** Decomposers are not only bacteria; fungi and detritivores are equally central.
**Mains hook:** Soil biota loss (a supporting service) reduces long-term fertility, which is why soil-organism decline matters to food security.
**Back:** *"Bacteria and fungi dissolve; worms and beetles shred first."*

### Flashcard ENV-1012 — Stratification and productivity as structural measures
**Front:** Name the two structural features that define how developed an ecosystem is.
**Definition (learn):** Stratification is the vertical layering of species (canopy, understorey, shrub, forest floor). Productivity is the rate of biomass generation.
**Breakdown:**
- Stratification: more layers generally mean more niches and more coexisting species
- Productivity: the rate at which biomass is produced
- Structure (stratification) enables function (productivity, nutrient cycling)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic; niche-differentiation logic ✅ owner 01 adv).
**Links:** ENV-1004, ENV-1013, ENV-1006
**Trap:** A structurally uniform monoculture can look orderly but has low stratification and low niche diversity.
**Mains hook:** Western Ghats montane forest stratification supports high endemism because each layer offers a distinct niche (⚠️ owner analytical linkage).
**Back:** *"Layers make niches; niches make species; rate is productivity."*

### Flashcard ENV-1013 — GPP, respiration and NPP
**Front:** Write the relation between gross primary productivity, respiration and net primary productivity.
**Definition (learn):** Net primary productivity (NPP) equals gross primary productivity (GPP) minus the respiratory loss by producers: NPP = GPP − R. NPP is the biomass actually available to consumers.
**Breakdown:**
- GPP: total fixation of energy by producers
- R: energy the producers use for their own respiration
- NPP: what remains for herbivores and decomposers
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Formula (✅ owner 01 adv, Must-Know list).
**Links:** ENV-1012, ENV-1014, ENV-1007
**Trap:** NPP is not GPP. Quoting gross figures as available food energy overstates what consumers receive.
**Mains hook:** Productivity figures for an ecosystem should state whether they are gross or net before comparing sites.
**Back:** *"Gross minus own breathing equals net food for the rest."*

### Flashcard ENV-1014 — Ecological efficiency and the roughly 10 percent rule
**Front:** What fraction of energy is transferred between trophic levels on average, and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** On average only about 10 percent of the energy of one trophic level is transferred to the next; the rest is lost mainly as metabolic heat. This is ecological efficiency and it explains short food chains and upright energy pyramids.
**Breakdown:**
- Loss mechanisms: respiration, metabolic heat, non-assimilated waste
- Consequence: roughly 90 percent is lost at each transfer
- Limit: it is an average heuristic; it varies by taxon and ecosystem (⚠️ owner 02 adv)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** About 10 percent average (✅ owner 01 adv; ✅ owner 01 basic on heat loss).
**Links:** ENV-1003, ENV-1010, ENV-1035
**Trap:** Transfer efficiency is not close to 100 percent; about 90 percent is lost. The 10 percent figure is not a fixed law.
**Mains hook:** Use the 10 percent logic to argue why apex predators are rare and why energy-based pyramids are always upright (qualified: average, not universal).
**Back:** *"One tenth goes up; nine tenths leaves as heat."*

### Flashcard ENV-1015 — Stability versus resilience
**Front:** Distinguish ecological stability from resilience, and give a monoculture example.
**Definition (learn):** Stability is the capacity to resist change. Resilience is the capacity to recover after a disturbance. They are distinct properties.
**Breakdown:**
- Stable-looking: a uniform plantation can appear orderly
- Resilient: a diverse web recovers after pest or drought stress
- Monoculture plantation: structurally uniform but fragile to pests and drought (⚠️ owner analytical reading)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 adv).
**Links:** ENV-1010, ENV-1012, ENV-1050 (succession)
**Trap:** A stable-looking ecosystem is not automatically resilient.
**Mains hook:** Afforestation with a single species can score high on area but low on resilience; judge plantations by recovery capacity.
**Back:** *"Stability resists; resilience bounces back."*

### Flashcard ENV-1016 — Four ecosystem-service categories (MEA)
**Front:** List the four categories of ecosystem services in the Millennium Ecosystem Assessment framework, with one loss example each.
**Definition (learn):** The Millennium Ecosystem Assessment (2005) classified ecosystem services into provisioning, regulating, supporting and cultural services.
**Breakdown:**
- Provisioning: food, timber, water, fibre (loss example: fishery collapse from wetland loss)
- Regulating: flood buffering, pollination, climate moderation (mangrove loss raises cyclone-surge damage)
- Supporting: nutrient cycling, soil formation, primary production (soil biota loss reduces fertility)
- Cultural: recreation, spiritual, aesthetic, scientific value (sacred-grove loss weakens community conservation)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** MEA 2005 framework (✅ owner 01 adv, standard MEA structure ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-1017 (NCP reframing), ENV-1018 (carrying capacity), ENV-1021 (MISHTI)
**Trap:** Supporting services are not the same as provisioning services; the first underpin all others.
**Mains hook:** For "value of an ecosystem" questions, name all four categories and flag which one an EIA usually fails to price (qualified: monetisation is hard).
**Back:** *"Provide, regulate, support, enjoy: the four service boxes."*

### Flashcard ENV-1017 — Nature's Contributions to People (IPBES)
**Front:** What reframing did IPBES introduce for ecosystem services, and why?
**Definition (learn):** IPBES (Intergovernmental Science-Policy Platform on Biodiversity and Ecosystem Services) reframed the MEA "ecosystem services" as "Nature's Contributions to People" (NCP). The reframing includes relational and indigenous or local-knowledge values alongside instrumental, market-type values.
**Breakdown:**
- MEA: four-fold instrumental classification
- NCP: broader lens including relational values
- Effect: "ecosystem services" is no longer purely an economic-valuation concept
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 adv; IPBES name ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-1016
**Trap:** Do not present NCP as a replacement for the four categories; it widens them.
**Mains hook:** Use NCP to argue that sacred groves and community conservation have value that market pricing misses (qualified: NCP is a framing, not a valuation method).
**Back:** *"Services became Contributions: nature's value includes meaning, not only money."*

### Flashcard ENV-1018 — Carrying capacity (2019 GS-III PYQ route)
**Front:** How should carrying capacity be defined for an ecosystem answer?
**Definition (learn):** Carrying capacity is the population or project load an ecosystem can sustain over time without eroding its regenerative functions. It is a conditional threshold, not a fixed headcount.
**Breakdown:**
- Regenerative supply, assimilative capacity, habitat connectivity, institutional safeguards (EIA, forest and protected-area law)
- Consumption pattern, technology and governance shift the threshold
- 20-mark spine: compare resilient mangrove or wetland with a simplified plantation or sealed urban catchment
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Routed PYQ: 2019 GS-III Q17, 15 marks, 250 words, "ecosystem carrying capacity concept and sustainable development planning" (✅ owner 01 basic and adv PYQ ledger).
**Links:** ENV-1016, ENV-1015
**Trap:** Carrying capacity is not a fixed population number that applies everywhere.
**Mains hook:** Conclude that governance and technology change the threshold, but do not abolish ecological limits.
**Back:** *"Carrying capacity: the load you can carry without breaking the carrier."*

### Flashcard ENV-1019 — Competitive exclusion and resource partitioning
**Front:** What is competitive exclusion, and how does resource partitioning respond to it?
**Definition (learn):** Two species with identical niches in the same habitat cannot coexist indefinitely; one excludes the other. Resource partitioning is the division of resources (food, space, time) that lets similar species coexist.
**Breakdown:**
- Competitive exclusion: identical niche, one species eliminated
- Resource partitioning: different feeding times, zones or diets
- Stratification gives more niches, hence more coexisting species
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic).
**Links:** ENV-1004, ENV-1005, ENV-1012
**Trap:** Coexistence does not mean identical niches; it means differentiated niches.
**Mains hook:** Introducing a non-native species can disrupt partitioning and cause local extinctions (qualified: outcome depends on species and site).
**Back:** *"Same niche, one wins; split the niche, both stay."*

### Flashcard ENV-1020 — Institutions for ecosystem science and policy
**Front:** Which Indian institutions underpin ecosystem and species baseline data, and what does each do?
**Definition (learn):** MoEFCC is the apex ministry for ecosystem and biodiversity policy and for implementing international conventions. WII (Dehradun) does ecosystem and species research for management plans. BSI (Botanical Survey of India) and ZSI (Zoological Survey of India) conduct taxonomic and ecological surveys of flora and fauna.
**Breakdown:**
- MoEFCC: policy and convention implementation
- WII (Dehradun): research supporting management, population assessments
- BSI and ZSI: flora and fauna inventories and taxonomy
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic and adv section 4).
**Links:** ENV-1076 (biodiversity levels), ENV-1129 (protected-area categories)
**Trap:** BSI and ZSI do not manage protected areas; they generate the baseline data others use.
**Mains hook:** Institutions produce data but enforcement sits with state agencies and courts; name the gap in any institutional answer.
**Back:** *"Policy at MoEFCC, research at WII, inventories at BSI and ZSI."*

### Flashcard ENV-1021 — MISHTI mangrove restoration
**Front:** What is MISHTI and what are its headline targets as stated in the owner?
**Definition (learn):** MISHTI (Mangrove Initiative for Shoreline Habitats and Tangible Incomes) is India's coastal mangrove restoration initiative: structure (mangrove zonation) purchased for function (surge buffering, fish nursery, blue carbon).
**Breakdown:**
- Target (owner-stated): about 540 sq km restoration across 9 coastal States and 4 UTs, 2023-2028
- Owner-stated employment: about 22.8 million person-days; estimated carbon sink about 4.5 million tonnes
- These are programme targets and estimates, not audited outcomes
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner-only, from Economic Survey 2025-26 Ch. 10 as cited in owner 01 adv; not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-1016, ENV-1022, ENV-1190 (Amrit Dharohar)
**Trap:** Say "targets" or "envisages", not "has restored", unless a completion figure is cited.
**Mains hook:** A worked example of ecosystem structure bought as adaptation infrastructure (qualified: target vs achievement).
**Back:** *"Mangroves bought for surge, fish and carbon: targets, not yet results."*

### Flashcard ENV-1022 — ISFR 2023 forest and tree cover (time-varying)
**Front:** What did the owner report as India's total forest and tree cover in ISFR 2023, and what is the caveat?
**Definition (learn):** The India State of Forest Report (ISFR) 2023 (released December 2024) is the FSI biennial report; the owner reports total forest and tree cover of 8,27,357 sq km, or 25.17 percent of geographical area (forest cover 21.76 percent, tree cover 3.41 percent).
**Breakdown:**
- Forest cover and tree cover are reported together
- Measure is canopy extent, not composition, age or function
- ISFR is biennial, so check the newest edition before quoting
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner-only (owner 01 basic section 8; ISFR is an FSI publication), not fetched. Cite the edition and year.
**Links:** ENV-1073 (ISFR limitation), ENV-1038
**Trap:** Forest-cover growth does not prove ecosystem health or native-forest recovery.
**Mains hook:** Use canopy extent as a baseline, then flag the quality distinction (qualified: extent is not quality).
**Back:** *"Canopy extent is not forest quality."*

### Flashcard ENV-1023 — Core theme: structure and function
**Front:** What is the core analytical thesis of the ecosystem-structure topic?
**Definition (learn):** Treat ecosystem structure (components and their arrangement) and function (energy flow, matter cycling, resilience) as two faces of one system. Evaluate any disturbance by asking which structural link a critical function depends on.
**Breakdown:**
- Structure: components, stratification, food-web links
- Function: one-way energy, cycling matter, productivity, resilience
- Test: does the disturbance break a link the function needs?
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Answer thesis (✅ owner 01 basic section 10).
**Links:** ENV-1001, ENV-1003, ENV-1012, ENV-1015
**Trap:** Protecting one flagship species is not the same as protecting ecosystem structure.
**Mains hook:** Ecosystem-based management protects structure to preserve function, not a single species (qualified: site-specific data for many Indian ecosystems is thin).
**Back:** *"Structure enables function; break the link, lose the service."*

### Flashcard ENV-1024 — PYQ route: fig pollination, parasitoids and oxygen sources
**Front:** Which recent Prelims ecosystem demands are routed to this topic, and what must the answer rule be?
**Definition (learn):** Routed Prelims demands in the owner are: 2024 GS-I Q18 (parasitoid species among organisms), 2024 GS-I Q28 (tree uniquely pollinated by a coevolved insect, i.e., fig), and 2025 GS-I Q40 (sources of the planet's oxygen: rainforests, phytoplankton, surface water).
**Breakdown:**
- Parasitoids: organisms that live on or in a host and kill it (concept, not a list answer)
- Fig: pollinated by coevolved fig wasps (⚠️ standard fact)
- Oxygen question: know which producers release oxygen through photosynthesis; the owner records the demand but not the answer key
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner PYQ ledgers (✅ owner 01 basic PYQ integration). Answer keys are held locally but not recorded in the owner; no answer is inferred here.
**Links:** ENV-1009, ENV-1011, ENV-1025
**Trap:** Do not answer from memory where the official key is available; check the official Set-A key.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains demand. Use for statement-level elimination only.
**Back:** *"Fig, parasite, oxygen: check the key, don't guess."*

### Flashcard ENV-1025 — Keystone species and trophic cascade
**Front:** What is a trophic cascade, and why is apex-predator loss a structure-function risk?
**Definition (learn):** A trophic cascade is an indirect effect that propagates through a food web when a top predator is removed or added, changing the abundance of species several links down. Keystone species have disproportionate effects relative to their abundance.
**Breakdown:**
- Apex predator removal can release herbivores, which overgraze vegetation
- Overgrazing can contribute to desertification (⚠️ owner analytical example)
- Qualification: well-evidenced globally, thinly documented for many Indian ecosystems
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 01 basic and adv section 5, qualitative).
**Links:** ENV-1010, ENV-1023, ENV-1012
**Trap:** A keystone species is not necessarily the largest or the most abundant.
**Mains hook:** Qualify keystone claims as illustrative for Indian sites unless site data are cited.
**Back:** *"Take the top, the middle shifts, the bottom pays."*

### GROUP — Biogeochemical Cycles and Ecological Pyramids

### Flashcard ENV-1026 — Biogeochemical cycle
**Front:** Define a biogeochemical cycle.
**Definition (learn):** A biogeochemical cycle is the circulation of a chemical element or compound between living organisms and the physical environment (air, water, rock, soil).
**Breakdown:**
- Living biomass exchanges elements with abiotic reservoirs
- Two families: gaseous cycles (atmosphere or ocean reservoir) and sedimentary cycles (crust reservoir)
- Pyramids quantify the biomass at each trophic level at one point in time (see ENV-1035 to ENV-1038)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic).
**Links:** ENV-1027, ENV-1048
**Trap:** A cycle is a continuous circulation, not a one-way sink.
**Mains hook:** Human acceleration changes the rate of cycling, not the existence of the cycle (qualified: rate and balance matter).
**Back:** *"Element out of rock or air, through life, and back again."*

### Flashcard ENV-1027 — Gaseous versus sedimentary cycles
**Front:** Which elements follow gaseous cycles and which follow sedimentary cycles?
**Definition (learn):** Gaseous cycles have the atmosphere or ocean as main reservoir: carbon, nitrogen, oxygen and water. Sedimentary cycles have the earth's crust as main reservoir: phosphorus, sulphur and calcium. Gaseous cycles are faster and self-regulating; sedimentary cycles are slower and more easily disrupted.
**Breakdown:**
- Gaseous: C, N, O, water (fast atmospheric exchange)
- Sedimentary: P, S, Ca (weathering and mining-driven exchange)
- Reversibility: gaseous excess can be partly scrubbed from air; phosphorus loss is hard to recover
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic and adv section 7).
**Links:** ENV-1028, ENV-1031, ENV-1032
**Trap:** Phosphorus is not a gaseous cycle; it has no significant atmospheric phase.
**Mains hook:** Carbon, nitrogen and phosphorus create different externalities and need different regulation (qualified: sulphur acid deposition is a distinct case).
**Back:** *"Gas: C, N, O, water. Rock: P, S, Ca."*

### Flashcard ENV-1028 — Carbon cycle
**Front:** Describe the carbon cycle and the main sinks and sources.
**Definition (learn):** Producers fix atmospheric or dissolved CO2 by photosynthesis; respiration, decomposition and combustion return it. Oceans and forests are major sinks.
**Breakdown:**
- Fixation: photosynthesis
- Release: respiration, decomposition, fossil-fuel combustion
- Human acceleration: fossil-fuel combustion, deforestation, cement production (adv)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic; adv table ✅ owner 02 adv).
**Links:** ENV-1027, ENV-1044, ENV-1047
**Trap:** Forests and oceans are sinks, not infinite stores; deforestation converts sinks into sources.
**Mains hook:** Carbon-cycle science is the scientific spine of the climate-policy stack (qualified: global cycle, not India-only measurement).
**Back:** *"Plants take it in; breathing, rotting and burning give it back."*

### Flashcard ENV-1029 — Nitrogen fixation and fixers
**Front:** Name the routes of nitrogen fixation and one bacterium for each main biological route.
**Definition (learn):** Nitrogen fixation converts inert atmospheric N2 into usable ammonia or nitrate. Routes: biological (bacteria, free-living or symbiotic), atmospheric (lightning), and industrial (Haber-Bosch).
**Breakdown:**
- Free-living bacteria: Azotobacter (owner-listed)
- Symbiotic bacteria: Rhizobium in legume root nodules
- Cyanobacteria also fix nitrogen; plants do not fix nitrogen themselves
- Industrial: Haber-Bosch synthetic fertiliser (see ENV-1040)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic, Must-Know section; cyanobacteria listed in owner trap ✅).
**Links:** ENV-1030, ENV-1040, ENV-1049
**Trap:** Nitrogen fixation is not done by plants themselves, and a legume does not fix nitrogen alone; the nodule bacteria do it.
**Mains hook:** Legume-based crop rotation uses biological fixation to reduce synthetic input (qualified: effect depends on crop and soil).
**Back:** *"Rhizobium in the nodule, Azotobacter in the soil, lightning in the storm, Haber in the factory."*

### Flashcard ENV-1030 — Nitrogen steps: ammonification, nitrification, denitrification
**Front:** Give the sequence of microbial steps that follow nitrogen fixation, with the organisms named in the owner.
**Definition (learn):** Ammonification: decomposers convert organic nitrogen to ammonia. Nitrification: Nitrosomonas oxidises ammonia to nitrite, and Nitrobacter oxidises nitrite to nitrate. Denitrification: Pseudomonas-type bacteria reduce nitrate back to N2, returning it to the atmosphere.
**Breakdown:**
- Ammonification (decomposers)
- Nitrification (Nitrosomonas then Nitrobacter)
- Denitrification (returns N2 to air)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic, Must-Know list).
**Links:** ENV-1029, ENV-1033
**Trap:** Do not reverse nitrification and denitrification. Nitrification makes nitrate, denitrification removes it as N2.
**Mains hook:** Excess nitrate from fertiliser runoff bypasses the loop and drives eutrophication (qualified: denitrification in wetlands can remove some of it).
**Back:** *"Ammonia, then nitrite by Nitrosomonas, then nitrate by Nitrobacter, then N2 back by denitrifiers."*

### Flashcard ENV-1031 — Phosphorus cycle
**Front:** Why is phosphorus a sedimentary cycle, and why is it often the limiting nutrient?
**Definition (learn):** Phosphorus cycles through rock weathering, uptake by plants, passage through consumers and decomposers, and eventual re-sedimentation. It has no significant atmospheric phase, so it is often the limiting nutrient.
**Breakdown:**
- Source: weathering of phosphate rock
- Path: plants, consumers, decomposers, sediment
- No gaseous buffer, so loss is hard to reverse
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic; 2021 Prelims routed demand in owner PYQ ledger).
**Links:** ENV-1027, ENV-1042, ENV-1043, ENV-1049
**Trap:** Phosphorus scarcity and phosphorus excess are both possible; do not confuse the cycle with a carbon-type atmospheric buffer.
**Mains hook:** Phosphorus stewardship is a distinct policy category from carbon mitigation (qualified: limiting nutrient depends on the ecosystem).
**Back:** *"Rock to plant to sediment: no air, no quick reset."*

### Flashcard ENV-1032 — Sulphur cycle and acid deposition
**Front:** Name the main human acceleration pathway for the sulphur cycle and its problem.
**Definition (learn):** Sulphur is a sedimentary cycle (listed in owner basic). Its advanced entry links human acceleration to fossil-fuel, especially coal, combustion, producing acid deposition that affects soils and monuments.
**Breakdown:**
- Natural sources: volcanic and biogenic emission; sedimentation
- Human source: coal and fossil-fuel combustion
- Problem: acid deposition on soils and monuments
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sulphur as sedimentary (✅ owner 02 basic); acid-deposition link (⚠️ owner 02 adv analytical entry).
**Links:** ENV-1027, ENV-1028
**Trap:** The acid-deposition link is an owner analytical point, not a Must-Know Prelims fact; do not present it as a settled Prelims statement.
**Mains hook:** Coal combustion links sulphur and carbon problems in one energy-transition argument (qualified).
**Back:** *"Coal burns sulphur into acid rain on rock and monuments."*

### Flashcard ENV-1033 — Eutrophication: natural versus cultural
**Front:** Distinguish natural eutrophication from cultural eutrophication.
**Definition (learn):** Eutrophication is nutrient and sediment enrichment of a water body. Natural eutrophication is the slow ageing of a lake through gradual filling. Cultural (anthropogenic) eutrophication is the accelerated form caused by sewage and fertiliser runoff.
**Breakdown:**
- Natural: gradual lake ageing
- Cultural: sewage and fertiliser runoff, accelerated
- Owner exam point: the two are not synonyms
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic, definition and Must-Know list).
**Links:** ENV-1034, ENV-1030, ENV-1046
**Trap:** Eutrophication is not always man-made; natural lake ageing is a process too. Only the accelerated form is cultural.
**Mains hook:** Treat cultural eutrophication as a governance problem of input control, not only of downstream treatment (qualified: point and diffuse sources differ).
**Back:** *"Old lake ages slowly; sewage and fertiliser age it fast."*

### Flashcard ENV-1034 — Eutrophication effects and oxygen depletion
**Front:** What chain of events follows nutrient overload in a water body?
**Definition (learn):** Excess nitrogen and phosphorus cause algal blooms; when the algae die, decomposer activity spikes, dissolved oxygen falls, and fish kills follow.
**Breakdown:**
- Nutrient overload: N and P from runoff or sewage
- Algal bloom, then die-off
- Decomposer respiration depletes dissolved oxygen, causing fish kills
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic Must-Know; ✅ owner 02 adv section 7).
**Links:** ENV-1033, ENV-1046
**Trap:** Eutrophication is not a sign of a healthy nutrient-rich ecosystem; it signals overload and oxygen loss.
**Mains hook:** Link water-quality monitoring (CPCB parameters such as BOD and dissolved oxygen) to nutrient inputs (qualified: national totals are modelled estimates).
**Back:** *"Bloom, die, decompose, suffocate."*

### Flashcard ENV-1035 — Pyramid of energy is always upright
**Front:** Which ecological pyramid is always upright, and why?
**Definition (learn):** The pyramid of energy is always upright because energy dissipates at each transfer (second law of thermodynamics), so each lower level must hold more energy than the one above.
**Breakdown:**
- Energy at each level is smaller than the level below
- Rests on the roughly 10 percent transfer rule (ENV-1014)
- Captures how much life a level can support
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic and adv section 3).
**Links:** ENV-1014, ENV-1036, ENV-1037
**Trap:** Only the energy pyramid is always upright. Number and biomass pyramids can invert.
**Mains hook:** Use the energy pyramid for "how much life can a level support" questions (qualified: 10 percent is an average).
**Back:** *"Energy always falls going up; that pyramid never flips."*

### Flashcard ENV-1036 — Pyramid of numbers: upright or inverted
**Front:** When is the pyramid of numbers inverted? Give the classic example.
**Definition (learn):** A pyramid of numbers plots the count of organisms at each trophic level. It is upright in a grassland (many insects, fewer birds, fewer predators) and inverted when a single large producer supports many consumers, such as one large tree hosting many insects.
**Breakdown:**
- Upright: grassland (producers many, consumers fewer)
- Inverted: single tree with many insects
- Shape depends on ecosystem and parameter
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic and adv section 3).
**Links:** ENV-1035, ENV-1037
**Trap:** Numbers can invert while energy does not. Do not treat a numbers inversion as an energy anomaly.
**Mains hook:** Use pyramids as diagnostics for a specific measure, not as a universal shape (qualified).
**Back:** *"One big tree, many bugs: the number pyramid flips."*

### Flashcard ENV-1037 — Pyramid of biomass and standing crop
**Front:** Why is the biomass pyramid often inverted in aquatic systems?
**Definition (learn):** A pyramid of biomass plots standing dry organic mass per level. It is usually upright on land but commonly inverted in aquatic systems, because small, fast-reproducing phytoplankton hold little standing biomass yet support larger consumer biomass through rapid turnover.
**Breakdown:**
- Standing crop (biomass) vs turnover rate (productivity)
- Aquatic inversion reflects fast replenishment, not dysfunction
- Only the energy pyramid captures the full throughput
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic and adv section 3).
**Links:** ENV-1007, ENV-1035, ENV-1036
**Trap:** An inverted biomass pyramid does not mean an unhealthy ecosystem.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish standing stock from productivity before concluding an ecosystem is degraded.
**Back:** *"Little phytoplankton standing, big turnover: the biomass pyramid flips."*

### Flashcard ENV-1038 — Pyramids as point-in-time snapshots
**Front:** What does an ecological pyramid show, and what does it not show?
**Definition (learn):** An ecological pyramid is a graphical representation of number, biomass or energy at successive trophic levels at one point in time. It is a snapshot of a continuously flowing system.
**Breakdown:**
- Shows: level-by-level quantity at one time
- Does not show: flows, seasonal shifts, omnivory, multiple overlapping food webs
- Use the energy pyramid for throughput questions
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 basic; ✅ owner 02 adv section 6 on simplification).
**Links:** ENV-1026, ENV-1035, ENV-1010
**Trap:** A pyramid is a teaching simplification, not a law of shape.
**Mains hook:** Cite the simplification when a question asks about real ecosystem complexity.
**Back:** *"A pyramid is a photo, not a film."*

### Flashcard ENV-1039 — Nitrous oxide as a nitrogen-cycle by-product
**Front:** Why is nitrous oxide (N2O) a favourite cross-topic link for the nitrogen cycle?
**Definition (learn):** N2O is a nitrogen-cycle by-product of fertilised soils. The owner states it is a potent greenhouse gas and a significant stratospheric ozone-depleting substance not controlled by the Montreal Protocol.
**Breakdown:**
- Source: fertilised soil (nitrogen cycle)
- Effect 1: potent greenhouse gas
- Effect 2: ozone depletion (owner, per ozone literature)
- Control gap: not covered by the Montreal Protocol (⚠️ owner-stated; verify before quoting)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner basic section 6 (✅ owner 02 basic; the Montreal Protocol point is ⚠️ literature claim, not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-1030, ENV-1040, ENV-1028
**Trap:** Montreal Protocol governs ozone-depleting substances; check its scope before saying N2O is included. Do not confuse with Montreux Record (ENV-1181).
**Mains hook:** Nitrogen cycling links ozone and climate governance, which are separate treaties (qualified).
**Back:** *"Fertiliser breathes out a greenhouse gas that also eats ozone."*

### Flashcard ENV-1040 — Haber-Bosch as the largest human nitrogen addition
**Front:** What is the largest anthropogenic addition to the nitrogen cycle?
**Definition (learn):** The Haber-Bosch process is industrial nitrogen fixation making ammonia from atmospheric N2, used for synthetic fertiliser. It is the largest human addition to the nitrogen cycle.
**Breakdown:**
- Input: atmospheric N2 converted to ammonia industrially
- Use: synthetic fertiliser
- Fate: runoff causes eutrophication; N2O emissions from soil (ENV-1039)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 02 adv section 7).
**Links:** ENV-1029, ENV-1039, ENV-1043
**Trap:** Haber-Bosch is not a plant process and not a biological fixation route.
**Mains hook:** Argue for precision nutrient management to protect yields while cutting runoff (qualified: trade-offs with food security).
**Back:** *"The factory fixes more nitrogen than any bacterium now."*

### Flashcard ENV-1041 — Planetary boundaries and nutrient flows
**Front:** Why does the owner say nitrogen and phosphorus flows are judged more transgressed than the climate boundary?
**Definition (learn):** In the planetary-boundaries reading, biogeochemical flows are a boundary. The owner states that the nitrogen and phosphorus boundary is generally assessed as more transgressed than the climate boundary, though climate gets more policy attention.
**Breakdown:**
- Attention asymmetry: carbon has UNFCCC, markets and IPCC; reactive N and P lack an equivalent treaty
- Soft instruments: UNEP resolutions; Colombo Declaration aspirational halving of nitrogen waste (owner-stated)
- Difference lies in governance architecture, not severity (owner)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical reading (owner 02 adv section 2A); planetary-boundaries framing not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-1027, ENV-1042, ENV-1044
**Trap:** Do not assume nutrient pollution is smaller than climate change because it gets less attention.
**Mains hook:** Nutrient governance needs cycle-specific instruments (qualified).
**Back:** *"Less policy, more transgressed: the nutrient gap is governance, not size."*

### Flashcard ENV-1042 — Phosphate has no substitute for food security
**Front:** Why is a phosphorus constraint described as a food-security constraint rather than only an emissions problem?
**Definition (learn):** Phosphate rock has no substitute as a plant nutrient, so a phosphorus constraint directly limits food production. Phosphate reserves are also geographically concentrated, putting nutrient security alongside critical-mineral security.
**Breakdown:**
- Carbon has substitutes (renewables, nuclear); phosphate has none as a nutrient
- Geographic concentration of reserves creates supply risk
- Peak phosphorus: a long-term resource scarcity risk (owner adv)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical reading (owner 02 adv section 2A and section 5); Economic Survey critical-mineral framing per owner.
**Links:** ENV-1031, ENV-1041, ENV-1043
**Trap:** Phosphorus is not interchangeable with carbon mitigation options; its policy category is distinct.
**Mains hook:** Treat fertiliser feedstock as a strategic input alongside critical minerals (qualified: depends on reserve data).
**Back:** *"No substitute for phosphate: scarcity is a food problem."*

### Flashcard ENV-1043 — Fertiliser subsidy skew: urea versus NPK
**Front:** What implementation gap does the owner flag in India's fertiliser subsidy design?
**Definition (learn):** The owner states that India's fertiliser subsidy design has historically favoured nitrogenous fertiliser (urea) price control over balanced nitrogen-phosphorus-potassium (NPK) use. This skews soil nutrient ratios and raises eutrophication risk in intensive river basins.
**Breakdown:**
- Lever: Department of Fertilizers and Ministry of Agriculture and Farmers Welfare
- Problem: urea-skewed use, unbalanced NPK
- Consequence: nutrient runoff into intensive basins
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical critique (owner 02 adv section 4); not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-1040, ENV-1033, ENV-1046
**Trap:** The subsidy is not the same as a ban on fertiliser; the critique is about ratio and balance.
**Mains hook:** Precision and soil-test-based dosing can protect yields and reduce runoff (qualified: needs state-level delivery).
**Back:** *"Urea cheap, NPK neglected: the ratio is the problem."*

### Flashcard ENV-1044 — IPCC AR6 and the 1.1 degree observed warming
**Front:** What did the IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report (2023) state about observed warming, and what is the caveat?
**Definition (learn):** The owner cites the IPCC AR6 Synthesis Report (2023) for roughly 1.1 degree Celsius of observed warming above pre-industrial levels, driven overwhelmingly by anthropogenic CO2.
**Breakdown:**
- Use: evidence of human-scale carbon-cycle acceleration
- Scale: global science output, not an Indian measurement
- Date: state with the AR6 year (2023)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner-cited (owner 02 basic section 8 and adv section 9); not fetched in this session. Time-varying.
**Links:** ENV-1028, ENV-1041, ENV-1045
**Trap:** Do not present a global warming figure as an India-specific measurement.
**Mains hook:** Climate policy traces back to this carbon-cycle evidence (qualified: date-stamp the figure).
**Back:** *"One point one degrees, AR6, global: not an Indian thermometer."*

### Flashcard ENV-1045 — IPCC AR7 carbon dioxide removal inventory methodology
**Front:** What is the IPCC AR7 methodology item on carbon removal, and why does it matter?
**Definition (learn):** The owner states the IPCC seventh assessment cycle includes a 2027 Methodology Report on Carbon Dioxide Removal Technologies, CCUS for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories, whose content was agreed at IPCC-63 (Lima, October 2025).
**Breakdown:**
- Shift: carbon removals become an accounting object needing inventory rules
- Link: Topic 21 carbon markets and CCUS
- Date: owner verified as on 2 August 2026
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner-only, time-sensitive (owner 02 adv section 9). Not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-1044, ENV-1028
**Trap:** Removal methodology is not the same as a measured removal result.
**Mains hook:** Removals need inventory and verification rules before they count in climate policy (qualified).
**Back:** *"Removals need rulebooks, not just science."*

### Flashcard ENV-1046 — Ganga-basin nutrient runoff example
**Front:** What Indian example does the owner give for eutrophication from fertiliser runoff?
**Definition (learn):** Excess nitrogen and phosphorus fertiliser runoff into rivers and lakes, in parts of the Ganga basin, causes eutrophication and algal blooms.
**Breakdown:**
- Source: fertiliser and sewage runoff
- Effect: algal blooms, oxygen depletion
- Link: Topic 14 water-pollution governance
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical example (owner 02 basic section 5); not fetched with site data.
**Links:** ENV-1033, ENV-1034, ENV-1043
**Trap:** Name the basin and the mechanism, not a general claim of "Ganga is polluted" without data.
**Mains hook:** Use the Ganga example for nutrient input control (qualified: site data needed).
**Back:** *"Fields feed the river; algae feed on the field."*

### Flashcard ENV-1047 — Blue and brown carbon sinks
**Front:** What do "blue carbon" and "brown carbon" refer to in the owner's examples?
**Definition (learn):** The owner uses blue carbon for carbon stored in mangrove sinks and brown carbon for carbon in peatlands, both outside standard forest-carbon accounting.
**Breakdown:**
- Mangroves: blue carbon (owner-cited Sundarbans example elsewhere)
- Peatlands: brown carbon (owner usage)
- Lesser-known carbon-cycle pathways
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical linkage (owner 02 basic section 5). Terminology is owner usage; verify against a dated source before quoting.
**Links:** ENV-1028, ENV-1021 (MISHTI), ENV-1044
**Trap:** Do not treat blue and brown carbon as the same class; the owner uses them for different ecosystems.
**Mains hook:** Coastal and peatland sinks are a reason to count wetlands as climate infrastructure (qualified).
**Back:** *"Mangrove blue, peat brown: sinks outside the forest ledger."*

### Flashcard ENV-1048 — Core theme: biogeochemical cycles and pyramids
**Front:** What is the single thesis for answers on cycles and pyramids?
**Definition (learn):** Human activity has not created new cycles; it has altered the rate at which gaseous and sedimentary cycles operate. Problems differ by cycle: climate change from carbon, eutrophication from nitrogen and phosphorus. Responses must be cycle-specific.
**Breakdown:**
- Step 1: classify reservoir (gaseous or sedimentary)
- Step 2: identify human acceleration (combustion, synthetic fertiliser, mining)
- Step 3: name the Indian lever and its gap
- Step 4: use pyramids only when standing stock or throughput is asked
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Answer thesis (✅ owner 02 basic section 10 and adv section 11).
**Links:** ENV-1026, ENV-1027, ENV-1035, ENV-1043
**Trap:** Do not use a pyramid to answer a cycle question unless trophic stock is asked.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish at least two failure modes (climate versus eutrophication) to show range.
**Back:** *"Cycle, rate, reservoir, lever: four steps before any answer."*

### Flashcard ENV-1049 — PYQ route: nitrogen compounds, phosphorus source and nitrogen-fixing plants
**Front:** Which cycle-related Prelims demands are routed to this owner, and what must the answer rule be?
**Definition (learn):** Routed demands: 2019 GS-I Q41 (nitrogen compounds released from agricultural and livestock activities), 2021 GS-I Q27 (phosphorus cycle and rock weathering as source), and 2022 GS-I Q48 (nitrogen-fixing plant species identification in agriculture).
**Breakdown:**
- Nitrogen releases: agriculture and livestock (cross-routed to externality owner)
- Phosphorus: rock weathering is the natural source
- Nitrogen-fixing plants: legumes with Rhizobium nodules
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner PYQ ledgers (✅ owner 02 basic PYQ integration). Official keys are not held locally in the owner; no answer is inferred.
**Links:** ENV-1029, ENV-1031, ENV-1039
**Trap:** Do not answer from memory where an official key exists; use statement-level elimination.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains demand; use for statement elimination.
**Back:** *"Agriculture releases nitrogen; rock feeds phosphorus; legumes fix it."*

### GROUP — Ecological Succession and Biomes

### Flashcard ENV-1050 — Ecological succession
**Front:** Define ecological succession.
**Definition (learn):** Ecological succession is the gradual, predictable change in the species composition of a community over time, moving toward a relatively stable endpoint.
**Breakdown:**
- Direction: from pioneer to intermediate to mature communities
- Each stage modifies the environment, often favouring the next (facilitation)
- Diagnostic (owner): later communities are usually more complex
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 2; owner 03 basic section 6 on complexity).
**Links:** ENV-1051, ENV-1052, ENV-1055, ENV-1075
**Trap:** Succession is not random change; it is a directional sequence. Complexity, not just change, is the diagnostic.
**Mains hook:** Restoration must follow the sequence rather than jump to climax species (qualified: site condition decides the stage).
**Back:** *"Pioneers first, climax later, each step making room for the next."*

### Flashcard ENV-1051 — Primary succession
**Front:** What is primary succession, and give two examples.
**Definition (learn):** Primary succession starts on a substrate with no pre-existing soil or life, such as bare rock, new volcanic land or glacial retreat. It is the slowest successional pathway.
**Breakdown:**
- Start: bare rock or new land, no soil
- Pioneers (lichens) weather rock and begin soil formation
- Then mosses and herbs, shrubs, trees
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 2 and 3).
**Links:** ENV-1052, ENV-1053, ENV-1070
**Trap:** Primary succession is not faster simply because it is new land; soil formation makes it slow.
**Mains hook:** Mine-spoil reclamation is a primary-succession-like process (ENV-1070).
**Back:** *"No soil at the start: rock first, forest last."*

### Flashcard ENV-1052 — Secondary succession
**Front:** What is secondary succession, and why is it faster than primary succession?
**Definition (learn):** Secondary succession occurs on a substrate that already has soil and often a seed bank, after disturbance such as fire or farm abandonment. It skips soil formation, so it proceeds markedly faster toward a similar climax.
**Breakdown:**
- Start: existing soil after disturbance
- Fast colonisers, then grasses and herbs, then shrubs and mature community
- Seed bank retained (owner-stated)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 2 and 3).
**Links:** ENV-1051, ENV-1071 (jhum fallow)
**Trap:** Secondary succession is not "the same speed"; it is faster because soil already exists.
**Mains hook:** Use the soil criterion to explain why jhum fallow recovers faster than mined land (qualified: depends on fallow length).
**Back:** *"Soil stays, seeds wait: the second start is quicker."*

### Flashcard ENV-1053 — Pioneer species and lichens
**Front:** Which organisms are the classic pioneers of primary succession on bare rock?
**Definition (learn):** Pioneer species are the first colonisers of a primary-succession site. Lichens are the classic example on bare rock; they weather rock and start soil formation.
**Breakdown:**
- Pioneers: lichens (and mosses in later stages)
- Lichens weather rock and add organic matter
- Pioneer stage: stabilise substrate before climax species
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 6).
**Links:** ENV-1051, ENV-1054, ENV-1074
**Trap:** Pioneers are not the climax; they prepare the site for later species.
**Mains hook:** Planting climax trees directly on bare spoil usually fails (qualified; ⚠️ owner adv restoration design logic).
**Back:** *"Lichen pioneers the rock, and soil follows."*

### Flashcard ENV-1054 — Lichens are not single plants
**Front:** What is a lichen?
**Definition (learn):** A lichen is a symbiotic association of a fungus with an alga or cyanobacterium. It is not a single plant species.
**Breakdown:**
- Partners: fungus plus photosynthetic partner
- Role: colonises bare rock and weathers it
- Not a plant
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 7).
**Links:** ENV-1053, ENV-1074
**Trap:** Calling a lichen a plant or a single species is a classic trap.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; use for statement-level elimination.
**Back:** *"Fungus and alga living together: a lichen, not a plant."*

### Flashcard ENV-1055 — Climax community
**Front:** What is a climax community, and can it change?
**Definition (learn):** A climax community is the relatively stable, self-perpetuating end-community of succession, in equilibrium with the local climate. It is not permanently fixed; a major disturbance such as fire, storm or land-use change can reset succession.
**Breakdown:**
- Relative stability until the next major disturbance
- Climate constrains the climax but disturbance history shapes it (see ENV-1064)
- Grassland or desert scrub can itself be a stable climax
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic sections 2, 6 and 7).
**Links:** ENV-1050, ENV-1064, ENV-1058
**Trap:** Succession does not always end in forest, and a climax never changes is false.
**Mains hook:** Treat climax as climate-constrained but not uniquely determined (qualified).
**Back:** *"Stable end, not a fixed end: disturbance can reset it."*

### Flashcard ENV-1056 — Sere and seral stage
**Front:** Distinguish a sere from a seral stage.
**Definition (learn):** A sere is the whole successional sequence at a site. A seral stage (seral community) is one intermediate community within that sequence.
**Breakdown:**
- Sere: the whole sequence
- Seral stage: one community in it
- Analogy: the sequence is the film, a seral stage is one frame
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 2 and 7).
**Links:** ENV-1057, ENV-1050
**Trap:** A sere and a seral stage do not mean the same thing.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; use for terminology accuracy.
**Back:** *"Sere is the whole sequence; seral is one step."*

### Flashcard ENV-1057 — Named seres by starting substrate
**Front:** Name the five seres classified by starting substrate, with what each begins on.
**Definition (learn):** Lithosere: bare rock. Psammosere: sand. Halosere: saline or salt marsh. Hydrosere: fresh water. Xerosere: dry substrate.
**Breakdown:**
- Lithosere: rock
- Psammosere: sand
- Halosere: salt
- Hydrosere: water
- Xerosere: dry land
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 2 and 6; flagged as a high-frequency Prelims matching set).
**Links:** ENV-1056, ENV-1058
**Trap:** Match the sere to its starting substrate, not to its climax.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item; use for matching.
**Back:** *"Rock, sand, salt, water, dry: five starts, five seres."*

### Flashcard ENV-1058 — Hydrarch and xerarch succession
**Front:** What do hydrarch and xerarch succession start from, and where do they tend?
**Definition (learn):** Hydrarch succession begins in water and moves toward a drier, mesic terrestrial climax as the basin fills. Xerarch succession begins on dry substrate and moves toward more mesic conditions. The owner states both typically converge toward a similar mesic climax.
**Breakdown:**
- Hydrarch: water to land (basin fills)
- Xerarch: dry to more mesic
- Convergence: both approach climate-determined vegetation
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 6 and adv section 7).
**Links:** ENV-1057, ENV-1055
**Trap:** Hydrarch succession does not move toward a wetter climax; it moves away from open water.
**Mains hook:** Use convergence to argue climate sets the endpoint (qualified).
**Back:** *"Water fills in toward dry land; dry land toward mesic."*

### Flashcard ENV-1059 — Biome and its classifying variables
**Front:** What is a biome, and which two variables classify biomes?
**Definition (learn):** A biome is a large regional ecosystem type characterised by distinctive climate, vegetation and animal community. Temperature and precipitation are the two dominant classifying variables.
**Breakdown:**
- Classifiers: temperature and precipitation regime
- Parallel to Koppen-type climate classification (Geography)
- Examples: tropical rainforest, savanna, desert, tundra
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 2 and 3; Köppen link ✅ owner 03 adv).
**Links:** ENV-1060, ENV-1062
**Trap:** Biomes are not synonyms for ecosystems; climate defines the category.
**Mains hook:** Climatic determinism explains broad biome type but not fine-scale variation (qualified; soil and fire modify it).
**Back:** *"Heat and rain draw the biome map."*

### Flashcard ENV-1060 — Biome sequence from tropical forest to tundra
**Front:** Give the owner's biome sequence from tropical forest toward tundra.
**Definition (learn):** The owner lists the sequence: tropical forest, savanna, desert, temperate grassland, temperate forest, taiga, tundra. The order is a spatial gradient, not a time sequence.
**Breakdown:**
- Tropical forest, savanna, desert
- Temperate grassland, temperate forest
- Taiga (boreal), tundra
- Spatial framework, not succession (owner)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 1 and 3).
**Links:** ENV-1059, ENV-1061
**Trap:** A biome sequence is not a succession timeline.
**Mains hook:** Use biome fidelity to argue against inappropriate afforestation (ENV-1066).
**Back:** *"Forest, savanna, desert, grass, forest, taiga, tundra: a climate line."*

### Flashcard ENV-1061 — Tundra: growing season as the binding constraint
**Front:** Why is tundra a climatically arrested climax, and what limits its vegetation?
**Definition (learn):** Tundra vegetation (low herbs, sedges, cotton grasses, grasses, mosses and lichens) is a low-complexity climax in which the growing season, not soil, is the binding constraint.
**Breakdown:**
- Vegetation: low herbs, sedges, cotton grasses, mosses, lichens
- Constraint: growing season length
- Low structural complexity
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 6).
**Links:** ENV-1060, ENV-1055
**Trap:** Tundra is not a degraded forest; it is a climate-determined climax.
**Mains hook:** Low-complexity climax still needs protection (qualified).
**Back:** *"Short summer, low green: the season is the limit."*

### Flashcard ENV-1062 — Biome versus ecosystem
**Front:** Distinguish a biome from an ecosystem.
**Definition (learn):** A biome is a large climate-defined category containing many individual ecosystems. An ecosystem is a specific functional unit within it.
**Breakdown:**
- Biome: category, climate-defined
- Ecosystem: unit within a biome
- Scale difference: regional vs local
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 7).
**Links:** ENV-1001, ENV-1059
**Trap:** Biome and ecosystem are not synonyms.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains item alone; use to avoid scale errors.
**Back:** *"Biome is the big class; ecosystem is one member."*

### Flashcard ENV-1063 — India's biome diversity
**Front:** Which biome types occur within India, according to the owner?
**Definition (learn):** India spans tropical wet evergreen (Western Ghats and Northeast), tropical dry deciduous (peninsula and central India), thorn forest or desert (Thar), and montane temperate or alpine zones (Himalaya).
**Breakdown:**
- Tropical wet evergreen: Western Ghats, Northeast
- Tropical dry deciduous: central India and peninsula
- Thorn forest or desert: Thar
- Montane and alpine: Himalaya
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 basic section 5; adv section 3 on latitude, monsoon and orographic causes).
**Links:** ENV-1059, ENV-1008, ENV-1085
**Trap:** India is not a single biome; its diversity is a result of latitude, monsoon gradient and orography.
**Mains hook:** India's biome spread makes one-size-fits-all afforestation inappropriate (qualified).
**Back:** *"Wet in the Ghats, dry in the centre, thorny in Thar, alpine on the Himalaya."*

### Flashcard ENV-1064 — Clementsian climax versus disturbance-shaped climax
**Front:** Why is the classical deterministic climax view contested?
**Definition (learn):** The classical Clementsian view holds that succession converges deterministically on one climatic climax. Modern ecology accepts that disturbance history, species availability and chance colonisation (initial floristic composition) can produce multiple plausible stable states.
**Breakdown:**
- Classical: one climatic endpoint
- Revised: climate-constrained but not uniquely determined
- Alternative stable states within climate limits
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical framing (owner 03 adv section 1); not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-1055, ENV-1058
**Trap:** Do not state the climax as a single fixed endpoint in an advanced answer.
**Mains hook:** Use this nuance to argue for flexible, site-specific restoration (qualified).
**Back:** *"Climate sets the range; history picks the spot."*

### Flashcard ENV-1065 — Open Natural Ecosystems (ONEs)
**Front:** What are Open Natural Ecosystems (ONEs), and why does the owner call them a land-record problem?
**Definition (learn):** ONEs are India's grasslands, savannas, scrublands, ravines and rocky outcrops. They are natural climax biomes but are recorded administratively largely as wasteland, culturable waste or degraded forest.
**Breakdown:**
- Classification error: they are a legitimate climax biome
- Funding incentive: wasteland is the default for afforestation, solar parks and offsets
- Carbon: grassland carbon is largely below ground, undervalued by canopy metrics
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical framing (owner 03 adv section 3A); the term is owner usage and not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-1066, ENV-1067, ENV-1068
**Trap:** Wasteland in land records is an administrative label, not an ecological description.
**Mains hook:** The ONE argument turns generic "plant native species" into a critique of the land-classification layer (qualified).
**Back:** *"Grass is called waste; the land is a climax biome."*

### Flashcard ENV-1066 — Wasteland, forest and grassland biome: three labels
**Front:** Distinguish the three labels: wasteland, forest and grassland biome.
**Definition (learn):** Wasteland is an administrative or revenue label. Forest is a legal category (as judicially construed). Grassland biome is an ecological category.
**Breakdown:**
- Administrative: wasteland (revenue record)
- Legal: forest (judicially construed)
- Ecological: grassland biome
- Keep the three apart in any answer
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 adv section 3A and 9).
**Links:** ENV-1065, ENV-1069
**Trap:** A "forest" in law is not automatically an ecological forest, and "wasteland" is not an ecological category.
**Mains hook:** A green-credit plantation on grassland is a category error (qualified).
**Back:** *"Revenue calls it waste, law calls it forest, ecology calls it grass."*

### Flashcard ENV-1067 — Great Indian Bustard as a grassland-dependent species
**Front:** Why does the owner use the Great Indian Bustard to argue that grassland decline differs from forest loss?
**Definition (learn):** The owner lists the Great Indian Bustard, lesser florican, Indian wolf, blackbuck, caracal and Indian fox as open natural ecosystem dependent. Their decline tracks grassland conversion, not forest loss, so forest cover can rise while they collapse.
**Breakdown:**
- ONE-dependent species list (owner)
- Driver: grassland conversion
- Implication: forest-cover statistics miss grassland losses
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical claim (owner 03 adv section 3A). Species status: see ENV-1120.
**Links:** ENV-1065, ENV-1120, ENV-1073
**Trap:** Rising forest cover does not mean grassland species are safe.
**Mains hook:** Grassland species need grassland protection, not tree planting (qualified: site-specific).
**Back:** *"Bustard falls when grass falls, whatever the forest does."*

### Flashcard ENV-1068 — Staged restoration: pioneer, intermediate, climax
**Front:** What three-stage logic does the owner use for restoration design?
**Definition (learn):** Restoration should match the successional stage. Pioneer stage stabilises the substrate (e.g., legume cover crops with nitrogen fixation). Intermediate or shrub stage builds soil depth and microclimate. Climax-approach stage introduces climax-forest species only once canopy microclimate and soil depth are adequate.
**Breakdown:**
- Pioneer: stabilise, add organic matter, fix nitrogen
- Intermediate: fast-growing natives, microclimate
- Climax-approach: climax species last
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner design logic (owner 03 adv section 2).
**Links:** ENV-1050, ENV-1070, ENV-1069
**Trap:** Planting climax hardwoods directly on bare spoil typically fails.
**Mains hook:** Staged restoration is the test for afforestation quality (qualified).
**Back:** *"Stabilise, build, then climax: the order matters."*

### Flashcard ENV-1069 — Afforestation and Green Credit Programme
**Front:** What is the owner's critique of the Afforestation and Green Credit Programme?
**Definition (learn):** The owner states that the Economic Survey 2025-26 (Ch. 10) records the Afforestation and Green Credit Programme as incentivising public and private participation in compensatory afforestation and degraded-forest restoration. Credits earned per unit of plantation reward the pioneer or intermediate stage and are silent on native-climax fidelity.
**Breakdown:**
- Metric: plantation units
- Gap: native-species fidelity and successional stage not measured
- Critique: green credits can equal monoculture
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner-only, Economic Survey 2025-26 as cited (owner 03 adv section 9); not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-1066, ENV-1068, ENV-1073
**Trap:** A green-credit plantation is not equivalent to restored natural forest.
**Mains hook:** Judge afforestation by successional stage and native fidelity, not hectares (qualified: programme details need verification).
**Back:** *"Credits count trees, not the forest they should become."*

### Flashcard ENV-1070 — Mine-spoil reclamation as primary succession in India
**Front:** Why is mine-spoil reclamation a primary-succession analogy in India?
**Definition (learn):** Mine-spoil or landslide-scarred land recovers in a primary-succession-like way, beginning with pioneer lichens and mosses, which forms the basis of ecological mine-reclamation projects.
**Breakdown:**
- Start: spoil with little or no soil
- Pioneers: lichens, mosses
- Restoration: staged planting
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical example (owner 03 basic section 5).
**Links:** ENV-1051, ENV-1068
**Trap:** Mine spoil is not secondary succession; soil must be built first.
**Mains hook:** Measure reclamation by soil and native composition, not survival alone (qualified).
**Back:** *"Spoil starts with no soil: build soil before planting climax."*

### Flashcard ENV-1071 — Jhum fallow as secondary succession in Northeast India
**Front:** What does jhum (shifting cultivation) fallow illustrate, and what controls recovery?
**Definition (learn):** Jhum fallow land in Northeast India shows classic secondary succession. The length of the fallow period determines how close vegetation gets to a climax-like forest before the next clearing cycle.
**Breakdown:**
- Start: existing soil and seed bank
- Control: fallow length
- Risk: short fallows prevent recovery
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner analytical example (owner 03 basic section 5).
**Links:** ENV-1052, ENV-1062
**Trap:** Jhum fallow is not automatically degraded forest; the fallow length is the variable.
**Mains hook:** Shorter fallow cycles reduce recovery and matter for livelihood trade-offs (qualified).
**Back:** *"Long fallow, forest back; short fallow, scrub stays."*

### Flashcard ENV-1072 — "Forests precede civilizations and deserts follow them" (2024 essay)
**Front:** What does the owner link this 2024 Mains essay theme to, and what is its verdict?
**Definition (learn):** The owner states the 2024 Mains Essay (Section A) theme "Forests precede civilizations and deserts follow them." The succession engine: forests build soil and regulate water; disturbance beyond a threshold can reverse the sere toward degraded states.
**Breakdown:**
- Forest builds soil and water regulation
- Disturbance beyond threshold reverses sere
- Verdict: a warning about overshoot, not a deterministic claim
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner-cited essay (owner 03 basic section 9); not fetched as a UPSC paper text.
**Links:** ENV-1050, ENV-1055, ENV-1068
**Trap:** The aphorism is not a claim that every desert is human-made.
**Mains hook:** Use succession plus land-degradation to answer the essay (qualified; pair with Topic 23 desertification).
**Back:** *"Forests build the soil; break it past a threshold and the desert follows."*

### Flashcard ENV-1073 — Forest-cover data cannot show successional stage
**Front:** Why can forest-cover statistics not separate young secondary growth from climax forest?
**Definition (learn):** FSI forest cover is a canopy-density threshold. A young secondary stand and a mature climax forest can register identically as moderately dense forest.
**Breakdown:**
- Metric: canopy density
- Cannot tell: composition, age, function
- Needed: repeated monitoring of native species and structure
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 03 adv section 5 and 8).
**Links:** ENV-1022, ENV-1069
**Trap:** A rise in reported forest cover is not proof of climax recovery.
**Mains hook:** Pair canopy trend with species and soil indicators (qualified).
**Back:** *"Canopy counts, climax needs composition."*

### Flashcard ENV-1074 — PYQ route: pioneer organisms on surfaces without soil
**Front:** Which Prelims demand routes to this topic, and what must the answer rule be?
**Definition (learn):** The 2021 GS-I Q20 demand concerns pioneer organisms surviving on surfaces without soil.
**Breakdown:**
- Pioneers: lichens on bare rock (classic)
- Test: soil-presence criterion
- Method: eliminate options that assign soil-dependent organisms to bare rock
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner PYQ ledger (✅ owner 03 basic PYQ integration). Official key is not held locally; no answer is inferred.
**Links:** ENV-1053, ENV-1051
**Trap:** Do not answer from memory where an official key exists.
**Mains hook:** Not a Mains demand; statement elimination only.
**Back:** *"No soil, no problem for lichens: pioneers live where others cannot."*

### Flashcard ENV-1075 — Core theme: succession is time, biome is space
**Front:** What is the answer thesis for succession and biomes?
**Definition (learn):** Succession is the time axis and biomes are the space axis of ecosystem classification. Sound restoration must respect both: the correct successional stage and the correct native biome for the site.
**Breakdown:**
- Time axis: staged succession
- Space axis: biome fidelity
- Test: is the intervention matched to both?
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Answer thesis (✅ owner 03 basic section 10).
**Links:** ENV-1050, ENV-1059, ENV-1068, ENV-1066
**Trap:** Tree planting is not automatically ecological restoration.
**Mains hook:** Conclude with a staged, biome-appropriate restoration recommendation, not a blanket afforestation call (qualified).
**Back:** *"Right stage, right biome: restoration on both axes."*

### GROUP — Biodiversity Levels and Hotspots

### Flashcard ENV-1076 — Three levels of biodiversity
**Front:** Name the three levels of biodiversity and give one example of each.
**Definition (learn):** Biodiversity is assessed at three nested levels: genetic (variation within a species), species (number and variety of species), and ecosystem (variety of habitats, communities and processes across a landscape).
**Breakdown:**
- Genetic: variation within species (e.g., rice landraces)
- Species: richness and abundance within a region
- Ecosystem: habitat variety (forest, wetland, grassland, coral reef)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 basic section 2; NCERT Class XII Biology per owner).
**Links:** ENV-1077, ENV-1078, ENV-1079, ENV-1101
**Trap:** Biodiversity is not only species diversity; genetic and ecosystem levels are equally important.
**Mains hook:** Argue that a strategy must address all three levels (qualified).
**Back:** *"Genes within, species between, habitats across."*

### Flashcard ENV-1077 — Genetic diversity and crop landraces
**Front:** Why does genetic diversity matter, and what is the owner's Indian example?
**Definition (learn):** Genetic diversity provides the raw material for a species' adaptability, for example drought-resistant crop landraces. Its loss narrows resilience.
**Breakdown:**
- Raw material for adaptation
- Example: indigenous rice varieties in the Northeast and eastern India, conserved by farmers
- Conservation outside formal protected areas
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner-cited illustration (owner 04 basic section 5); the specific variety counts are not given and are not verified here.
**Links:** ENV-1076, ENV-1095
**Trap:** Do not infer a precise national landrace count without a dated source.
**Mains hook:** Agrobiodiversity conservation is a food-security issue, not only ecological (qualified).
**Back:** *"Farmers' rice keeps the genetic reserve alive."*

### Flashcard ENV-1078 — Alpha, beta and gamma diversity
**Front:** Define alpha, beta and gamma diversity.
**Definition (learn):** Alpha diversity is species diversity within a single community or habitat. Beta diversity is the turnover of species between communities along an environmental gradient. Gamma diversity is total diversity across a whole landscape or region, integrating alpha and beta.
**Breakdown:**
- Alpha: local count within a habitat (richness and evenness)
- Beta: turnover between habitats
- Gamma: landscape total
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Whittaker terminology (✅ owner 04 basic section 2 and 6).
**Links:** ENV-1079, ENV-1080
**Trap:** High beta does not mean each habitat is species-rich; each can be poor while composition turns over sharply.
**Mains hook:** Corridors preserve beta connectivity across a landscape (qualified).
**Back:** *"Alpha is the room, beta the change between rooms, gamma the whole house."*

### Flashcard ENV-1079 — Richness versus evenness
**Front:** What is the difference between species richness and evenness?
**Definition (learn):** Richness is the number of species. Evenness is how equally abundant those species are. Two sites with the same richness can differ in diversity index values because of evenness.
**Breakdown:**
- Richness: count
- Evenness: distribution of abundance
- Diversity: both components
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 basic section 2 and 6).
**Links:** ENV-1078, ENV-1076
**Trap:** A "more diverse" site is not automatically the one with more species.
**Mains hook:** Use both measures when comparing restored and natural sites (qualified).
**Back:** *"Richness counts names; evenness shares the abundance."*

### Flashcard ENV-1080 — Endemic species
**Front:** Define an endemic species and distinguish it from a rare species.
**Definition (learn):** An endemic species occurs naturally only within one defined geographic region. Endemism is about restricted natural range, not local rarity.
**Breakdown:**
- Endemic: restricted natural range
- Rare: low abundance, may be widespread
- Endemism raises extinction risk because there is no alternative range (see ENV-1116)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 04 basic section 2 and 7; ✅ owner 05 basic).
**Links:** ENV-1081, ENV-1116, ENV-1115
**Trap:** Endemic does not mean endangered, and rare does not mean endemic.
**Mains hook:** Endemism is the variable that explains disproportionate extinction risk (qualified).
**Back:** *"Endemic means one home only, not few members."*

### Flashcard ENV-1081 — Biodiversity hotspot: criteria
