**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 14 routing ledgers). 2024 and 2025 Set-A keys are held locally; no answer letter recorded here.
**Links:** ENV-2133; ENV-2148; ENV-2149.
**Trap:** Do not infer answers from these summaries.
**Mains hook:** Not standalone.
**Back:** *"Five water routes: sand, microbeads, PFAS, membranes, carbon."*

### Flashcard ENV-2152 — Current anchors: Environment Audit Rules, 2025 and PARIVESH 3.0
**Front:** What do the Environment Audit Rules, 2025 and PARIVESH 3.0 introduce, and what is the balanced critique?
**Definition (learn):** The Environment Audit Rules, 2025 introduce certified third-party environmental auditors under major environmental statutes. PARIVESH 3.0 provides a single-window clearance and post-approval compliance platform (Economic Survey 2025-26, Ch. 10). The critique: third-party auditors are paid by the audited entity, a conflict of interest.
**Breakdown:**
- Audit Rules 2025: certified third-party auditors.
- PARIVESH 3.0: single-window clearance and compliance.
- Logic: outsource verification, retain sanction.
- Risk: payment conflict.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 📰 (✅ owner 14 advanced; Economic Survey 2025-26 Ch. 10 cited).
**Links:** ENV-2142; ENV-2132; ENV-2144.
**Trap:** Third-party verification does not replace state sanction.
**Mains hook:** Argue for outsourced monitoring with safeguards. Qualify: conflicts of interest need controls.
**Back:** *"Audit outsourced, sanction kept, payment conflict watched."*

### Flashcard ENV-2153 — NMCG and CPCB: statutory vs society (trap summary)
**Front:** Which water-governance bodies are statutory regulators, and which are implementers?
**Definition (learn):** CPCB and SPCBs are statutory regulators under the Water Act, 1974. NMCG is an implementing registered society with Authority powers under the 2016 Ganga Order. The National Ganga Council is a PM-chaired apex body under the same 2016 Order.
**Breakdown:**
- Statutory regulator: CPCB; SPCBs.
- Implementer: NMCG.
- Apex: National Ganga Council.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 14 basic and advanced)
**Links:** ENV-2137; ENV-2136; ENV-2131.
**Trap:** Do not treat implementers as regulators or the reverse.
**Mains hook:** Map the roles in a governance answer.
**Back:** *"Regulators: CPCB and SPCBs. Implementer: NMCG. Apex: PM."*

### Flashcard ENV-2154 — Concept: water governance from regulation to river missions
**Front:** What is the core theme of water pollution governance and how should an answer frame it?
**Definition (learn):** India's water governance combines a general regulatory framework (Water Act, CPCB and SPCB consent) with dedicated river missions (Namami Gange). Both are needed. Durable river quality depends on sewage treatment operated and connected, not on river-front work alone.
**Breakdown:**
- Regulation: Water Act consent and standards.
- Mission: Namami Gange.
- Test: capacity operated, connected, and stretch-level quality.
- Source: sewage first; industry consent; runoff hard.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 14)
**Links:** ENV-2131; ENV-2135; ENV-2144; ENV-2145; ENV-2146.
**Trap:** Do not judge success by visible river-front improvement alone.
**Mains hook:** Conclude with infrastructure plus O&M plus participation. Qualify: ULB capacity and public participation are needed.
**Back:** *"Consent guards the discharge; missions fix the river; O&M keeps it running."*

## ENVIRONMENT AND ECOLOGY — Part 3: Waste Rules, EIA, Climate Science, IPCC, UNFCCC and India Climate Policy

### GROUP — Solid Plastic and E-Waste Rules

### Flashcard ENV-3001 — Three parallel waste-stream rule frameworks and the EPR convergence
**Front:** How are India's municipal, plastic and e-waste rules structured, and what common mechanism links them?
**Definition (learn):** India regulates three major waste streams through separate rule frameworks, all made under the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986: Solid Waste Management Rules (municipal/household waste), Plastic Waste Management Rules (plastic packaging and products) and E-Waste (Management) Rules (electronic/electrical waste). The common accountability mechanism is Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR).
**Breakdown:**
- Municipal solid waste → SWM Rules: segregation, collection, processing (ULB-led).
- Plastic → PWM Rules: EPR for packaging, single-use-plastic restrictions.
- E-waste → E-Waste Rules: EPR, authorised dismantling and recycling.
- Shared logic: shift from municipal-only disposal to producer/brand-owner accountability.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Parent statute: Environment (Protection) Act, 1986 (✅ owner 15). Rules are subordinate legislation, not standalone Acts (✅ owner 15 basic §6).
**Links:** ENV-3002, ENV-3003, ENV-3007, ENV-3011, ENV-3010
**Trap:** A common parent statute does not make the three frameworks one combined rule document (✅ owner 15 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Argue that EPR moves lifecycle responsibility upstream to producers; judge it by verified recovery, not by target percentages on paper.
**Back:** *"Three streams, one parent Act, one shared lever: EPR."*

### Flashcard ENV-3002 — Environment (Protection) Act, 1986 as parent statute
**Front:** Under which statute are the SWM, Plastic Waste and E-Waste Rules made?
**Definition (learn):** The Environment (Protection) Act, 1986 is the umbrella environmental law; the waste-stream frameworks are rules issued under its delegated powers.
**Breakdown:**
- Parent Act → rules (SWM 2016, PWM 2016 as amended, E-Waste 2022, Battery 2022, ELV 2025).
- Rules carry the operational obligations; the Act carries the enabling power.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Act year 1986 (✅ owner 15 basic header); each rule's year as listed in its own card.
**Links:** ENV-3001, ENV-3020, ENV-3019
**Trap:** Calling the EPR rules "Acts" or "statutes" — they are subordinate rules under the 1986 Act (✅ owner 15 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** Subordinate rule-making lets the Centre update waste regimes quickly, but it also means accountability depends on notification quality and enforcement.
**Back:** *"Rules hang from the 1986 Act like leaves from a branch."*

### Flashcard ENV-3003 — Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016: three-way segregation
**Front:** What segregation categories and implementing body does the Solid Waste Management Rules, 2016 prescribe?
**Definition (learn):** The 2016 SWM Rules require source segregation of municipal/household waste into wet (biodegradable), dry (recyclable) and hazardous/domestic-hazardous categories, with Urban Local Bodies as primary implementers.
**Breakdown:**
- W = wet (biodegradable)
- D = dry (recyclable)
- H = hazardous / domestic-hazardous
- Implementers: ULBs (collection, processing infrastructure); generators (households, bulk generators) support.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rules notified 2016 (✅ owner 15 basic §1–2). Segregation at source; door-to-door collection (✅ owner 15 basic §2).
**Links:** ENV-3004 (2026 successor), ENV-3017 (ULB role)
**Trap:** The 2016 scheme is three-way; the 2026 scheme (ENV-3004) is four-way. Do not mix the classification counts.
**Mains hook:** Weak household segregation compliance, which varies across cities, is a core constraint on processing.
**Back:** *"W-D-H at the bin, ULB at the end."*

### Flashcard ENV-3004 — Solid Waste Management Rules, 2026: four-stream segregation (verify)
**Front:** What is new in the Solid Waste Management Rules, 2026 on segregation?
**Definition (learn):** According to the owner file, SWM Rules 2026 supersede the 2016 Rules from 1 April 2026 and move to four streams: wet, dry, sanitary and special-care / domestic-hazardous waste.
**Breakdown:**
- 2016: wet, dry, hazardous (3 streams).
- 2026: wet, dry, sanitary, special-care/domestic-hazardous (4 streams).
- Also formal recognition of Material Recovery Facilities (MRFs).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Effective 1 April 2026 (✅ owner 15 basic §14, sourced to a 2026 exam OCR and Vajiram & Ravi, Jan 2026). ⚠️ Not verified against an MoEFCC gazette or fetched page in this build.
**Links:** ENV-3003, ENV-3005, ENV-3006
**Trap:** Do not treat the 2026 Rules as replacing EPR for plastic or e-waste; they update the municipal framework only (✅ owner 15 basic §14 trap).
**Mains hook:** Finer segregation raises the compliance burden on households and ULBs; the Rules' value depends on the processing capacity that follows the bins.
**Back:** *"Four bins now, not three; check the gazette before you cite."*

### Flashcard ENV-3005 — Bulk Waste Generator thresholds and EBWGR (2026 Rules)
**Front:** What thresholds define a Bulk Waste Generator under the SWM Rules, 2026, and what principle applies?
**Definition (learn):** A Bulk Waste Generator (BWG) is an establishment above specified size or throughput levels that carries enhanced waste obligations under an Extended Bulk Waste Generator Responsibility (EBWGR) principle, the bulk-generator parallel to EPR.
**Breakdown:**
- Floor area ≥ 20,000 sq m, OR
- Water consumption ≥ 40,000 litres/day, OR
- Waste generation ≥ 100 kg/day.
- Principle: EBWGR, parallel to producer EPR.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Thresholds as stated in owner 15 basic §14 (⚠️ not fetched; verify the notified text).
**Links:** ENV-3004, ENV-3001
**Trap:** Any one of the three triggers can qualify; they are not cumulative tests. Read the "OR" carefully.
**Mains hook:** Bulk-generator accountability shifts the cost of waste from municipal budgets to large consumers, a fiscal and governance argument.
**Back:** *"Any one of 20,000 sq m, 40,000 L or 100 kg makes you a bulk generator."*

### Flashcard ENV-3006 — RDF mandate, Polluter Pays and CPCB portal in SWM Rules 2026
**Front:** What enforcement and offtake tools does the 2026 SWM framework add?
**Definition (learn):** The 2026 framework pairs processing offtake with economic deterrence: a Refuse-Derived Fuel (RDF) mandate for cement plants, Polluter Pays compensation, and a CPCB online monitoring portal.
**Breakdown:**
- RDF: cement plants phased from 5% to 15% over six years, creating assured demand for processed waste.
- Polluter Pays: landfill fees set higher than the cost of segregation, so compliance is the cheaper option.
- CPCB centralised online monitoring portal for waste-stream tracking.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** RDF 5%→15% over six years; fee design (✅ owner 15 basic §14, ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3004, ENV-3015, ENV-3017
**Trap:** RDF is a fuel-substitution offtake, not a segregation rule; do not present it as a plastic EPR target.
**Mains hook:** Price signals (landfill fees above segregation costs) can make the compliant option cheaper, but only if enforcement is credible.
**Back:** *"Five to fifteen in six years, and dumping costs more than sorting."*

### Flashcard ENV-3007 — Plastic Waste Management Rules, 2016 (as amended)
**Front:** What do the Plastic Waste Management Rules, 2016 (as amended) regulate?
**Definition (learn):** The PWM Rules regulate plastic packaging and products. They impose EPR on producers, importers and brand owners and phase out identified single-use-plastic items; they were amended in 2021 and 2022.
**Breakdown:**
- Obligation holders: producers, importers, brand owners.
- Duty: a defined share of plastic packaging must be collected and recycled/processed.
- Tools: EPR targets plus item-specific single-use bans.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rules 2016, amended incl. 2021/2022 (✅ owner 15 basic §2; ✅ owner 15 advanced header).
**Links:** ENV-3008, ENV-3009, ENV-3010, ENV-3023
**Trap:** Plastic EPR obligations sit with producers/brand owners, not Urban Local Bodies (✅ owner 15 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** EPR gives plastic packaging a producer-funded recovery channel, but certificate integrity decides whether recovery is real.
**Back:** *"Plastic packaging: the maker pays, the ULB does not."*

### Flashcard ENV-3008 — Single-use plastic ban: 12 identified items from 1 July 2022
**Front:** Which single-use-plastic items were banned from 1 July 2022, and how many?
**Definition (learn):** India banned 12 identified single-use-plastic items with high littering potential and low utility. The policy is item-specific, not a blanket plastic ban.
**Breakdown:**
- Ear buds with plastic sticks; plastic sticks for balloons; plastic flags; candy sticks; ice-cream sticks.
- Thermocol (polystyrene) for decoration.
- Plates, cups, glasses, cutlery (forks, spoons, knives, straws, trays).
- Wrapping/packing films around sweet boxes, invitation cards, cigarette packets.
- PVC banners under 100 microns; stirrers.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 12 items; 1 July 2022 (✅ owner 15 basic §2, cited to Economic Survey 2025-26 Ch. 10). ⚠️ Item list not fetched against a gazette; cite the notification, not the Survey, for a legal claim.
**Links:** ENV-3007, ENV-3009, ENV-3024
**Trap:** "India has banned single-use plastic generally" is wrong; other SUPs remain legal under EPR and thickness rules (✅ owner 15 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Targeted bans are cheaper to enforce than blanket bans, but they are only as good as market surveillance.
**Back:** *"Twelve items, July 2022: targeted, not total."*

### Flashcard ENV-3009 — Carry-bag thickness raised to 120 microns
**Front:** What is the thickness standard for plastic carry bags and why?
**Definition (learn):** Plastic carry-bag thickness was raised progressively to 120 microns so bags are easier to collect and recycle. It is a design-standard lever, not a ban.
**Breakdown:**
- Lever: thickness standard (design).
- Purpose: collection and recycling feasibility.
- Contrast: item bans (ENV-3008) remove products; thickness rules shape those that remain.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 120 microns (✅ owner 15 basic §2). ⚠️ Effective dates of each step are not verified in this build; cite the amending notification.
**Links:** ENV-3007, ENV-3008
**Trap:** Thickness is a standard for bags that stay on the market, not a prohibition.
**Mains hook:** Design rules improve recyclability at the product stage, which is the earliest point of waste prevention.
**Back:** *"120 microns: thicker to be collected, not banned."*

### Flashcard ENV-3010 — Extended Producer Responsibility (EPR): definition and reach
**Front:** Define EPR and state how many waste streams India's EPR frameworks now cover.
**Definition (learn):** EPR is a policy principle that makes producers, importers and brand owners financially or physically responsible for collecting, recycling or disposing of their products after consumer use.
**Breakdown:**
- Plastic packaging EPR (PWM Rules).
- E-waste EPR (E-Waste Rules 2022).
- Batteries (Battery Rules 2022), waste tyres, used oil, end-of-life vehicles, construction and demolition waste, non-ferrous metal scrap.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Eight EPR streams (✅ owner 15 basic §6, cited to Economic Survey 2025-26 Ch. 10; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3001, ENV-3015, ENV-3016, ENV-3020
**Trap:** "EPR applies only to plastic and e-waste" is wrong; the owner lists eight streams. Also, EPR is not municipal responsibility.
**Mains hook:** EPR converts waste management into a producer-financed market; its success depends on verified recovery and on integrating informal collectors.
**Back:** *"Producers own the product's afterlife; eight streams wear the badge."*

### Flashcard ENV-3011 — E-waste rules history: 2011, 2016, 2022
**Front:** Trace the e-waste rule sequence and the first use of EPR in it.
**Definition (learn):** The E-Waste (Management and Handling) Rules, 2011 came into effect in May 2012. They were replaced by the E-Waste (Management) Rules, 2016, which introduced EPR. The E-Waste (Management) Rules, 2022 are the current framework per the owner file.
**Breakdown:**
- 2011 Rules → effective May 2012 (✅ Wikipedia: Electronic waste in India).
- 2016 Rules → replaced 2011 in October 2016; introduced EPR (✅ Wikipedia).
- 2022 Rules → current framework; EPR-based recycling; authorised dismantlers and recyclers (✅ owner 15 basic §2–3; ⚠️ year and effective date not confirmed by the fetched page).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2011 effective May 2012; 2016 replacement Oct 2016 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ENV-3012, ENV-3010, ENV-3001
**Trap:** The 2016 E-Waste Rules are not the current rules; the 2022 Rules replaced them. Also, the 2011 Rules are "Management and Handling", not "Management".
**Mains hook:** The move from 2011 to 2016 to 2022 shows the shift from handling to producer accountability.
**Back:** *"2011 handled it, 2016 made producers pay, 2022 formalised the recyclers."*

### Flashcard ENV-3012 — E-waste targets (2018 amendment) and generation scale
**Front:** What collection targets did the 2018 amendment set, and what is India's e-waste scale?
**Definition (learn):** A 2018 amendment to the 2016 E-Waste Rules set staged collection targets, loosening earlier requirements. Generation scale figures are reported estimates.
**Breakdown:**
- Collection targets: 10% (2017–18), 20% (2018–19), 30% (2019–20) and rising thereafter.
- Scale: roughly 2 million tonnes of e-waste per year; India described as the third-largest e-waste producer (no year attached).
- Growth: UNCTAD reports a 163% rise in screen, computer and small IT/telecom e-waste from 2010 to 2022; India's share rose from 3.1% to 6.4%.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Targets and UNCTAD figures (✅ Wikipedia: Electronic waste in India). "2 million tonnes" has no year: ⚠️ do not cite without a date.
**Links:** ENV-3011, ENV-3015
**Trap:** Targets in the 2016 framework were amended in 2018; do not read the 2018 numbers as the 2022 Rules' targets.
**Mains hook:** Rising e-waste with a large informal dismantling base is the case for authorised recycling and formal EPR.
**Back:** *"Ten, twenty, thirty: the 2018 ladder, and two million tonnes with no year."*

### Flashcard ENV-3013 — Battery Waste Management Rules, 2022
**Front:** What do the Battery Waste Management Rules, 2022 cover?
**Definition (learn):** An EPR framework for all battery types (portable, automotive, industrial, electric vehicle) that replaced the earlier lead-acid-focused rules and sets minimum recovery and recycled-content obligations.
**Breakdown:**
- Scope: portable, automotive, industrial, EV batteries.
- Tools: minimum recovery and recycled-content obligations.
- Replaced: lead-acid-only regime.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rules 2022 (✅ owner 15 basic §2; ⚠️ year not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3010, ENV-3014
**Trap:** Batteries are not covered by the E-Waste Rules' name; they have their own rules.
**Mains hook:** Battery EV growth makes recovery of lithium and other critical materials a resource-security issue, not just waste control.
**Back:** *"All batteries, not just lead-acid, under EPR."*

### Flashcard ENV-3014 — Circular Economy Action Plans across 10 waste categories
**Front:** Name the 10 waste categories covered by India's Circular Economy Action Plans.
**Definition (learn):** Action plans that treat material streams, not only municipal bins, as the unit of governance.
**Breakdown:**
- Lithium-ion batteries
- E-waste
- Toxic and hazardous industrial waste
- Ferrous and non-ferrous scrap metal
- Tyre and rubber
- End-of-life vehicles
- Gypsum
- Used oil
- Solar panels
- Municipal solid waste
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ten categories (✅ owner 15 basic §2; cited to Economic Survey 2025-26 Ch. 10; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3010, ENV-3013, ENV-3024
**Trap:** Solar panels and gypsum are in the list; candidates often omit them (✅ owner 15 basic §6).
**Mains hook:** A materials policy, not a waste-rules list, is the reframing for a top-tier answer (✅ owner 15 advanced §9).
**Back:** *"Ten streams: batteries, e-waste, toxics, scrap, tyres, ELVs, gypsum, oil, solar, MSW."*

### Flashcard ENV-3015 — CPCB as EPR administrator and the portal data (as on 14 Nov 2025)
**Front:** Which body administers EPR registration and target tracking, and what were the portal figures on 14 November 2025?
**Definition (learn):** The Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB) administers EPR registration, target setting and compliance monitoring through digital portals for plastic and e-waste.
**Breakdown:**
- Administrator: CPCB.
- Participants: producers, importers, brand owners (registration, targets), recyclers (certificates).
- Figures as on 14 Nov 2025: 69,116 producers and 4,377 recyclers registered; about 308 lakh tonnes recycled; 296.53 lakh tonnes of EPR certificates generated.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Figures from Economic Survey 2025-26 Ch. 10 (✅ owner 15 basic §8). ⚠️ Rolling cumulative figure: always date-stamp.
**Links:** ENV-3016, ENV-3023, ENV-3010
**Trap:** CPCB administers EPR; it does not itself carry the producer's obligation.
**Mains hook:** Tracking systems are the test of whether EPR is real; a certificate economy needs audit capacity.
**Back:** *"CPCB keeps the ledger: 69,116 producers, 296.53 lakh tonnes of certificates."*

### Flashcard ENV-3016 — Registered vs recycled vs certificate: the measurement discipline
**Front:** Why are EPR registrations, recycled tonnage and certificates different measures?
**Definition (learn):** Registrations measure participation; recycled tonnage measures throughput; EPR certificates measure a tradable instrument. None of the three directly measures material returned to productive use.
**Breakdown:**
- Participation → registrations.
- Throughput → tonnes recycled.
- Instrument → certificates generated.
- Missing link → verified circular use.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Figures from ENV-3015 (✅ owner 15 basic §8 caution box).
**Links:** ENV-3015, ENV-3023
**Trap:** Quoting "296.53 lakh tonnes recycled" when the figure is certificates generated.
**Mains hook:** Use the three measures precisely; say which one a cited number is before judging performance.
**Back:** *"Registered is who, recycled is how much, certificate is what was traded."*

### Flashcard ENV-3017 — Institutional roles: ULBs, SPCBs and CPCB in waste management
**Front:** Who implements segregation and collection, who authorises processing facilities, and who monitors EPR?
**Definition (learn):** Under the waste rules, Urban Local Bodies implement segregation, collection and processing infrastructure; State Pollution Control Boards authorise and monitor state-level processing and recycling facilities; CPCB administers EPR tracking.
**Breakdown:**
- ULBs: primary implementers of SWM Rules.
- SPCBs: authorisation and monitoring of facilities.
- CPCB: national EPR portals and compliance.
- Informal sector: waste-pickers, informal recyclers (substantial, under-integrated; ⚠️).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 15 basic §4).
**Links:** ENV-3003, ENV-3015, ENV-3018, ENV-3027
**Trap:** ULBs are not the EPR compliance party for plastic or e-waste (✅ owner 15 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** ULB capacity, not rule design, is often the binding constraint on segregation compliance.
**Back:** *"ULB sorts, SPCB licenses, CPCB audits."*

### Flashcard ENV-3018 — Informal waste sector: structural role and integration dilemma
**Front:** What role does the informal waste sector play, and what is the policy dilemma?
**Definition (learn):** Waste-pickers, kabadiwalas and informal e-waste dismantlers carry a large share of real plastic and e-waste collection, often with unsafe methods such as open burning of e-waste components.
**Breakdown:**
- Strength: collection reach and livelihoods.
- Harm: toxic exposure and uncontrolled emissions.
- Dilemma: abrupt formalisation can displace an already vulnerable workforce; no formalisation leaves harm unchecked.
- Integration route: recognition, safety equipment, channelling through informal collectors into authorised processors.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ analytical (✅ owner 15 advanced §3; ⚠️ owner 15 basic §4).
**Links:** ENV-3017, ENV-3023, ENV-3024
**Trap:** "Informal waste-pickers play no significant role" is wrong; equally, EPR is not "fully integrated" with them (✅ owner 15 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Two-sided dilemma: formalise with livelihood transition, do not romanticise informality.
**Back:** *"They collect the most and burn the worst: integrate, don't evict."*

### Flashcard ENV-3019 — Contaminated Sites Rules, 2025 and the flow-versus-stock distinction
**Front:** What do the Environment Protection (Management of Contaminated Sites) Rules, 2025 add, and how do they differ from EPR?
**Definition (learn):** These 2025 Rules set a framework to identify and remediate contaminated sites. The Environmental Relief Fund, under the amended Public Liability Insurance Act, can fund that remediation. EPR governs flows of new waste; contaminated-site rules address the stock of historic contamination.
**Breakdown:**
- Flow → new waste → EPR and SWM rules.
- Stock → legacy contamination → Contaminated Sites Rules 2025.
- Funding link: Environmental Relief Fund (amended Public Liability Insurance Act).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rules 2025; ERF (✅ owner 15 basic §8 and owner 15 advanced §9, cited to Economic Survey 2025-26 Ch. 10; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3010, ENV-3020, ENV-3026
**Trap:** Treating legacy dumps as covered by the 2016 or 2026 SWM Rules' new-waste logic.
**Mains hook:** Naming the flow-versus-stock distinction shows command of waste governance (✅ owner 15 advanced §9).
**Back:** *"EPR for the flow, remediation rules for the stock."*

### Flashcard ENV-3020 — End-of-Life Vehicles Rules, 2025
**Front:** When did the Environment Protection (End-of-Life Vehicles) Rules, 2025 take effect and what chain do they create?
**Definition (learn):** A vehicle-specific EPR and scrapping chain covering producers, owners or bulk consumers, and registered scrapping and collection entities.
**Breakdown:**
- Notification: S.O. 98(E).
- Effective: 1 April 2025.
- Chain: producer → owner/bulk consumer → registered scrapper/collector.
- Batteries, plastics, e-waste and hazardous waste stay under their own rules.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** S.O. 98(E), effective 1 April 2025 (✅ owner 15 basic §8 with MoEFCC link; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3010, ENV-3019, ENV-3013
**Trap:** The ELV Rules do not fold batteries or e-waste into a single vehicle regime.
**Mains hook:** Vehicle EPR extends the producer-accountability model to durable goods.
**Back:** *"S.O. 98(E), April 2025: vehicles join the EPR club."*

### Flashcard ENV-3021 — Delhi vs Indore: remediation versus prevention (2026 GS-III Q17)
**Front:** How should Delhi and Indore be contrasted in solid-waste management?
**Definition (learn):** Delhi is a legacy-landfill remediation case (biomining at Ghazipur, Bhalswa and Okhla). Indore is a prevention case: landfill-free, source-segregation-led from the outset.
**Breakdown:**
- Delhi: biomining; rising rate (about 6,000 TPD in 2022–23 to a peak of about 20,000 TPD, ✅ owner); about 100 acres reclaimed (✅ owner); Waste-to-Energy plants planned.
- Indore: 100% door-to-door segregated collection; Gobar-Dhan biogas (about 550 TPD, ✅ owner); repeated Swachh Survekshan "cleanest city" rankings (✅ owner).
- Verdict: Delhi = remediation in progress; Indore = design-level success.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Figures as in owner 15 basic §14 (⚠️ not fetched; verify against the latest MCD/DPCC report before citing tonnage).
**Links:** ENV-3003, ENV-3004, ENV-3022
**Trap:** "Delhi failed, Indore succeeded, full stop" is the named trap. Frame Delhi as remediation-in-progress, not unqualified failure.
**Mains hook:** The contrast is legacy-correction versus prevention, not a binary.
**Back:** *"Delhi digs out the past; Indore stops the pile."*

### Flashcard ENV-3022 — 2026 GS-III Q17 answer spine (15 marks, 250 words)
**Front:** What is the answer route for the 2026 question on SWM challenges, policy framework and Delhi vs Indore?
**Definition (learn):** The question asks challenges, the governmental policy framework, and success or failure of Delhi and Indore. Use a fixed four-part spine.
**Breakdown:**
- Challenges: rising waste volumes, weak segregation, legacy landfill backlogs, limited processing, informal-sector under-integration, ULB financing gaps.
- Policy framework: SWM Rules 2016 base → SWM Rules 2026 (four streams, BWG/EBWGR, RDF, Polluter Pays, CPCB portal).
- Delhi: legacy remediation via biomining; salient feature = recovery from historical failure.
- Indore: landfill-free, segregation-first; salient feature = prevention-first, citizen-driven.
- Verdict: two workable models: legacy-correction and prevention.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Exact question wording preserved in owner 15 basic §14 (✅ owner 15, OCR of QP-CSM-26-010926).
**Links:** ENV-3003, ENV-3004, ENV-3021
**Trap:** Do not claim the 2026 Rules replaced EPR for plastics or e-waste.
**Mains hook:** Structure: challenge → framework → two cases → verdict in about 250 words.
**Back:** *"Problems, framework, two cities, one verdict."*

### Flashcard ENV-3023 — Advanced: certificate-trading design and verification risk
**Front:** Why is India's EPR certificate system a verification problem, not only a policy-design problem?
**Definition (learn):** Producers can meet EPR obligations by buying certificates from registered recyclers, not necessarily from recyclers handling their own waste. Market flexibility needs robust audit to prevent fraud and double counting.
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: producer obligation → purchase certificate → registered recycler issues certificate.
- Analogy: tradable-certificate compliance systems.
- Risk: certificates issued without matching throughput; double counting.
- Need: physical audit capacity built progressively.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ analytical (✅ owner 15 advanced §1–2).
**Links:** ENV-3015, ENV-3016, ENV-3024
**Trap:** "Producers must recycle their own waste through a linked recycler" is wrong; certificate trading allows any registered recycler (✅ owner 15 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** Verification capacity, not target-setting, determines whether EPR works.
**Back:** *"A certificate is only as good as the bale it claims."*

### Flashcard ENV-3024 — Advanced: targeted SUP bans and enforcement reality
**Front:** Why do item-specific single-use-plastic bans face enforcement challenges?
**Definition (learn):** Targeted bans rest on criteria such as low utility, high littering potential and recycling difficulty. Enforcement requires surveillance of many small manufacturers and retailers.
**Breakdown:**
- Design logic: targeted (item-specific), not material-wide.
- Enforcement: informal supply chains keep banned items available; uneven state capacity.
- Critique: "ban announced, not enforced".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 15 advanced §4; ⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** ENV-3008, ENV-3023, ENV-3018
**Trap:** Targeted does not mean comprehensive; plastic packaging is governed by EPR, not by the item list.
**Mains hook:** A targeted ban is the right design only if surveillance is sustained.
**Back:** *"Targeted bans need a surveillance budget."*

### Flashcard ENV-3025 — PYQ demand: plastic in everyday items (Prelims 2024 and 2025)
**Front:** What plastic-related Prelims GS-I demands appear in the 2024 and 2025 papers, per the owner file?
**Definition (learn):** Two objective questions route to this topic: 2024 Q22 on chewing-gum plastic base as environmental pollution, and 2025 Q44 on everyday items containing plastic (cigarette butts, eyeglass lenses, car tyres).
**Breakdown:**
- 2024 Prelims GS-I Q22: chewing-gum plastic base.
- 2025 Prelims GS-I Q44: cigarette butts, eyeglass lenses, car tyres.
- Answers: not recorded in the owner file; official Set-A keys are held locally.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 15 basic PYQ block 2024–2025).
**Links:** ENV-3026, ENV-3008
**Trap:** Material-identification examples are not proof that every such item falls under the same ban or EPR duty (✅ owner 15 basic §13.3).
**Mains hook:** Use these as material-stream identification, not as legal coverage.
**Back:** *"Gum, butts, lenses, tyres: plastic hides in ordinary things."*

### Flashcard ENV-3026 — PYQ routing 2018–2023: waste-rule questions
**Front:** Which 2018–2023 PYQs route to the waste-rule owner, and what do they test?
**Definition (learn):** The owner routes seven demands: a 2018 Mains GS-III Q6 on solid-waste disposal and toxic waste, and Prelims items on pyrolysis and plasma gasification (2019 Q26), SWM Rules 2016 (2019 Q59), EPR introduction in Indian rules (2019 Q78), steel slag (2020 Q76), R2 Code of Practices for e-waste recycling (2021 Q16) and PET properties (2022 Q46).
**Breakdown:**
- 2018 GS-III Q6 (10 marks, 150 words): disposal challenges and toxic waste.
- Prelims 2019: pyrolysis/plasma gasification; SWM Rules 2016; EPR introduction.
- Prelims 2020–2022: steel slag uses; R2 e-waste code; PET recycling.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Keys not held locally for 2018–2023 Prelims (✅ owner 15 basic PYQ block 2018–2023).
**Links:** ENV-3003, ENV-3010, ENV-3025
**Trap:** Prelims items often test which rule covers which stream; do not answer from the 2026 framework for a 2019 question.
**Mains hook:** Use the 2018 Mains prompt to practise a 10-mark structure: challenge, disposal, toxic handling, counterpoint, conclusion.
**Back:** *"Seven demands: rules, EPR, plasma, slag, R2, PET, and toxic disposal."*

### GROUP — Environmental Impact Assessment and NGT

### Flashcard ENV-3027 — EIA and NGT as a preventive-and-corrective governance pair
**Front:** How do the EIA clearance system and the National Green Tribunal work together?
**Definition (learn):** The EIA Notification, 2006 decides before a project starts whether and how its impacts are assessed and mitigated (preventive). The NGT, set up under the NGT Act, 2010, adjudicates environmental disputes and clearance challenges after the fact (corrective).
**Breakdown:**
- Preventive layer: EIA screening, scoping, public consultation, appraisal, clearance.
- Corrective layer: NGT original jurisdiction (specified Acts) and appellate jurisdiction (clearances).
- Judicial overlay: High Courts and Supreme Court keep writ jurisdiction (✅ owner 16 basic §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** EIA Notification 2006 under EP Act 1986; NGT Act 2010 (✅ owner 16 basic header; NGT establishment year 2010 ✅ Wikipedia: National Green Tribunal).
**Links:** ENV-3028, ENV-3033, ENV-3040
**Trap:** NGT does not replace constitutional courts' jurisdiction over environmental matters (✅ owner 16 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Integrity of the preventive stage matters more than its existence; NGT only repairs what the clearance process missed.
**Back:** *"EIA prevents, NGT repairs, courts stand behind both."*

### Flashcard ENV-3028 — EIA Notification 2006: Category A and central clearance
**Front:** Who appraises and who clears a Category A project?
**Definition (learn):** Category A projects are large projects requiring central clearance. The Expert Appraisal Committee (EAC) appraises and recommends; the MoEFCC, as the central regulatory authority, grants, conditions or rejects clearance.
**Breakdown:**
- Category: A.
- Appraisal: EAC (recommends only).
- Decision: MoEFCC.
- Typical examples: large infrastructure, mining and industrial projects (⚠️ owner 16 basic §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** EIA Notification, 2006 (✅ owner 16 basic §2).
**Links:** ENV-3029, ENV-3032, ENV-3030
**Trap:** EAC is not the final clearance authority.
**Mains hook:** Central appraisal for large projects gives uniform scrutiny, but it places heavy demand on central expert capacity.
**Back:** *"A: EAC advises, MoEFCC decides."*

### Flashcard ENV-3029 — Category B, B1 and B2
**Front:** How do Category B1 and B2 projects differ, and who decides Category B clearance?
**Definition (learn):** Category B projects are state-level. The State Expert Appraisal Committee (SEAC) appraises and recommends; the State Environment Impact Assessment Authority (SEIAA) decides. B1 requires a full EIA report and public consultation; B2 is screened out of the detailed EIA and public-consultation requirement.
**Breakdown:**
- B1 → full EIA report + public consultation.
- B2 → no detailed EIA report (screened out).
- Appraisal: SEAC. Decision: SEIAA.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §1–2, §7).
**Links:** ENV-3028, ENV-3031, ENV-3032
**Trap:** "B2 requires a full EIA like B1" is wrong (✅ owner 16 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** The B1/B2 screening line is where political pressure on clearance speed concentrates.
**Back:** *"B1 gets the full report, B2 gets the shortcut."*

### Flashcard ENV-3030 — The four stages of the EIA process
**Front:** List the stages of the EIA process in order.
**Definition (learn):** The EIA process runs through screening, scoping, public consultation and appraisal, after which the competent authority records the clearance decision.
**Breakdown:**
- 1. Screening (Category B where required) — is a full EIA needed?
- 2. Scoping — define the terms of reference for the assessment.
- 3. Public consultation — public hearing and written comments.
- 4. Appraisal — EAC/SEAC expert review and recommendation.
- Then: clearance by MoEFCC (A) or SEIAA (B).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Four stages (✅ owner 16 basic §6).
**Links:** ENV-3031, ENV-3028, ENV-3029
**Trap:** Public consultation comes before appraisal, not after clearance.
**Mains hook:** Stage order matters: consultation feeds appraisal, so skipping it weakens the expert's evidence base.
**Back:** *"Screen, scope, consult, appraise, then clear."*

### Flashcard ENV-3031 — Public consultation: scope and documented critiques
**Front:** Which projects need public consultation, and what are the documented weaknesses?
**Definition (learn):** Public consultation (a public hearing plus written comments) lets affected communities raise concerns before the EAC/SEAC finalises its recommendation. It applies to projects needing a detailed EIA report, broadly Category A and B1.
**Breakdown:**
- Design: surface local concerns and livelihood impacts.
- Critiques: short notice; draft EIA not accessible to remote or low-literacy communities; technical language not translated; hearing venue and timing contested.
- Balanced view: the requirement is sound; implementation quality is the problem.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §6, advanced §2).
**Links:** ENV-3030, ENV-3029, ENV-3045
**Trap:** Consultation is not mandatory for every category; and critiques concern quality, not the absence of the requirement (✅ owner 16 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** Measure consultation by two-way engagement and accessible documents, not by hearing count.
**Back:** *"Hearings exist; quality is the fight."*

### Flashcard ENV-3032 — Appraisal committee versus regulatory authority
**Front:** Who appraises and who decides for each category, and why does the distinction matter?
**Definition (learn):** EAC (Category A) and SEAC (Category B) are expert committees that appraise and recommend. MoEFCC (A) and SEIAA (B) are the regulatory authorities that record the clearance decision.
**Breakdown:**
- A: EAC → MoEFCC.
- B: SEAC → SEIAA.
- Committees advise; authorities decide.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §4).
**Links:** ENV-3028, ENV-3029, ENV-3027
**Trap:** Naming the committee as the clearance granter. Examiners test the advisory-decision split.
**Mains hook:** Separating expert advice from the decision is a governance safeguard; it also concentrates accountability on the authority.
**Back:** *"Committees counsel, authorities sign."*

### Flashcard ENV-3033 — National Green Tribunal: establishment, benches and seat
**Front:** Where are the NGT's Principal Bench and regional benches?
**Definition (learn):** The NGT is a statutory tribunal set up in 2010 under the National Green Tribunal Act, 2010 for speedy, specialised environmental adjudication.
**Breakdown:**
- Principal Bench: New Delhi.
- Regional benches: Bhopal, Pune, Kolkata, Chennai.
- Nature: specialised, expert and judicial members.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Established 2010 (✅ Wikipedia: National Green Tribunal; exact day not given there). Benches (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 16 basic §6).
**Links:** ENV-3027, ENV-3034, ENV-3036
**Trap:** Bhopal, Pune, Kolkata and Chennai are the regional benches; a bench in Mumbai or Hyderabad is not in the list.
**Mains hook:** Regional benches improve access, but staffing gaps slow disposal (✅ owner 16 advanced §3).
**Back:** *"Delhi plus Bhopal, Pune, Kolkata, Chennai."*

### Flashcard ENV-3034 — NGT composition and procedure
**Front:** What is the NGT's composition, and which laws bind it?
**Definition (learn):** The NGT has a full-time Chairperson plus 10–20 full-time Judicial Members and 10–20 full-time Expert Members. It is not bound by the Code of Civil Procedure or the Indian Evidence Act, but must follow natural justice.
**Breakdown:**
- Composition: Chair + 10–20 judicial + 10–20 expert members.
- Procedure: not bound by CPC, 1908 or Evidence Act, 1872.
- Principle: natural justice.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §2, §6, citing NGT Act). Tribunal Reforms Act, 2021 read with appointments (✅ owner 16 basic §2).
**Links:** ENV-3033, ENV-3035
**Trap:** "NGT follows CPC and the Evidence Act" is wrong (✅ owner 16 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** A flexible procedure helps speed; natural justice protects against arbitrary orders.
**Back:** *"One chair, twenty judges, twenty experts, no CPC, yes natural justice."*

### Flashcard ENV-3035 — NGT timelines: six months, 90 days, five years, 30 days
**Front:** What are the NGT's disposal target, appeal window and the two limitation clocks?
**Definition (learn):** The NGT aims to dispose of applications within six months. An appeal lies to the Supreme Court within 90 days. A compensation application is ordinarily filed within five years of the cause of action; an appeal against a clearance order is filed within 30 days (extendable).
**Breakdown:**
- Disposal target: six months (✅ Wikipedia: National Green Tribunal).
- SC appeal: within 90 days (✅ Wikipedia).
- Compensation claim: five years from cause of action (✅ owner 16 basic §6).
- Clearance appeal: 30 days, extendable (✅ owner 16 basic §6; ⚠️ statutory section not checked).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As above.
**Links:** ENV-3034, ENV-3036
**Trap:** Different remedies run on different limitation clocks; do not apply the 30-day clearance rule to compensation claims.
**Mains hook:** Time limits make NGT relief fast but also strictly bounded.
**Back:** *"Six months to decide, ninety days to appeal, five years to claim, thirty days to challenge."*

### Flashcard ENV-3036 — NGT jurisdiction and the Schedule I list
**Front:** Which seven enactments are listed in Schedule I of the NGT Act, and what is excluded?
**Definition (learn):** The NGT's original jurisdiction covers substantial questions relating to the environment arising under the statutes in its Schedule I. The Schedule lists seven Acts.
**Breakdown:**
- Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1974
- Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Cess Act, 1977
- Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980
- Air (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act, 1981
- Environment (Protection) Act, 1986
- Public Liability Insurance Act, 1991
- Biological Diversity Act, 2002
- Excluded: Indian Forest Act, 1927; Wildlife (Protection) Act, 1972; state tree and forest laws (✅ Wikipedia: National Green Tribunal).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Seven Acts (✅ Wikipedia). Exclusion of WPA 1972 and IFA 1927 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ENV-3037, ENV-3038, ENV-3049
**Trap:** "NGT has unlimited environmental jurisdiction" is wrong; wildlife matters have raised forum questions (✅ owner 16 advanced §3).
**Mains hook:** Schedule-bound jurisdiction keeps the NGT specialised but leaves gaps that ordinary courts fill.
**Back:** *"Seven Acts on the list; Wildlife and Indian Forest stay out."*

### Flashcard ENV-3037 — The Water Cess Act, 1977: list versus operative status (conflict)
**Front:** Is the Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Cess Act, 1977 still operative, given its place in NGT Schedule I?
**Definition (learn):** The NGT Act's Schedule I names the 1977 Cess Act. The owner file states the Cess Act was repealed in 2017, so the schedule text and the current operative status must be distinguished.
**Breakdown:**
- Schedule I text: lists the 1977 Cess Act (✅ Wikipedia).
- Owner claim: repealed in 2017 (✅ owner 16 basic §2, §6). ⚠️ This build did not verify the repealing Act.
- Exam handling: say what the schedule says and state the repeal status separately.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1977 Cess Act; repeal 2017 (owner claim, unverified).
**Links:** ENV-3036
**Trap:** Treating the schedule as proof the Act is in force. Owner and Wikipedia wording differ on current status; flag before citing.
**Mains hook:** Not needed for Mains; use only for a Prelims statement on NGT Schedule I.
**Back:** *"On the list, off the books: check the repeal."*

### Flashcard ENV-3038 — NGT Section 20: sustainable development and the core principles
**Front:** What principles must the NGT apply in deciding cases?
**Definition (learn):** Section 20 of the NGT Act directs the Tribunal to apply the principles of sustainable development, the precautionary principle and the polluter pays principle.
**Breakdown:**
- Sustainable development.
- Precautionary principle.
- Polluter pays principle.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §2, NGT Act s.20).
**Links:** ENV-3039, ENV-3036
**Trap:** Section 20 principles are applied as directions, not as optional slogans; yet a precautionary order still needs reasoning.
**Mains hook:** Use NGT cases to show these principles operating in practice, not as textbook definitions (✅ owner 16 basic §10).
**Back:** *"Section 20: sustain, guard early, polluter pays."*

### Flashcard ENV-3039 — Polluter Pays versus Precautionary Principle
**Front:** Define the Polluter Pays and Precautionary principles and state their key trap.
**Definition (learn):** Polluter Pays places the remediation cost on the party causing pollution. The Precautionary Principle allows preventive action even without full scientific certainty of harm.
**Breakdown:**
- Polluter Pays: cost of remediation falls on the polluter.
- Precautionary: act before full certainty.
- Trap: precaution does not require certainty first.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §2, §6–7).
**Links:** ENV-3038, ENV-3040
**Trap:** "Precautionary Principle requires full scientific certainty before action" is wrong (✅ owner 16 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Pair the two principles: polluter pays addresses who bears cost; precaution addresses when to act.
**Back:** *"Payer pays the cure; the precautious act before the proof."*

### Flashcard ENV-3040 — Post-facto ("ex post facto") environmental clearance
**Front:** What is post-facto environmental clearance and how have courts treated it?
**Definition (learn):** Post-facto clearance is clearance granted after a project has started or completed construction or operation without prior clearance. Courts and the NGT treat it as exceptional, not routine. The Supreme Court in Alembic Pharmaceuticals Ltd. v. Rohit Prajapati (2020) held it alien to environmental jurisprudence.
**Breakdown:**
- Critic view: inverts the preventive logic of EIA; a regularisation tool.
- Supporter view: brings uncleared projects under monitoring.
- Judicial stance: exceptional, not routine.
- 2025 ruling: the owner says an executive route for regularisation was struck down; name and date unverified.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Alembic (2020) (✅ owner 16 basic §7; not fetched). 2025 ruling (⚠️ owner: verify case name and date before citing).
**Links:** ENV-3027, ENV-3041, ENV-3045
**Trap:** Saying post-facto clearance is "judicially endorsed as routine" is wrong (✅ owner 16 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** Prior clearance is the substance of prevention; an assessment after construction cannot change the decision it was meant to inform.
**Back:** *"Clearance after the concrete? Alembic says alien."*

### Flashcard ENV-3041 — Instrument hierarchy: draft, notification, office memorandum, judgment
**Front:** Rank the legal force of a draft EIA notification, a final notification, an office memorandum and a judgment.
**Definition (learn):** Each instrument has different legal force. A judgment binds, a notification has statutory effect, an office memorandum is an executive instruction, and a draft is a proposal with no force until notified.
**Breakdown:**
- Statute and binding judgment: highest.
- Final gazette notification: statutory effect under the Act.
- Office memorandum: executive instruction; cannot override a notification or statute.
- Draft notification: proposal only.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 advanced §9 caution; ⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** ENV-3040, ENV-3042, ENV-3043
**Trap:** Treating a draft or office memorandum as settled law.
**Mains hook:** Many EIA controversies are disputes about this hierarchy; saying so is high-value analysis.
**Back:** *"Statute, judgment, notification, memo, draft: descending power."*

### Flashcard ENV-3042 — PARIVESH and PARIVESH 3.0
**Front:** What is PARIVESH and what does version 3.0 add?
**Definition (learn):** PARIVESH (Pro-Active and Responsive facilitation by Interactive, Virtuous and Environmental Single window Hub) is the national single-window platform for environment, forest, wildlife and CRZ clearances. PARIVESH 3.0 adds baseline environmental data, compensatory-afforestation land banks, inter-ministerial dashboards and AI-enabled support.
**Breakdown:**
- Coverage: environment, forest, wildlife, CRZ clearances.
- 3.0 features: baseline data; compensatory-afforestation land banks; dashboards; AI support.
- Post-approval compliance monitoring is included in the platform's role.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §2, §6, cited to Economic Survey 2025-26 Ch. 10; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3043, ENV-3047
**Trap:** Single-window does not remove the need for substantive appraisal or consultation.
**Mains hook:** Digital single windows cut delay but do not fix weak post-clearance monitoring.
**Back:** *"PARIVESH: one window, three-point-oh, with AI."*

### Flashcard ENV-3043 — Reform direction and Survey-listed weaknesses (2025–26)
**Front:** What reform direction does the Economic Survey 2025-26 describe for EIA, and what weaknesses does it admit?
**Definition (learn):** The Survey describes a shift to risk-based, outcome-oriented, trust-based regulation with PARIVESH 3.0, the Environment Audit Rules, 2025 (certified third-party auditors), uniform SPCB consent guidelines, a Blue industry category, streamlined rules for critical minerals and an October 2025 green-belt rationalisation by pollution potential instead of a blanket 33% norm.
**Breakdown:**
- Reform tools: PARIVESH 3.0; Environment Audit Rules 2025; uniform SPCB consent; Blue category; critical-mineral streamlining; green-belt rationalisation (Oct 2025).
- Admitted weaknesses: multiplicity of clearances, weak post-clearance monitoring, SPCB capacity, inter-state variation, litigation uncertainty.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §8, owner 16 advanced §9, cited to Economic Survey 2025-26 Ch. 10; ⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3042, ENV-3041, ENV-3044
**Trap:** Quoting the Survey's reform list without its own admitted weaknesses; the advanced answer uses both halves.
**Mains hook:** Trust-based regulation presumes credible verification, which the Survey itself says is weak.
**Back:** *"Trust-based reform, weak monitoring admitted: quote both halves."*

### Flashcard ENV-3044 — PYQ 2020 GS-III Q7: Draft EIA 2020 versus 2006 notification
**Front:** What does the 2020 PYQ on the draft EIA Notification test?
**Definition (learn):** The 2020 GS-III Q7 (10 marks, 150 words) asks how the Draft EIA Notification 2020 differs from the 2006 notification.
**Breakdown:**
- Compare: categories, screening, public consultation, post-facto provisions, appraisal.
- Qualify: a draft is not a final notification (ENV-3041).
- Route: owner routes it to the owning topic.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §PYQ block 2018–2023).
**Links:** ENV-3041, ENV-3031, ENV-3043
**Trap:** Describing the draft as the law in force.
**Mains hook:** Use the draft-versus-notification distinction as the opening line of a 10-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Draft is a proposal; 2006 is the rule until notified."*

### Flashcard ENV-3045 — PYQ 2024 GS-III Q8: NGOs and EIA outcomes (cases one and two)
**Front:** Which cases can support "role of environmental NGOs and activists in EIA outcomes"?
**Definition (learn):** The 2024 GS-III Q8 (10 marks, 150 words, cite examples) asks about NGO and activist influence. Two owner-verified cases:
**Breakdown:**
- Mopa Greenfield Airport, Goa — Hanuman Laxman Aroskar v. Union of India (SC, 29 March 2019): litigation exposed appraisal and baseline concerns; Court required fresh expert review and suspended the earlier route.
- Niyamgiri bauxite, Odisha — Orissa Mining Corporation v. MoEF (18 April 2013): Dongria Kondh mobilisation and Gram Sabha process shaped the refusal; a forest and community-consent case.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dates as in owner 16 advanced §13.1 (⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3046, ENV-3031, ENV-3040
**Trap:** Do not call Mopa a permanent cancellation; later orders changed its procedural position. Niyamgiri is principally a forest-rights example, not an EIA-2006 hearing.
**Mains hook:** Activism influences scrutiny, remand or refusal; it does not replace expert appraisal.
**Back:** *"Mopa re-tested, Niyamgiri consented; neither is a routine hearing."*

### Flashcard ENV-3046 — PYQ 2024 GS-III Q8: POSCO and Kudremukh (cases three and four)
**Front:** Which further cases complete the four-example answer on NGO and activist influence?
**Definition (learn):** Two more owner-listed cases:
**Breakdown:**
- POSCO steel, Jagatsinghpur, Odisha — Prafulla Samantray v. Union of India (NGT Appeal 8/2011, 30 March 2012): NGT suspended or set aside the 31 January 2011 EC, because only the first 4-MTPA phase of a 12-MTPA project had been appraised; fresh full-project review ordered.
- Kudremukh iron ore, Karnataka — T.N. Godavarman / K.M. Chinnappa v. Union of India (30 October 2002): mining inside the National Park confined and wind-up ordered by end-2005.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Dates per owner 16 advanced §13.1 (⚠️ not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3045, ENV-3047
**Trap:** Do not claim activism alone cancelled POSCO; the NGT remedy was procedural. Kudremukh is a wildlife case, not a 2006 EIA hearing.
**Mains hook:** Each case shows a different route: appraisal gap, protected-area enforcement.
**Back:** *"POSCO's phases, Kudremukh's park: procedure and wildlife."*

### Flashcard ENV-3047 — PYQ 2025 GS-III Q17: mining as environmental hazard
**Front:** What hazard chain and remedies does the 2025 mining question (15 marks, 250 words) require?
**Definition (learn):** Mining hazards form a chain: vegetation and topsoil loss leads to erosion and habitat loss; drilling, blasting and haulage bring dust and noise; overburden and tailings bring sediment and chemical drainage; dewatering brings water stress; abandoned workings bring long-lived safety liability.
**Breakdown:**
- Hazards: erosion/habitat loss; dust/noise; drainage/chemicals; groundwater stress; abandoned dumps.
- Remedies: strategic siting and cumulative impact assessment; mine plan, EMP and monitoring; progressive reclamation and closure finance; community/FRA due diligence.
- Cases: Kudremukh as conservation risk; Jharia fires only as legacy illustration.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 advanced §13.2; ⚠️ no area or casualty figures verified).
**Links:** ENV-3046, ENV-3016, ENV-3048
**Trap:** Do not attach unverified casualty or financial figures to Jharia.
**Mains hook:** Closure plans are not proof of restoration; test them by achieved reclamation.
**Back:** *"Dig, dust, drain, dewater, abandon: remedy at every stage."*

### Flashcard ENV-3048 — Trap: NGT replaces courts and all clearances are required
**Front:** Which two EIA and NGT myths does the owner flag as Prelims traps?
**Definition (learn):** Myth one: NGT has replaced the Supreme Court and High Courts for environmental cases. Myth two: every project needs environmental clearance under the Notification.
**Breakdown:**
- Myth one: NGT is an additional forum; courts keep writ jurisdiction.
- Myth two: only scheduled projects and activities need clearance; others fall outside the Notification.
- Also: public consultation is not required for all categories.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §7).
**Links:** ENV-3027, ENV-3028, ENV-3050
**Trap:** The two myths above are classic close-option pairs.
**Mains hook:** Specialised tribunals complement constitutional courts; they do not displace them.
**Back:** *"NGT adds a forum, not a monopoly; only scheduled projects need clearance."*

### Flashcard ENV-3049 — Trap: NGT orders are self-executing and jurisdiction is unlimited
**Front:** Why are NGT orders not self-executing, and why is NGT jurisdiction not unlimited?
**Definition (learn):** NGT orders depend on executive and state-agency compliance. Its original jurisdiction is limited to the Schedule I statutes and some environment-adjacent matters raise forum questions.
**Breakdown:**
- Compliance: executive capacity, not tribunal design, decides implementation.
- Jurisdiction: schedule-bound (ENV-3036).
- Staffing: bench strength and regional availability can slow disposal (✅ owner 16 advanced §3).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 advanced §3, §7–8).
**Links:** ENV-3036, ENV-3033, ENV-3050
**Trap:** "NGT orders need no further action" is wrong; "NGT can hear any environmental dispute" is wrong.
**Mains hook:** A well-designed tribunal is necessary but not sufficient without matching implementation capacity.
**Back:** *"Orders need officers; jurisdiction needs a schedule."*

### Flashcard ENV-3050 — Trap: B2 needs a full EIA, and the Precautionary Principle needs certainty
**Front:** Which two EIA traps test category logic and the precautionary principle?
**Definition (learn):** Category B2 projects are screened out of the detailed EIA and public-consultation requirement. The Precautionary Principle allows preventive action without full scientific certainty of harm.
**Breakdown:**
- B2: no detailed EIA report (ENV-3029).
- Precautionary: act before full certainty (ENV-3039).
- Both are close-option pairs in Prelims.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 16 basic §7).
**Links:** ENV-3029, ENV-3039, ENV-3048
**Trap:** "All projects require clearance" and "public consultation is for every category" are the other traps in the same owner.
**Mains hook:** Precaution justifies early refusal or conditions; it is not a licence for unlimited restriction.
**Back:** *"B2 skips the report; precaution skips the proof, not the reason."*

### GROUP — Climate Change Science Greenhouse Effect

### Flashcard ENV-3051 — Natural greenhouse effect versus human enhancement
**Front:** How do the natural greenhouse effect and anthropogenic climate change differ?
**Definition (learn):** The natural greenhouse effect is the life-enabling trapping of outgoing longwave radiation by atmospheric gases. Climate change, in the policy sense, is the human-driven enhancement of that effect through rising greenhouse-gas concentrations.
**Breakdown:**
- Natural: sun's shortwave in, surface re-emits longwave, GHGs absorb and re-radiate.
- Enhanced: fossil fuels, deforestation, agriculture, industry raise GHG concentrations.
- Result: more heat trapped → rising global average temperature.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Warming effect about 33°C above a no-greenhouse Earth (✅ owner 17 basic §3).
**Links:** ENV-3052, ENV-3053, ENV-3054
**Trap:** The greenhouse effect itself is not the problem; its human enhancement is.
**Mains hook:** Frame causation precisely: natural process, anthropogenic forcing, impact pathway.
**Back:** *"Natural blanket keeps us alive; human extra blankets are the problem."*

### Flashcard ENV-3052 — Greenhouse mechanism: shortwave in, longwave out
**Front:** Trace the energy path that produces the greenhouse effect.
**Definition (learn):** Incoming solar shortwave radiation passes largely through the atmosphere and warms the surface. The surface re-emits energy as longwave (infrared). Greenhouse gases absorb part of this and re-radiate it in all directions, including back to the surface.
**Breakdown:**
- Step 1: shortwave from the Sun reaches the surface.
- Step 2: surface emits longwave (infrared).
- Step 3: GHGs absorb and re-radiate longwave.
- Step 4: surface stays warm enough for life.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** About 33°C warmer than without the effect (✅ owner 17 basic §3).
**Links:** ENV-3051, ENV-3053
**Trap:** Confusing shortwave (incoming) with longwave (outgoing, absorbed by GHGs).
**Mains hook:** Use the mechanism to explain why adding GHGs raises radiative forcing.
**Back:** *"Short in, long out, gases hold it back."*

### Flashcard ENV-3053 — Principal greenhouse gases
**Front:** Name the principal greenhouse gases and note the water-vapour exception.
**Definition (learn):** The principal greenhouse gases are water vapour, carbon dioxide, methane, nitrous oxide and fluorinated gases such as HFCs.
**Breakdown:**
- Water vapour: most abundant; responds to temperature rather than driving new forcing; not a primary policy target.
- CO₂: long-lived, large volume.
- CH₄: high potency, shorter lifetime.
- N₂O: long-lived, nitrogen-cycle linked.
- Fluorinated gases (e.g., HFCs).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §2, §6; ✅ owner 17 advanced §2).
**Links:** ENV-3055, ENV-3056, ENV-3057
**Trap:** "Water vapour is the main policy target" is wrong; it is the most abundant but a feedback, not a driver.
**Mains hook:** Gas-specific mitigation needs gas-specific potency and lifetime data.
**Back:** *"Water, carbon, methane, nitrous, fluorinated: water follows temperature."*

### Flashcard ENV-3054 — Global warming versus climate change
**Front:** Distinguish global warming from climate change.
**Definition (learn):** Global warming is the observed long-term rise in Earth's average surface temperature, attributed mainly to anthropogenic GHG emissions. Climate change is the broader term: warming plus shifts in precipitation, extreme events and sea-level rise.
**Breakdown:**
- Global warming: temperature.
- Climate change: temperature plus weather-pattern and sea-level effects.
- Interchangeable use is a trap.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §2).
**Links:** ENV-3051, ENV-3065
**Trap:** "Global warming and climate change are identical" is wrong (✅ owner 17 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Use the broader term when discussing impacts; the narrower one when discussing temperature.
**Back:** *"Warming is the thermometer; climate change is the whole weather."*

### Flashcard ENV-3055 — Radiative forcing
**Front:** Define radiative forcing.
**Definition (learn):** Radiative forcing is the change in Earth's energy balance caused by a factor such as a GHG or an aerosol. Positive forcing warms; negative forcing cools.
**Breakdown:**
- CO₂, CH₄, N₂O: positive forcing (warming).
- Aerosols: net negative forcing (cooling), partially masking GHG warming (ENV-3060).
- Use: compare drivers on a common energy-balance scale.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §2; ✅ owner 17 advanced §2).
**Links:** ENV-3053, ENV-3060
**Trap:** Forcing is an energy-balance change, not a temperature reading.
**Mains hook:** Radiative forcing is the bridge from physics to policy comparison across gases.
**Back:** *"Forcing: the energy-balance push, plus or minus."*

### Flashcard ENV-3056 — Global Warming Potential and the time horizon
**Front:** What is GWP, and why must the horizon be stated?
**Definition (learn):** GWP compares a gas's heat-trapping over a set period with CO₂, which equals 1 by definition. Results depend strongly on the horizon, so GWP-20 and GWP-100 must be quoted separately.
**Breakdown:**
- CO₂ = 1 (reference).
- Methane looks more urgent on a 20-year horizon than on a 100-year one.
- Rule: never quote a bare "GWP".
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Horizon rule (✅ owner 17 basic §2, §6). Owner gives no numeric GWP values; none are cited here.
**Links:** ENV-3057, ENV-3062
**Trap:** Quoting a bare GWP figure without its horizon.
**Mains hook:** Gas-specific policy must choose a horizon and say why.
**Back:** *"CO₂ is the ruler; always say 20 or 100 years."*

### Flashcard ENV-3057 — Atmospheric lifetime: methane versus CO₂
**Front:** Compare the atmospheric lifetime of methane and CO₂.
**Definition (learn):** Methane has a much higher potency than CO₂ over short periods but a much shorter atmospheric lifetime, about a decade (owner advanced). CO₂ persists for centuries, so past emissions keep warming.
**Breakdown:**
- Methane: high potency, short lifetime (~decade), near-term cuts pay off faster.
- CO₂: long lifetime (centuries), a stock problem.
- Implication: methane cuts give near-term relief; CO₂ cuts address cumulative warming.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Methane ~decade; CO₂ centuries (✅ owner 17 advanced §2, ⚠️ approximate, not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-3056, ENV-3062, ENV-3069
**Trap:** Saying methane is more potent than CO₂ "molecule for molecule, always" without the horizon.
**Mains hook:** Differentiate mitigation by lifetime and potency, not a single "reduce emissions" message.
**Back:** *"Methane burns hot and fades; CO₂ lingers for centuries."*

### Flashcard ENV-3058 — Albedo and the ice-albedo effect
**Front:** Define albedo and explain why it matters in warming.
**Definition (learn):** Albedo is a surface's reflectivity. Fresh snow and ice have high albedo; open ocean and dark soil have low albedo.
**Breakdown:**
- High albedo: reflects incoming solar energy.
- Low albedo: absorbs heat.
- Melting ice exposes darker surfaces → more absorption → more melting (ice-albedo feedback).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §2).
**Links:** ENV-3059, ENV-3061
**Trap:** Albedo is a surface property, not a gas property.
**Mains hook:** Himalayan ice loss has a feedback risk beyond water supply.
**Back:** *"White reflects, dark absorbs, melt feeds melt."*

### Flashcard ENV-3059 — Climate feedbacks: positive means amplifying
**Front:** Name the key positive feedbacks and define "positive".
**Definition (learn):** A feedback is a process triggered by warming that either amplifies it (positive) or dampens it (negative). Positive here means amplifying, not beneficial.
**Breakdown:**
- Ice-albedo: melt exposes dark surfaces.
- Water vapour: warmer air holds more vapour, itself a GHG.
- Permafrost carbon and methane release.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §2, §6).
**Links:** ENV-3058, ENV-3053
**Trap:** "A positive feedback is a beneficial one" is wrong (✅ owner 17 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Feedbacks explain why warming can accelerate after a threshold, a point to raise in impact answers.
**Back:** *"Positive is amplifying, never a good thing."*

### Flashcard ENV-3060 — Aerosols cool and the air-quality masking tension
**Front:** Why does cutting air pollution have a climate trade-off?
**Definition (learn):** Aerosols such as sulphate from coal combustion mostly cool the surface by scattering sunlight and seeding clouds. Removing them for health reasons can unmask some warming.
**Breakdown:**
- Aerosol effect: net cooling.
- Health gain: real and immediate.
- Climate effect: near-term warming can rise as masking falls.
- Resolution: cut CO₂ and methane regardless.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §2, §6; ✅ owner 17 advanced §2, §6).
**Links:** ENV-3055, ENV-3061, ENV-3013
**Trap:** "All particles warm the planet" is wrong; most aerosols cool (✅ owner 17 basic §7). "Pollution reduction has no bearing on warming" is also wrong (✅ owner 17 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** Air-quality and climate goals are complementary but not automatically aligned in the short run.
**Back:** *"Clean air unmasks heat: cut CO₂ anyway."*

### Flashcard ENV-3061 — Black carbon: a warming short-lived forcer
**Front:** Why is black carbon uniquely important in the Himalaya and the Indo-Gangetic Plain?
**Definition (learn):** Black carbon is a short-lived climate forcer that warms the atmosphere and darkens snow and ice, reducing albedo. It is the notable warming exception among aerosols.
**Breakdown:**
- Warms the air.
- Darkens snow/ice (albedo loss).
- Sources named in owner: cookstoves, brick kilns, diesel.
- Dual benefit: health and near-term temperature.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §2, §5, §6).
**Links:** ENV-3062, ENV-3058, ENV-3060
**Trap:** "Aerosols always cool" fails for black carbon.
**Mains hook:** Black carbon control gives health co-benefits and rapid temperature relief in Indian regions.
**Back:** *"Black carbon warms the air and darkens the snow."*

### Flashcard ENV-3062 — Short-lived climate forcers and the CO₂ trap
**Front:** Define SLCFs and state the near-term versus long-term trap.
**Definition (learn):** SLCFs (short-lived climate forcers) are gases and particles with atmospheric lifetimes far shorter than CO₂: methane, tropospheric ozone, black carbon and some HFCs.
**Breakdown:**
- Near-term: cutting SLCFs yields quick temperature benefit.
- Long-term: only CO₂ reduction addresses cumulative warming.
- Co-benefit: black carbon control improves health.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §2, §7).
**Links:** ENV-3057, ENV-3061, ENV-3069
**Trap:** "Cutting methane and black carbon equals cutting CO₂" is wrong (✅ owner 17 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Two-timescale strategy: SLCFs for speed, CO₂ for the stock.
**Back:** *"Fast fades for speed; CO₂ for the long haul."*

### Flashcard ENV-3063 — Carbon sinks, sources and the flip risk
**Front:** Define a carbon sink and a source, and state the flip risk.
**Definition (learn):** A carbon sink absorbs more carbon than it releases (forests, oceans, soils); a source releases more than it absorbs.
**Breakdown:**
- Sinks: forests, oceans, soils.
- Source: a reservoir net-releasing carbon.
- Flip: a forest after severe fire or dieback can become a source.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §2).
**Links:** ENV-3064, ENV-3069
**Trap:** Assuming a sink is permanent.
**Mains hook:** Sink credits (forests, land) need durability checks.
**Back:** *"Sinks can turn sources after fire."*

### Flashcard ENV-3064 — Ocean acidification: lower pH, not acidic seawater
**Front:** Explain ocean acidification and its two traps.
**Definition (learn):** Oceans absorb a large share of atmospheric CO₂, which forms carbonic acid and lowers pH. Average seawater remains alkaline; acidification is a decline in pH and carbonate-ion availability.
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: CO₂ uptake by oceans, not temperature alone.
- Effect: lower pH, reduced carbonate-ion availability.
- Status: still alkaline on average.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §3, §6).
**Links:** ENV-3063, ENV-3065
**Trap:** "Caused by warming alone" and "the ocean has become acidic" are both wrong (✅ owner 17 basic §7).
**Mains hook:** Acidification is a non-temperature consequence of CO₂, relevant to reefs and fisheries.
**Back:** *"CO₂ in, pH down, still alkaline."*

### Flashcard ENV-3065 — Small-island habitability pathway
**Front:** How does climate change threaten small islands beyond inundation?
**Definition (learn):** Relative sea-level rise combined with waves, surge and reef or sediment change produces chronic flooding, erosion and saline intrusion. These undermine infrastructure, settlement space, freshwater lenses, crops and sanitation, so habitability fails before permanent submergence.
**Breakdown:**
- Chain: relative sea-level rise + waves/surge + reef/sediment change.
- Pathways: chronic flooding; erosion and land loss; saline intrusion.
- Consequences: infrastructure, settlement, freshwater and food; culture and sovereignty.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §5.1, cited to IPCC AR6 WG-II Ch. 15 and SROCC Ch. 4).
**Links:** ENV-3066, ENV-3067, ENV-3018
**Trap:** Deterministic claims that every island vanishes on one date (ENV-3066 qualification).
**Mains hook:** Frame the answer as habitability and adaptation limits, not only sea level.
**Back:** *"Flood, erode, salt: habitability falls before the sea wins."*

### Flashcard ENV-3066 — Named island examples and qualifications
**Front:** Which island countries does the owner use as examples, and what qualifications apply?
**Definition (learn):** The owner uses the Maldives, Kiribati, Tuvalu and Marshall Islands as low-lying island examples. Each differs in elevation, exposure and adaptation capacity.
**Breakdown:**
- Named: Maldives, Kiribati, Tuvalu, Marshall Islands.
- Freshwater lens dependence (thin lenses under coral islands).
- Reef sediment maintains islands; warming, bleaching and acidification weaken it.
- Some reef islands can accrete, migrate or be raised.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §5.1).
**Links:** ENV-3065, ENV-3064
**Trap:** Treating the four islands as identical cases.
**Mains hook:** Use a named case to illustrate a pathway, not to forecast.
**Back:** *"Maldives, Kiribati, Tuvalu, Marshall: same risk chain, different odds."*

### Flashcard ENV-3067 — PYQ 2025 GS-I Q4: small islands and sea-level rise (10 marks)
**Front:** What answer route does the 2025 GS-I Q4 require?
**Definition (learn):** The question asks how climate change and sea-level rise affect island nations (10 marks, 150 words). Route: sea-level mechanism, flooding and erosion, freshwater and reef-sediment pathways, named islands, adaptation and its limits.
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: relative sea-level rise and extremes.
- Pathways: flood, erosion, saline intrusion, reef decline.
- Examples: two named islands.
- Close: adaptation limits; habitability rather than automatic disappearance.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic §9, §13).
**Links:** ENV-3065, ENV-3066, ENV-3091
**Trap:** Deterministic "islands will drown" conclusion.
**Mains hook:** Conclude on habitability and adaptation finance, the qualified verdict.
**Back:** *"Mechanism, pathways, two islands, limits, habitability."*

### Flashcard ENV-3068 — Remaining carbon budget and annual emissions (AR6)
**Front:** What remaining carbon budget does AR6 give for a 50% chance of holding 1.5°C, and from when?
**Definition (learn):** AR6 estimates roughly 500 GtCO₂ remaining for a 50% chance of limiting warming to 1.5°C, counted from the start of 2020. The budget depletes with every year of emissions.
**Breakdown:**
- Budget: about 500 GtCO₂ from start-2020 (✅ owner 17 basic §8, ✅ owner 17 advanced §3).
- Global emissions: about 40 GtCO₂ per year (⚠️ owner advanced estimate; not verified).
- Implication: budget ÷ annual rate gives approximate years remaining.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** AR6 Synthesis Report, March 2023 (✅ Wikipedia: IPCC for report completion date; ⚠️ the 500 GtCO₂ figure was not confirmed by a fetched page in this build).
**Links:** ENV-3069, ENV-3018
**Trap:** Quoting 500 GtCO₂ as a static figure; always attach its 2020 baseline year.
**Mains hook:** Quantitative budget reasoning makes the case for front-loaded cuts.
**Back:** *"Five hundred gigatonnes from 2020, shrinking every year."*

### Flashcard ENV-3069 — Carbon budget logic: front-loaded cuts over distant net zero
**Front:** Why does a finite carbon budget argue for near-term cuts rather than a distant net-zero date?
**Definition (learn):** Under a fixed cumulative-emissions limit, delaying cuts uses the budget faster and requires steeper reductions later. Timing and rate matter, not only the end date.
**Breakdown:**
- Fixed budget → depleting resource.
- Delay → steeper later cuts.
- Equity link: developed countries have used a disproportionate share (ENV-3110).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 advanced §3; ⚠️ analytical).
**Links:** ENV-3068, ENV-3062, ENV-3110
**Trap:** A distant net-zero date alone keeps the budget safe regardless of near-term emissions; this is wrong (✅ owner 17 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** Use the budget framing to argue for front-loaded reductions and equity.
**Back:** *"Delay spends the budget and raises the bill."*

### Flashcard ENV-3070 — Attribution science and variable confidence by event type
**Front:** What is attribution science and why is confidence uneven across event types?
**Definition (learn):** Attribution science compares observed events with climate-model ensembles run with and without human forcing to estimate how much human influence changed an event's likelihood or intensity.
**Breakdown:**
- High confidence: heatwaves and heavy precipitation categories (AR6 strengthened).
- Lower or more complex: specific storm tracks, droughts, individual tropical cyclones.
- Rule: do not blanket-attribute every flood to climate change.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 advanced §1, §5).
**Links:** ENV-3054, ENV-3051
**Trap:** "All extreme weather is equally attributable" is wrong (✅ owner 17 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** Precise attribution claims strengthen credibility in disaster and heat answers.
**Back:** *"Heatwaves: high confidence; storms and droughts: check the model."*

### Flashcard ENV-3071 — India's gas-specific emissions profile
**Front:** Which sectors dominate India's CO₂ and which dominate its methane and nitrous oxide?
**Definition (learn):** India's CO₂ is dominated by energy (power, industry, transport). Agriculture (livestock enteric fermentation, rice cultivation and fertiliser use) contributes a disproportionately larger share of methane and nitrous oxide.
**Breakdown:**
- CO₂: energy sector.
- CH₄ and N₂O: agriculture.
- Implication: India's mitigation priorities are gas-specific.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (⚠️ owner 17 advanced §4, from national communications and BURs, reference years not stated).
**Links:** ENV-3053, ENV-3072, ENV-3057
**Trap:** A uniform sectoral profile across all gases is wrong (✅ owner 17 advanced §8).
**Mains hook:** India's methane policy pulls agriculture into climate mitigation, a sensitive area.
**Back:** *"CO₂ is energy, methane and nitrous are farms."*

### Flashcard ENV-3072 — India's per-capita versus absolute emissions: metric discipline
**Front:** How should India's emissions be compared with developed countries?
**Definition (learn):** India's per-capita and historical cumulative emissions are far below major developed economies, while its absolute annual emissions have risen with growth. Each claim uses a different metric.
**Breakdown:**
- Per-capita: low.
- Cumulative historical: low.
- Absolute annual: rising.
- Rule: state the metric every time.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (⚠️ owner 17 advanced §4; not verified against a dated national inventory here).
**Links:** ENV-3071, ENV-3110, ENV-3136
**Trap:** Conflating per-capita and absolute totals.
**Mains hook:** Equity arguments require the metric to be named.
**Back:** *"Per head, cumulative, or total: name the ruler."*

### Flashcard ENV-3073 — PYQ routing 2018–2019: carbon fertilisation, geoengineering, methane hydrates
**Front:** Which 2018–2019 Prelims items route to climate science?
**Definition (learn):** Three objective items: 2018 Q65 (carbon fertilisation effect on plant growth and atmosphere), 2019 Q25 (geoengineering techniques to counter warming), 2019 Q34 (methane hydrate deposits and climate linkage).
**Breakdown:**
- 2018 Prelims GS-I Q65: carbon fertilisation.
- 2019 Prelims GS-I Q25: geoengineering.
- 2019 Prelims GS-I Q34: methane hydrates.
- Keys not held locally (✅ owner 17 basic PYQ block).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 17 basic PYQ block 2018–2023).
**Links:** ENV-3053, ENV-3057, ENV-3062
