**Key facts / Dates / Data:** DAC is generally more energy-intensive per tonne than CCUS (✅ owner 21, Section 6). Prelims 2025 Q36 routed to "Direct Air Capture as emerging technology" (answer not recorded in owner).
**Links:** ENV-4005, ENV-4003, ENV-4025
**Trap:** "DAC automatically delivers permanent removal." It only separates CO₂; a removal claim needs durable storage and lifecycle verification.
**Mains hook:** DAC is an emerging, small-scale, high-cost stage, so its value lies in balancing residual emissions, not in substituting for mitigation.
**Back:** *"Dilute air means big fans and big energy: DAC separates, it does not remove."*

### Flashcard ENV-4005 — DAC vs DACCS: separation vs removal
**Front:** Distinguish DAC, DACCS and a CDR claim.
**Definition (learn):** DACCS is DAC followed by transport and durable storage, normally geological. It qualifies as CDR only after lifecycle emissions, leakage, monitoring and storage durability are accounted for.
**Breakdown:**
- DAC → separation (step 1)
- DACCS → separation + transport + durable storage (step 1–3)
- DAC-to-fuel or short-lived products → recycling or possible avoidance, normally not durable removal
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 21, Sections 2 and 3.5)
**Links:** ENV-4004, ENV-4006, ENV-4022 (removal claim checklist)
**Trap:** Counting every tonne separated by DAC as removed, even when it is later combusted or released.
**Mains hook:** Judge DACCS by storage medium, expected duration, energy source, MRV and liability, not by the separation step alone.
**Back:** *"DAC is the pick-up, DACCS is the parking garage with a lock."*

### Flashcard ENV-4006 — CDR definition and portfolio (IPCC)
**Front:** What is carbon dioxide removal (CDR) and what methods make up the portfolio?
**Definition (learn):** Human activity that intentionally removes CO₂ from the atmosphere and durably stores it in geological, terrestrial, oceanic or product reservoirs. Avoiding an emission or issuing a market credit is not itself CDR.
**Breakdown:**
- Biological/terrestrial: afforestation, reforestation, ecosystem restoration, soil carbon
- Biological-product: biochar and durable harvested-wood/product routes
- Bioenergy-technological: BECCS
- Technological: DACCS
- Geochemical: enhanced weathering/mineralisation
- Ocean-based: ocean alkalinity enhancement and other proposed approaches
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** IPCC AR6 WGIII Chapter 12 Section 12.3 and CDR factsheet are the cited sources (✅ owner 21, Section 14). Owner-verified; not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-4001, ENV-4005, ENV-4007, ENV-4024 (method comparison)
**Trap:** Treating CDR routes as interchangeable or equally permanent. Each has different storage saturation, reversal, MRV and time-horizon risks.
**Mains hook:** The CDR portfolio is diversified but method-specific. Protecting an existing sink avoids emissions rather than creating a new removal.
**Back:** *"Six routes, one atmosphere: each stores carbon for a different length of time and needs its own proof."*

### Flashcard ENV-4007 — Avoidance vs removal: the central CCUS distinction
**Front:** Why is fossil-power-plant CCUS normally not "negative emissions"?
**Definition (learn):** CCUS on a fossil or industrial point source prevents part of the newly generated CO₂ from reaching the atmosphere. That is emissions avoidance or reduction, not atmospheric removal, even if the CO₂ is securely stored.
**Breakdown:**
- Avoidance → stops new CO₂ from entering the air (point-source CCUS)
- Removal → withdraws CO₂ already in the air and stores it durably (DACCS, BECCS if net-negative, biological CDR)
- The CCUS fossil-source chain is counted as reduction against a counterfactual
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 21, Advanced Section 3.1 and Section 8)
**Links:** ENV-4003, ENV-4006, ENV-4022
**Trap:** "All CCUS applications deliver negative emissions." Standard fossil/process-source CCUS does not.
**Mains hook:** Use avoidance-versus-removal to show analytical depth; judging CCUS as a reduction tool keeps it from becoming a licence to delay direct mitigation.
**Back:** *"Capture avoids what would have been added; removal takes out what is already there."*

### Flashcard ENV-4008 — Indian Carbon Market (CCTS): legal basis and dual structure
**Front:** What is the legal basis and structure of the Indian Carbon Market?
**Definition (learn):** India's domestic carbon market, enabled by the Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act, 2022 and given effect by the Carbon Credit Trading Scheme (CCTS) notified in June 2023. It has a dual structure: a compliance mechanism for obligated energy-intensive entities and an offset mechanism for voluntary registration by non-obligated entities.
**Breakdown:**
- Legal basis → Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act, 2022
- Scheme → CCTS, notified June 2023 (✅ owner 21 citing Economic Survey 2025-26, Ch. 10)
- Compliance leg → obligated entities with GEI targets (ENV-4009)
- Offset leg → approved sectors (ENV-4015)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act, 2022; CCTS notified June 2023; dual compliance + offset design (✅ owner 21). Not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-4009, ENV-4011, ENV-4012, ENV-4013
**Trap:** Saying India's carbon market is purely voluntary with no legal basis. It has a statutory basis under the 2022 amendment.
**Mains hook:** The Indian Carbon Market uses PAT's institutional base with a broader crediting mechanism; its legal basis is what turns it from a pilot into an enforceable price signal.
**Back:** *"Energy Act 2022 gave the law, CCTS June 2023 gave the scheme: two tracks, one market."*

### Flashcard ENV-4009 — CCTS compliance: emission-intensity baseline-and-credit
**Front:** Is India's compliance carbon market a cap-and-trade system?
**Definition (learn):** No. It is an emission-intensity baseline-and-credit system: obligated entities receive a greenhouse-gas emission-intensity (GEI) target in tCO₂e per unit of output. Those who beat their target earn Carbon Credit Certificates; those who miss it must acquire and surrender certificates.
**Breakdown:**
- Obligated entity → GEI target per unit of output
- Beat target → eligible for CCCs
- Miss target → buy and surrender CCCs
- No absolute economy-wide emissions cap
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 21; certificate denomination tCO₂e; exchange trading provided by Scheme and CERC regulations)
**Links:** ENV-4008, ENV-4010, ENV-4014, ENV-4016
**Trap:** "India's compliance market is a cap-and-trade like the EU ETS." It is intensity-based, so absolute emissions can rise with output.
**Mains hook:** An intensity system suits a growing economy and CBDR-RC reasoning, but it is not a net-zero instrument on its own; baseline generosity decides the price signal.
**Back:** *"Intensity, not cap: beat your tonnes-per-unit target and sell the surplus."*

### Flashcard ENV-4010 — Carbon Credit Certificate vs green credit vs ESCert
**Front:** Distinguish a Carbon Credit Certificate (CCC), a green credit and an Energy Saving Certificate (ESCert).
**Definition (learn):** CCC is the tradable CCTS instrument denominated in tCO₂e. A green credit (Green Credit Rules, 2023) rewards specified environmental actions and is not emission-denominated. An ESCert under PAT records the energy-saving attribute of one metric tonne of oil equivalent (toe).
**Breakdown:**
- CCC → tCO₂e, CCTS
- Green credit → specified environmental action; Green Credit Rules, 2023
- ESCert → one toe of energy saving; PAT (BEE)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ESCert denomination is one toe (✅ owner 21, BEE PAT/ESCert material at PDF p. 24). Green Credit Rules 2023 (✅ owner 21).
**Links:** ENV-4008, ENV-4011
**Trap:** Treating a green credit or ESCert as a CCC because all three are "credits" or "certificates".
**Mains hook:** Instrument design determines what is measured; a carbon-denominated unit is needed for a CO₂ mitigation claim.
**Back:** *"CCC counts carbon, ESCert counts toe, green credit counts actions."*

### Flashcard ENV-4011 — PAT scheme: precursor to the carbon market
**Front:** What was PAT and how is it linked to the Indian Carbon Market?
**Definition (learn):** Perform, Achieve and Trade (PAT) is a BEE-administered market-based energy-efficiency scheme in which energy-intensive industries traded Energy Saving Certificates. It is an institutional precursor, not the same instrument as the CCTS.
**Breakdown:**
- PAT → Specific Energy Consumption (SEC) targets and ESCerts
- CCTS → GEI targets and CCCs in tCO₂e
- Shared institutional base: BEE, energy-intensive sectors (cement, steel, thermal power)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PAT regulates Specific Energy Consumption (✅ owner 21, Section 3.2). Examples of sectors (⚠️ owner 21 Section 5).
**Links:** ENV-4008, ENV-4009, ENV-4010
**Trap:** "PAT and the carbon market are unrelated." PAT is the foundation, but ESCerts and CCCs use different metrics and legal procedures.
**Mains hook:** PAT's proven trading experience gives India an institutional head start over a fresh carbon-market design.
**Back:** *"PAT trades energy saved, CCTS trades carbon: same BEE family, different ledger."*

### Flashcard ENV-4012 — Institutional roles in the Indian Carbon Market
**Front:** Who does what in India's Carbon Credit Trading Scheme?
**Definition (learn):** Four bodies share the scheme's functions: Ministry of Power (notified the scheme and chairs the National Steering Committee), BEE (Administrator), MoEFCC (co-chairs the Steering Committee and notifies GEI targets), Grid Controller of India Ltd (Registry), and CERC (regulates CCC trading).
**Breakdown:**
- Ministry of Power → notifies scheme; chairs National Steering Committee
- BEE → Administrator; methodologies, sector and entity identification, verification-agency accreditation
- MoEFCC → co-chairs Steering Committee; notifies entity-level GEI targets; leads Article 6 engagement
- Grid Controller of India Ltd → Registry (accounts, transaction and holding records)
- CERC → regulator for CCC trading
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 21, Section 4)
**Links:** ENV-4008, ENV-4013, ENV-4025
**Trap:** BEE, Grid Controller and CERC are interchangeable; MoEFCC runs the registry or exchange. Each has a separate function.
**Mains hook:** Clear institutional split prevents conflicts between target-setting, registration and trading oversight.
**Back:** *"Power starts it, BEE administers, MoEFCC sets targets, Grid Controller records, CERC trades."*

### Flashcard ENV-4013 — CERC trading regulations, 2026
**Front:** What did CERC notify in 2026 for carbon-credit trading, and what does it not prove?
**Definition (learn):** The Central Electricity Regulatory Commission notified the Terms and Conditions for Purchase and Sale of Carbon Credit Certificates Regulations, 2026. They govern exchange trading of CCCs and related market arrangements.
**Breakdown:**
- Regulator for CCC trading: CERC
- Notified on 27 February 2026; Gazette publication on 4 May 2026 (📰 owner 21, Section 8)
- Adds the exchange-trading rule layer to the 2023 scheme
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 27 February 2026 (notification); 4 May 2026 (Gazette) (✅ owner 21, dated current anchor; not fetched; CERC regulation PDF cited in owner).
**Links:** ENV-4008, ENV-4012, ENV-4014
**Trap:** Treating a notified trading rule as proof of a liquid market, a discovered price, or verified mitigation. Issuance, trade, surrender, price and liquidity data must be checked separately.
**Mains hook:** The sequence (scheme notified → targets notified → trading rules notified → operating market) is a status ladder, and each rung is not proof of the next.
**Back:** *"Rules are not a market: the trading layer exists, the volumes must be shown."*

### Flashcard ENV-4014 — GEI targets notified in 2025 for four sectors
**Front:** Which sectors received notified GEI targets in 2025 under the CCTS?
**Definition (learn):** Pro-rata greenhouse-gas emission-intensity (GEI) targets were notified in 2025 for four sectors: Aluminium, Cement, Chlor-Alkali, and Pulp and Paper.
**Breakdown:**
- Aluminium
- Cement
- Chlor-Alkali
- Pulp and Paper
- GEI target setting: estimate baseline GEI → compare within the same sub-sectoral group → assign targets (ENV-4016)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 notification for four sectors (✅ owner 21, Economic Survey 2025-26, Ch. 10).
**Links:** ENV-4009, ENV-4012, ENV-4016
**Trap:** Mixing up the four notified sectors with the ten offset sectors (ENV-4015). They are different lists.
**Mains hook:** Sector-by-sector notification shows the CCTS is being phased in; coverage is not yet economy-wide.
**Back:** *"ACCP: Aluminium, Cement, Chlor-alkali, Pulp-paper: targets set in 2025."*

### Flashcard ENV-4015 — Offset mechanism: approved sectors
**Front:** Which sectors does India's CCTS offset mechanism cover?
**Definition (learn):** Voluntary, project-based crediting for non-obligated entities across an approved list of ten sectors.
**Breakdown:**
- Energy
- Industries
- Agriculture
- Waste handling and disposal
- Forestry
- Transport
- Fugitive emissions
- Construction
- Solvent use
- Carbon capture, utilisation and storage (CCUS)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ten approved sectors, including CCUS (✅ owner 21, Economic Survey 2025-26, Ch. 10).
**Links:** ENV-4007, ENV-4008, ENV-4026
**Trap:** Thinking the offset mechanism is only for compliance entities. Offsets are for voluntary registration by non-obligated entities.
**Mains hook:** Opening a 10-sector offset window carries the same oversupply risk the EU saw in its early phase (ENV-4026).
**Back:** *"Ten sectors, incl. CCUS and forestry, credit the voluntary way."*

### Flashcard ENV-4016 — GEI target-setting principle: efficiency and softest target
**Front:** How does the BEE methodology assign GEI targets within a sub-sectoral group?
**Definition (learn):** Each obligated entity's baseline GEI is estimated, units in the same sub-sectoral group are compared, and the least demanding target is assigned to the most efficient unit while the most demanding goes to the least efficient.
**Breakdown:**
- Step 1: estimate baseline GEI per entity
- Step 2: compare within the same sub-sectoral group
- Step 3: assign targets (softest to most efficient, toughest to least efficient)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Methodology per BEE (✅ owner 21, Economic Survey 2025-26, Ch. 10; BEE detailed procedure cited in owner).
**Links:** ENV-4009, ENV-4014
**Trap:** Assuming "most demanding target for the most efficient" or an absolute cap. The principle protects competitiveness, but a soft benchmark caps the price signal.
**Mains hook:** Baseline generosity is the whole ballgame: softer benchmarks protect competitiveness and weaken the carbon price.
**Back:** *"Best plant, easiest target; worst plant, hardest target."*

### Flashcard ENV-4017 — EU ETS: the reference system and its phases
**Front:** What are the key facts of the EU Emissions Trading System used as a reference for India?
**Definition (learn):** The EU ETS, launched in 2005, is a cap-and-trade system now in its fourth phase; up to 57% of allowances are auctioned and the rest allocated free to limit carbon leakage.
**Breakdown:**
- Launched 2005
- Phase 4 (current phase)
- Up to 57% auctioned; remainder free allocation
- CBAM gradually replaces free allocation from 2026
- ETS2 (buildings, road transport, small-industry fuel combustion) by 2027
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2005; fourth phase; up to 57%; CBAM from 2026; ETS2 by 2027 (✅ owner 21, Economic Survey 2025-26, Ch. 10, Box X.5). Not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-4018, ENV-4019, ENV-4026
**Trap:** Calling the EU ETS a baseline-and-credit system. It is cap-and-trade, unlike India's CCTS intensity leg.
**Mains hook:** The EU's cap-and-trade plus CBAM shows that a domestic carbon price can become a trade constraint, so India's CCTS also has an industrial-competitiveness logic.
**Back:** *"Two thousand five, fifty-seven percent auctioned, CBAM 2026, ETS2 2027."*

### Flashcard ENV-4018 — Korea and China ETS as reference systems
**Front:** Which other carbon-trading systems does the Economic Survey use as comparison points?
**Definition (learn):** South Korea's Emissions Trading Scheme has operated since 2015; China runs a national ETS. Together with the EU ETS, these are the three reference systems against which India's design is compared.
**Breakdown:**
- EU ETS → 2005, reference
- Korea ETS → operational since 2015
- China national ETS → national scale
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Korea ETS 2015 (✅ owner 21, Economic Survey 2025-26, Box X.5). China national ETS (✅ owner 21; launch year not recorded in owner; not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-4017, ENV-4008
**Trap:** Treating these as identical systems; each has its own allocation, scope and offset design.
**Mains hook:** Comparisons across the EU, Korea and China show trade-off choices in design (allocation method, offsets, scope).
**Back:** *"EU 2005, Korea 2015, China national: the three mirrors."*

### Flashcard ENV-4019 — CBAM: a border adjustment, not an EU tax on all imports
**Front:** What is the Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) and why does it matter to India?
**Definition (learn):** A border adjustment within the EU framework that gradually replaces free allocation for carbon-leakage-exposed sectors from 2026. It applies to specified goods, not to imports generally.
**Breakdown:**
- Replaces EU free allocation (from 2026)
- Specified goods only (steel, aluminium and cement named in owner 21's analysis)
- Creates an export constraint for Indian steel, aluminium and cement
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** From 2026 (✅ owner 21, Economic Survey 2025-26, Box X.5).
**Links:** ENV-4017, ENV-4008, ENV-4009
**Trap:** "CBAM is an EU carbon tax on all imports." It targets specified goods and gradually replaces free allocation.
**Mains hook:** A domestic carbon price recognised against CBAM liability is an industrial-competitiveness measure as much as a climate one; this is a strong case for why India built the CCTS.
**Back:** *"CBAM taxes the carbon in specified goods at the border, not every import."*

### Flashcard ENV-4020 — IPCC-63 and the 2027 CDR and CCUS methodology report
**Front:** What did IPCC-63 agree about CDR and CCUS accounting?
**Definition (learn):** The scientific content of a 2027 Methodology Report on Carbon Dioxide Removal Technologies, Carbon Capture, Utilization and Storage for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories was agreed at IPCC-63 (Lima, Peru, October 2025). It sets the accounting rules for capturing and removing CO₂.
**Breakdown:**
- Venue and date: IPCC-63, Lima, Peru, October 2025
- Output: 2027 Methodology Report (CDR and CCUS in national GHG inventories)
- Effect: removals move from technology debate to accounting debate
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** IPCC-63 Lima October 2025; report year 2027 (✅ owner 21, ipcc.ch, verified 2 August 2026 per owner).
**Links:** ENV-4006, ENV-4021, ENV-4022
**Trap:** Treating an accounting methodology as proof of large-scale CCUS deployment.
**Mains hook:** Whoever writes inventory rules determines whether a captured tonne counts, for how long and against whose ledger.
**Back:** *"Lima 2025 writes the ledger; 2027 reports the rules for CO₂ removal."*

### Flashcard ENV-4021 — Market integrity: over-crediting, non-additionality and double-counting
**Front:** Why does a carbon market's climate value depend on MRV integrity?
**Definition (learn):** Without rigorous Monitoring, Reporting and Verification (MRV), a market can issue paper credits without atmospheric benefit through over-crediting (crediting more than occurred), non-additionality (crediting reductions that would have happened anyway) and double-counting (the same reduction claimed twice).
**Breakdown:**
- Over-crediting → quantity error
- Non-additionality → counterfactual error
- Double-counting → ownership or accounting error
- Remedy → robust MRV, unique issuance, exclusive accounting
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (⚠️ owner 21 Advanced Section 1, analytical)
**Links:** ENV-4002, ENV-4015, ENV-4022
**Trap:** "Existence of a market means emissions have fallen." Existence and integrity are different tests.
**Mains hook:** The concept is simple; the integrity architecture is hard. Conclude with MRV and liability design.
**Back:** *"Over-count, copy-count, or would-have-happened: MRV catches all three."*

### Flashcard ENV-4022 — Removal-claim checklist for CDR
**Front:** What must a credible carbon-removal claim identify?
**Definition (learn):** A removal claim needs the atmospheric source, lifecycle emissions, leakage, net removal, a named storage medium and expected duration, reversal monitoring or liability, and unique issuance and claim.
**Breakdown:**
- Atmospheric uptake
- minus full lifecycle emissions and leakage → net removal
- plus named storage medium and expected duration
- plus reversal monitoring/replacement or liability
- plus unique issuance and claim
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 21, basic Section 6 and advanced Section 5)
**Links:** ENV-4005, ENV-4006, ENV-4021
**Trap:** Booking a removal without naming the storage duration or the reversal control.
**Mains hook:** Durability is a time-horizon claim, not a binary label; a claims discipline is the analytical core of CDR governance.
**Back:** *"Uptake minus lifecycle, plus storage duration, plus reversal cover, plus one claim."*

### Flashcard ENV-4023 — CCUS and enhanced oil recovery: contested net benefit
**Front:** Why is CCUS used for enhanced oil recovery (EOR) not automatically climate-positive?
**Definition (learn):** EOR uses captured CO₂ to recover more oil. If the recovered oil is later combusted, the CO₂ re-enters the atmosphere, so the net climate benefit is contested and needs lifecycle accounting.
**Breakdown:**
- CCUS-EOR → capture, injection, extra oil recovery
- Recovered oil → combusted later → CO₂ released
- Net benefit depends on lifecycle accounting
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 21, Advanced Section 3.4 and Section 8)
**Links:** ENV-4003, ENV-4007, ENV-4022
**Trap:** "CCUS via EOR delivers an unambiguous net climate benefit." It does not if the oil is burned.
**Mains hook:** Storage claims need full lifecycle accounting; utilisation is not durable storage.
**Back:** *"Pump CO₂ to pump oil, burn oil, release CO₂: net benefit contested."*

### Flashcard ENV-4024 — CDR routes: storage media and integrity questions
**Front:** For each CDR route family, what is the main integrity question?
**Definition (learn):** Each route stores carbon in a different reservoir and carries different verification and reversal risks.
**Breakdown:**
- Biological/terrestrial (afforestation, restoration, soil) → saturation, fire, drought, harvest, additionality, land rights
- Biological-product (biochar, harvested wood) → feedstock counterfactual, product decay, permanence period
- BECCS → biomass sustainability, land-use change, capture coverage, storage
- DACCS → low-carbon energy, capture efficiency, transport, storage MRV, liability
- Geochemical (enhanced weathering) → mining, grinding, reaction rate, product fate
- Ocean-based → additionality, monitoring, ecological effects, transboundary governance
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 21, advanced Section 5 table)
**Links:** ENV-4006, ENV-4005, ENV-4022
**Trap:** "All CDR routes are interchangeable and permanent." Their permanence and MRV differ.
**Mains hook:** Diversify CDR for a narrowly defined residual, judged route by route, not one machine.
**Back:** *"Each reservoir leaks in its own way: soil burns, product rots, rock grinds, ocean needs proof."*

### Flashcard ENV-4025 — PYQ route: 2025 GS-III CCUS and Prelims ledger
**Front:** What are the key PYQ routes for carbon markets, CCUS and DAC?
**Definition (learn):** The owner records a 2025 GS-III direct demand, plus Prelims demands from 2021, 2023, 2024 and 2025. The 2025 Prelims key is recorded only as "available locally"; no answer is inferred in the owner.
**Breakdown:**
- 2025 GS-III Q7 (10 marks, 150 words): CCUS and its potential climate role
- 2024 Prelims GS-I Q96: EU Net-Zero Industry Act; EU carbon neutrality by 2040 (key available locally; answer not recorded)
- 2025 Prelims GS-I Q34: Paris Agreement Article 6 (key available locally; answer not recorded)
- 2025 Prelims GS-I Q36: DAC emerging technology (key available locally; answer not recorded)
- 2023 Prelims GS-I Q23: carbon markets as mitigation tools; 2021 Prelims GS-I Q29: UNEP Common Carbon Metric (keys unavailable locally)
- 2023 Prelims GS-I Q68: CCS methods, basalt lime injection (key unavailable locally)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed in owner 21 PYQ integration blocks (2018–2023 and 2024–2025).
**Links:** ENV-4003, ENV-4004, ENV-4008, ENV-4019 (Article 6 / CBAM), ENV-4025
**Trap:** Answering the 2024 or 2025 Prelims items from the card alone. Verify the Set-A key before recording.
**Mains hook:** For the 2025 10-mark question, use: define capture–transport–use/store, role in hard-to-abate sectors, limits (energy/cost and permanence/MRV), then a conclusion that reserves CCUS for residual emissions.
**Back:** *"Capture-source, avoid-or-remove, residual-only: the CCUS 10-mark spine."*

### Flashcard ENV-4026 — EU early-phase offset oversupply: the cautionary lesson for India
**Front:** Why does the Economic Survey cite the EU's early-phase offset experience for India's offset window?
**Definition (learn):** In the EU's early phase, oversupply of offset credits diluted the carbon price. The EU later discontinued offset credits in Phase 4. The Survey draws this as the specific cautionary lesson for India's ten-sector offset mechanism.
**Breakdown:**
- EU Phase 1 → offset oversupply → price dilution
- EU Phase 4 → offsets discontinued
- India → 10-sector offset window opened under CCTS
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** EU offset discontinuation in Phase 4 (✅ owner 21, Economic Survey 2025-26, Box X.5). Not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-4015, ENV-4017, ENV-4021
**Trap:** Presenting offsets as costless carbon supply. Oversupply of low-quality offsets can weaken the compliance price signal.
**Mains hook:** Offset design needs additionality and quantity discipline; otherwise the offset window becomes the leak in the carbon price.
**Back:** *"EU offsets flooded the market and sank the price; India must cap the leak."*

### Flashcard ENV-4027 — Indian CCUS pilots and voluntary credits (⚠️)
**Front:** What is the status of CCUS pilots and voluntary credits from India?
**Definition (learn):** CCUS pilots in India, including in cement and other industrial sectors, are at an early stage. Afforestation and renewable-energy projects have historically been common sources of Indian voluntary credits.
**Breakdown:**
- CCUS → early-stage pilots (cement and other industries)
- Voluntary credits → afforestation and renewable-energy projects historically common
- PAT → feasibility precedent for trading among energy-intensive industries
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** ⚠️ owner 21 Section 5 (analytical; no specific deployment figure recorded in owner; do not cite numbers without a dated source).
**Links:** ENV-4003, ENV-4011, ENV-4015
**Trap:** Citing a specific Indian CCUS capacity figure without a source and date.
**Mains hook:** Early-stage pilots show CCUS is a complement for hard-to-abate sectors, not a present-day mitigation fix at scale.
**Back:** *"Pilots are early, credits are historically forest and renewables."*

### GROUP — Multilateral Environmental Conventions CBD Basel Stockholm Montreal

### Flashcard ENV-4028 — Four conventions, four different problems and mechanisms
**Front:** Match the CBD, Basel, Stockholm and Montreal conventions to their problem and mechanism.
**Definition (learn):** Each convention addresses a distinct environmental problem with a different mechanism: CBD (biodiversity; target-setting and benefit-sharing), Basel (hazardous-waste movement; prior informed consent and ESM), Stockholm (persistent organic pollutants; listing and annex controls), Montreal (ozone-depleting substances; phase-out schedules).
**Breakdown:**
- CBD (1992) → biodiversity: conservation, sustainable use, benefit-sharing
- Basel (1989) → hazardous waste trade and disposal control
- Stockholm (2001) → POPs elimination or restriction
- Montreal (1987) → ODS phase-out; widely regarded as the most successful MEA
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 22; years confirmed: CBD 1992 (Wikipedia, opened for signature 5 June 1992), Basel 1989 (Wikipedia, signed March 1989 at Basel), Stockholm 2001 (Wikipedia, adopted 22 May 2001 at Stockholm), Montreal 1987 (Wikipedia, signed 16 September 1987 at Montreal))
**Links:** ENV-4029, ENV-4035, ENV-4040, ENV-4044
**Trap:** Matching the Basel Convention to ozone or the Montreal Protocol to waste. The four treaties have non-overlapping subject matter.
**Mains hook:** Convention performance depends on how well the mechanism fits the problem; a treaty's success cannot be copied mechanically to another problem.
**Back:** *"Biodiversity by targets, waste by consent, POPs by listing, ozone by phase-out."*

### Flashcard ENV-4029 — CBD (1992): objectives, venue and entry into force
**Front:** What are the three objectives of the Convention on Biological Diversity and when was it opened?
**Definition (learn):** UN treaty born from the Rio Earth Summit with three objectives: conservation of biological diversity, sustainable use of its components, and fair and equitable sharing of benefits arising from genetic resources.
**Breakdown:**
- Objective 1 → conservation of biological diversity
- Objective 2 → sustainable use of components
- Objective 3 → fair and equitable benefit-sharing from genetic resources
- Secretariat: Montreal, Canada (✅ owner 22)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Opened for signature 5 June 1992 at Rio de Janeiro; in force 29 December 1993 (✅ Wikipedia). Signing remained open until 4 June 1993 (✅ Wikipedia). Parties: 196 at time of fetch; as-of date not given, so ⚠️ do not quote as current.
**Links:** ENV-4028, ENV-4030, ENV-4031, ENV-4032
**Trap:** Saying the CBD covers conservation alone. Sustainable use and benefit-sharing are separate objectives.
**Mains hook:** The CBD's three objectives explain why biodiversity governance must include ABS and sustainable use, not only protected areas.
**Back:** *"Conserve, use sustainably, share fairly: three legs of the CBD."*

### Flashcard ENV-4030 — CBD protocols: Cartagena (biosafety) and Nagoya (ABS)
**Front:** What are the two CBD protocols and what do they govern?
**Definition (learn):** Cartagena Protocol on Biosafety (2000) governs safe transfer, handling and use of living modified organisms (LMOs) through advance informed agreement and the precautionary approach. Nagoya Protocol (2010) governs access to genetic resources and fair and equitable benefit-sharing (ABS). The Nagoya–Kuala Lumpur Supplementary Protocol (2010) covers liability and redress for Cartagena.
**Breakdown:**
- Cartagena (2000) → LMOs; advance informed agreement; precautionary approach
- Nagoya-Kuala Lumpur Supplementary Protocol (2010) → liability and redress
- Nagoya (2010) → ABS; India's Biological Diversity Act architecture implements it domestically (✅ owner 22)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Cartagena adopted 29 January 2000, in force 11 September 2003; Nagoya adopted 29 October 2010, in force 12 October 2014 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ENV-4029, ENV-4034
**Trap:** Treating Cartagena and Nagoya as protocols of different conventions. Both are CBD protocols; Basel, Stockholm and Montreal have their own separate frameworks.
**Mains hook:** Cartagena manages risk from LMOs; Nagoya manages the bargain over genetic resources. Name both to avoid conflating biosafety with benefit-sharing.
**Back:** *"Cartagena guards living modified organisms; Nagoya shares the benefits of genetic resources."*

### Flashcard ENV-4031 — KMGBF: Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework
**Front:** What is the Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework and what are its goals and headline target?
**Definition (learn):** The CBD's current global strategic framework, adopted at CBD COP15 in Montreal on 19 December 2022. It aims to halt and reverse biodiversity loss by 2030 and live in harmony with nature by 2050, through four 2050 goals and 23 targets for 2030.
**Breakdown:**
- Goal A → ecosystem integrity and species extinction halted
- Goal B → sustainable use and nature's contributions to people
- Goal C → fair and equitable benefit-sharing from genetic resources, DSI and traditional knowledge
- Goal D → means of implementation (finance, capacity, technology, science)
- Target 3 ("30x30") → effectively conserve or manage 30% of terrestrial, inland-water, coastal and marine areas by 2030
- Wikipedia notes the Goal D finance gap at USD 700 billion per year (as-of not stated)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Adopted 19 December 2022 at COP15, Montreal (✅ Wikipedia). Four goals and 23 targets (✅ owner 22 Section 14). Goal labels shown as numbers 1–4 by Wikipedia and as A–D in the owner; both are used.
**Links:** ENV-4029, ENV-4032, ENV-4033, ENV-4034
**Trap:** "KMGBF has a single target, 30x30." 30x30 is only Target 3 of 23 targets under four goals.
**Mains hook:** Name the four goals, then the targets grouped by theme, then India's NBSAP and NR-7 as domestic evidence.
**Back:** *"Four goals, twenty-three targets, thirty percent by 2030, harmony by 2050."*

### Flashcard ENV-4032 — KMGBF targets 18 and 19: finance and harmful incentives
**Front:** What do KMGBF Targets 18 and 19 require?
**Definition (learn):** Target 18 requires eliminating, phasing out or reforming incentives harmful to biodiversity, reducing them by at least USD 500 billion per year. Target 19 requires mobilising at least USD 200 billion per year from all sources for biodiversity, including USD 30 billion per year in international finance to developing countries.
**Breakdown:**
- Target 18 → harmful incentives reform, at least USD 500 billion per year
- Target 19 → resource mobilisation, at least USD 200 billion per year
- Target 19 sub-limit → USD 30 billion per year international finance to developing countries
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Target 18 and 19 amounts as in owner 22, Section 14 (✅ owner 22; the Wikipedia fetch did not return these amounts, so they remain owner-only). Verify against the CBD decision text before quoting in a formal answer.
**Links:** ENV-4031, ENV-4033, ENV-4034
**Trap:** Quoting Target 19 as a government-only figure. It is an all-source mobilisation target.
**Mains hook:** Finance and incentive reform are the implementation levers of Goal D; both must be quantified in a 10-mark answer.
**Back:** *"Eighteen cuts five-hundred-billion harm; nineteen raises two-hundred-billion good."*

### Flashcard ENV-4033 — India's KMGBF response: NBSAP 2024–2030 and NR-7
**Front:** What are India's main KMGBF-aligned instruments and national indicators?
**Definition (learn):** India's NBSAP 2024–2030 is its domestic national biodiversity strategy and action plan aligned with the KMGBF. The Seventh National Report (NR-7) to the CBD uses 142 national indicators mapped against 23 National Biodiversity Targets.
**Breakdown:**
- NBSAP 2024–2030 → domestic strategy aligned to KMGBF
- NR-7 → 142 national indicators
- 23 National Biodiversity Targets → India's translation of the 23 global targets
- Outcome examples: forest and tree cover 25.17% of geographical area; Ramsar sites 26 → 98; 58 Tiger Reserves; 33 Elephant Reserves; 106 National Parks; 574 Wildlife Sanctuaries
- Species: tigers 3,682; elephants 22,446; rhinos 4,014; Asiatic lions 891; river dolphins 6,327; 22 agrobiodiversity hotspots (✅ owner 22, NR-7 as reported in owner)
- Tools: PARIVESH single-window portal; Mission LiFE
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed (✅ owner 22; NR-7 figures not independently fetched; as-of date for the figures not recorded in the owner. Cite with source and date.)
**Links:** ENV-4031, ENV-4032, ENV-4034
**Trap:** "NBSAP and the KMGBF are the same document." NBSAP is India's domestic plan; KMGBF is the global framework it implements.
**Mains hook:** The 2026 GS-III Q8 asks exactly this: aim and goals, then India's commitments with examples. Use NBSAP and NR-7 as the Indian evidence, then Mission LiFE and PARIVESH as tools.
**Back:** *"NBSAP is India's own plan; NR-7 is the report card: 142 indicators, 23 targets."*

### Flashcard ENV-4034 — CBD COP16 (Cali 2024; resumed Rome 2025), Cali Fund and DSI
**Front:** What did CBD COP16 decide on digital sequence information (DSI) and the Cali Fund?
**Definition (learn):** COP16 in Cali (2024) and its resumed session in Rome (February 2025) completed decisions on resource mobilisation and KMGBF monitoring, operationalised the DSI benefit-sharing mechanism, and launched the Cali Fund.
**Breakdown:**
- Venue and dates: Cali 2024; resumed Rome February 2025
- DSI benefit-sharing mechanism operationalised
- Cali Fund launched
- Article 8(j) permanent subsidiary body created (✅ owner 22, advanced Section 10)
- India's domestic counterpart: Biological Diversity (Access to Biological Resources… and Fair and Equitable Sharing of Benefits) Regulations, 2025 (✅ owner 22, as cited to Economic Survey 2025-26, Ch. 10)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Cali 2024; Rome February 2025 (✅ owner 22 and CBD outcome page cited in owner; not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-4030, ENV-4031, ENV-4033
**Trap:** Treating the fund's establishment as completed distribution to communities. Mechanism adopted is not the same as benefit delivered.
**Mains hook:** DSI tests the Nagoya bargain: genetic information can be used without moving a physical sample, bypassing a physical-access model. India's 2025 Regulations are the response.
**Back:** *"Cali decides the DSI fund: information moves; the benefits must follow."*

### Flashcard ENV-4035 — Basel Convention (1989): PIC chain and ESM
**Front:** What is the control chain of the Basel Convention for hazardous-waste movements?
**Definition (learn):** The Basel Convention (1989) controls the transboundary movement and disposal or recovery of hazardous and other covered wastes through a chain of classification, advance notification, written import consent (and transit response where applicable), movement documents and environmentally sound management (ESM).
**Breakdown:**
- Classification of the waste
- Advance notification of a proposed movement
- Written consent from the importing State (prior informed consent)
- Movement documents
- Environmentally sound management (ESM)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Basel 1989, signed in Basel, Switzerland, March 1989 (✅ Wikipedia; the infobox gives 22 March 1989 and the text 21 March, a conflict flagged ⚠️). In force 5 May 1992 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ENV-4036, ENV-4037, ENV-4038, ENV-4028
**Trap:** "PIC makes every hazardous-waste shipment legal" or "Basel bans all hazardous-waste trade." Consent does not legalise dumping, and the Convention does not ban every shipment.
**Mains hook:** Basel regulates individual movements; its strength depends on consent and ESM enforcement, which is why e-waste and ship-breaking remain flashpoints.
**Back:** *"Classify, notify, consent, document, dispose soundly: consent is not a licence."*

### Flashcard ENV-4036 — Basel Ban Amendment (1995): Annex VII to non-Annex VII
**Front:** What does the Basel Ban Amendment prohibit and when did it enter into force?
**Definition (learn):** An amendment adopted in 1995 that bars an Annex VII Party bound by it from exporting specified hazardous wastes to a non-Annex VII State: Convention-covered wastes for Annex IV A final disposal, and Article 1(1)(a) hazardous wastes for Annex IV B recovery and recycling.
**Breakdown:**
- Operative test → Annex VII → non-Annex VII (not OECD vs non-OECD)
- Annex VII → OECD members, the EU and Liechtenstein
- Limb 1 → Annex IV A final disposal
- Limb 2 → Annex IV B recovery or recycling of Article 1(1)(a) wastes
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** In force 5 December 2019 for accepting Parties (✅ owner 22; ✅ Wikipedia consistent in the 90-days-after-ratification passage). Wikipedia's wording on whether it has entered into force is internally inconsistent; the owner's date is used ⚠️ conflict flagged. India had not accepted the Ban Amendment as of 5 October 2026 (✅ owner 22).
**Links:** ENV-4035, ENV-4037
**Trap:** "OECD to non-OECD is the Basel Ban test." The treaty test is Annex VII to non-Annex VII.
**Mains hook:** The Ban is a narrower prohibitory layer. Its slow ratification shows that a sound amendment still needs aligned political and economic incentives.
**Back:** *"Annex VII sends nothing to non-Annex VII under the Ban, once accepted."*

### Flashcard ENV-4037 — Basel plastic-waste and e-waste annex amendments and India's status
**Front:** When did the Basel plastic-waste and e-waste amendments take effect, and is India bound?
**Definition (learn):** Plastic-waste amendments became effective on 1 January 2021 and e-waste amendments on 1 January 2025, for Parties bound by them. As of 5 October 2026, India is bound by the plastic and e-waste amendments but has not accepted the Ban Amendment.
**Breakdown:**
- Plastic-waste amendments → effective 1 January 2021
- E-waste amendments → effective 1 January 2025
- India → bound by plastic and e-waste amendments; Ban Amendment not accepted
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1 January 2021; 1 January 2025; status as of 5 October 2026 (✅ owner 22). Wikipedia's plastic-amendment page gives no entry-into-force date and says "186 States" bound (time-varying; ⚠️ not quoted).
**Links:** ENV-4035, ENV-4036, ENV-4015 (e-waste context in owner 15)
**Trap:** Saying India is not bound by any Basel amendment. India is bound by the plastic and e-waste amendments.
**Mains hook:** Domestic import restrictions can be stricter than the treaty minimum; non-party status is not permission to import hazardous waste.
**Back:** *"Plastic 2021, e-waste 2025: India bound, Ban not accepted."*

### Flashcard ENV-4038 — Rotterdam Convention (1998): Prior Informed Consent for chemicals
**Front:** How does the Rotterdam Convention's PIC procedure work for chemicals in trade?
**Definition (learn):** The Rotterdam Convention (1998) applies prior informed consent to chemicals and pesticides listed in Annex III. The importing Party issues a general import response (consent, no consent, or consent subject to conditions), which the Secretariat circulates. Exporting Parties must ensure their exporters comply.
**Breakdown:**
- Annex III chemical → importer's general import response
- Circulated by Secretariat
- Chemical Review Committee (CRC) reviews notifications and recommends listing
- COP decides Annex III listing
- Secretariat jointly provided by FAO (Rome) and UNEP (Geneva)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Adopted 1998 (✅ owner 22). Other dates not fetched.
**Links:** ENV-4035, ENV-4039, ENV-4040
**Trap:** Thinking Rotterdam PIC means a fresh signed consent for each shipment (that is Basel). Rotterdam records a general import response for a listed chemical. Also, Rotterdam does not regulate waste.
**Mains hook:** Rotterdam enables informed trade decisions for dangerous chemicals; it does not impose production bans.
**Back:** *"Rotterdam: the importer's general answer, listed in Annex III, not a waste shipment permit."*

### Flashcard ENV-4039 — BRS cluster and the 2025 COP outcomes
**Front:** What are the BRS conventions and what did their 2025 COPs decide?
**Definition (learn):** Basel, Rotterdam and Stockholm form the coordinated BRS chemicals-and-waste cluster with back-to-back COPs. Coordination shares services but does not merge the separate legal mandates.
**Breakdown:**
- Basel COP17 → adopted the 2025–2031 Strategic Framework
- Rotterdam COP12 → added carbosulfan and a specified fenthion formulation to Annex III
- Stockholm COP12 → adopted three new Annex A listings and a UV-328 exemption amendment (advanced owner 22)
- Each decision follows its own entry-into-force and Party-status rule
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2025 COPs (✅ owner 22; decision texts cited in owner, not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-4035, ENV-4038, ENV-4040, ENV-4042
**Trap:** Assuming a common meeting week creates a single legal effect across the three treaties.
**Mains hook:** Status discipline: adopted, depositary communicated, entered into force and implemented are four different stages.
**Back:** *"BRS meet together, decide apart: each treaty keeps its own rule."*

### Flashcard ENV-4040 — Stockholm Convention (2001): POPs and the dirty dozen
**Front:** When and where was the Stockholm Convention adopted, and what did it first list?
**Definition (learn):** The Stockholm Convention on Persistent Organic Pollutants was adopted at a Conference of the Plenipotentiaries in Stockholm, Sweden, on 22–23 May 2001. It entered into force on 17 May 2004 and first listed twelve chemicals, the "dirty dozen."
**Breakdown:**
- Adopted 22–23 May 2001, Stockholm
- In force 17 May 2004 (ratification by 50 states plus 90 days)
- Twelve initial POPs, sorted into Annexes A, B and C
- Hexachlorobenzene and PCBs appear in both A and C
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 22 May 2001; 17 May 2004; 128 initial parties and 151 signatories at entry into force (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ENV-4041, ENV-4042, ENV-4043, ENV-4028
**Trap:** Stockholm and Basel addressing the same problem. Stockholm targets specific POPs; Basel targets hazardous-waste movement.
**Mains hook:** POPs are persistent and bioaccumulative; the listing-based approach is chemical-specific and reactive.
**Back:** *"Stockholm, twenty-oh-one, twelve dirty chemicals, in force twenty-oh-four."*

### Flashcard ENV-4041 — Stockholm annexes A, B and C with examples
**Front:** What do Stockholm Annexes A, B and C require, with examples?
**Definition (learn):** Annex A requires elimination of production and use, subject to listed specific exemptions. Annex B restricts production and use to acceptable purposes or exemptions. Annex C requires reduction and, where feasible, ultimate elimination of unintentional releases.
**Breakdown:**
- Annex A (elimination) → aldrin, chlordane, dieldrin, endrin, heptachlor, mirex, toxaphene, PCBs; endosulfan later with specific exemptions
- Annex B (restriction) → DDT (disease-vector control only); PFOS (restricted acceptable uses such as fire-fighting foam)
- Annex C (unintentional) → PCDD/PCDF (dioxins and furans); HCB and PCBs also in Annex A
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ Wikipedia; examples are standard listings, consistent with owner 22's examples)
**Links:** ENV-4040, ENV-4042, ENV-4043
**Trap:** "DDT is banned outright." It sits in Annex B, restriction with conditions for vector control.
**Mains hook:** Elimination, restriction and release reduction are different obligations; name the annex, not just the chemical.
**Back:** *"A eliminates, B restricts, C curbs the accidental: DDT is B, not outright ban."*

### Flashcard ENV-4042 — POPs listing process and India's Article 25(4) opt-in
**Front:** How is a chemical listed under Stockholm, and what does India's Article 25(4) declaration mean?
**Definition (learn):** A Party proposes a chemical; the POPs Review Committee (POPRC) screens it against Annex D criteria, prepares an Annex E risk profile and, if warranted, an Annex F risk-management evaluation, and recommends. The COP decides whether to amend Annex A, B or C. India made the Article 25(4) declaration, so each later annex amendment binds India only after India deposits its acceptance.
**Breakdown:**
- Annex D → screening criteria
- Annex E → risk profile
- Annex F → risk-management evaluation
- POPRC → recommends; does not amend
- COP → adopts listing under A, B or C
- India → opt-in per amendment (Article 25(4))
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 22, Stockholm POPRC and annex-status pages cited in owner)
**Links:** ENV-4041, ENV-4043, ENV-4039
**Trap:** "POPRC bans a chemical once it finds persistence or toxicity." It only recommends; the COP decides.
**Mains hook:** Scientific concern alone creates no operative listing; a COP decision and a Party-specific acceptance are needed.
**Back:** *"Screen D, profile E, evaluate F, POPRC recommends, COP lists, India accepts each."*

### Flashcard ENV-4043 — Regrettable substitution: a limit of chemical-by-chemical listing
**Front:** What is "regrettable substitution" and why does it challenge Stockholm-style listing?
**Definition (learn):** When a specific chemical is banned or restricted, industry may substitute a structurally similar, unassessed chemical that may share its hazards. Chemical-by-chemical listing can miss this pattern.
**Breakdown:**
- Ban on chemical X → substitute Y (not yet listed)
- Y may share hazardous properties
- Remedy → class-based regulatory design that anticipates substitution
- Montreal analogy → HFCs replaced CFCs and later became climate concern (Kigali)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (⚠️ owner 22, Advanced Section 4, analytical; no specific chemical pair given)
**Links:** ENV-4041, ENV-4042, ENV-4051
**Trap:** Presenting substitution as evidence that Stockholm failed. It is a structural limitation of listing design.
**Mains hook:** Effective chemical regulation should anticipate substitution patterns, as Kigali eventually did for HFCs.
**Back:** *"Ban one, the cousin steps in: regulate the class, not just the name."*

### Flashcard ENV-4044 — Vienna Convention (1985) and Montreal Protocol (1987)
**Front:** How are the Vienna Convention and Montreal Protocol related and when did the Montreal Protocol take effect?
**Definition (learn):** The Vienna Convention (1985) is the framework on ozone-layer science and cooperation. The Montreal Protocol (1987) is the operative protocol that controls the production and consumption of treaty-defined ozone-depleting substances (ODS).
**Breakdown:**
- Vienna (1985) → framework treaty
- Montreal (1987) → protocol under Vienna; operative controls
- Signed 16 September 1987 in Montreal; in force 1 January 1989 (✅ Wikipedia)
- Ozone Secretariat (UNEP) administers both (✅ owner 22)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Vienna 1985 (✅ owner 22; Wikipedia: 20 nations signed in 1985; venue Vienna is ⚠️ memory-only); Montreal signed 16 September 1987, in force 1 January 1989 (✅ Wikipedia).
**Links:** ENV-4028, ENV-4045, ENV-4046
**Trap:** Treating Montreal as a standalone treaty. It is a protocol under Vienna.
**Mains hook:** The framework-protocol structure let later amendments (London, Kigali) extend the same architecture.
**Back:** *"Vienna frames, Montreal controls: signed Montreal in eighty-seven, live in eighty-nine."*

### Flashcard ENV-4045 — Montreal Protocol controlled-substance annexes
**Front:** Which substances are in each Montreal Protocol annex?
**Definition (learn):** Annexes define controlled substances with separate control articles and timetables. The annexes are legal categories, not identical chemical or schedule groups.
**Breakdown:**
- Annex A → CFCs (Group I) and halons (Group II)
- Annex B → other fully halogenated CFCs, carbon tetrachloride, methyl chloroform (1,1,1-trichloroethane)
- Annex C → Group I HCFCs; Group II HBFCs; Group III bromochloromethane
- Annex E → methyl bromide
- Annex F → Group I HFCs; Group II HFC-23
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 22, Section 6)
**Links:** ENV-4044, ENV-4046, ENV-4049
**Trap:** Collapsing all Annex C groups into one HCFC schedule. Groups I–III each have separate control articles and timetables.
**Mains hook:** "Controlled substance" is an annex-defined category; the chemical property and timetable must be checked per group.
**Back:** *"A CFC-halon, B other CFC, C HCFC-HBFC-BCM, E methyl bromide, F HFC."*

### Flashcard ENV-4046 — London Amendment (1990): Annex B and the Multilateral Fund
**Front:** What did the London Amendment add and when was it adopted and in force?
**Definition (learn):** Adopted on 29 June 1990 and in force from 10 August 1992. It added Annex B controls (other fully halogenated CFCs, carbon tetrachloride, methyl chloroform) and established the Article 10 financial mechanism, which includes the Multilateral Fund.
**Breakdown:**
- Annex B substances added
- Article 10 financial mechanism
- Multilateral Fund → meets agreed incremental costs of eligible Article 5 Parties (conversion, technical assistance, training, institutions)
- Multilateral Fund agencies: UNEP, UNDP, UNIDO and the World Bank (✅ Wikipedia)
- Executive Committee: seven developed and seven Article 5 members (✅ Wikipedia)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Adopted 29 June 1990; in force 10 August 1992 (✅ owner 22). Multilateral Fund agencies and committee composition (✅ Wikipedia). Article 5 country counts are time-varying, so omitted.
**Links:** ENV-4044, ENV-4045, ENV-4047
**Trap:** London and Kigali being equivalent HFC amendments. London expanded ODS controls and finance; Kigali added the Annex F HFC phase-down.
**Mains hook:** The London finance mechanism is a key part of Montreal's success: developing countries get support for compliance.
**Back:** *"London in ninety: Annex B and the fund that pays the incremental cost."*

### Flashcard ENV-4047 — Multilateral Fund: Article 5 parties and incremental costs
**Front:** What is the Multilateral Fund and who does it serve?
**Definition (learn):** The Multilateral Fund for the Implementation of the Montreal Protocol supports developing-country Parties (Article 5 Parties) in meeting their control measures, by funding agreed incremental costs through conversion projects, technical assistance, training and institutional strengthening.
**Breakdown:**
- Serves Article 5 (developing-country) Parties
- Reflects the principle of common but differentiated responsibilities (1992)
- Four implementing agencies: UNEP, UNDP, UNIDO, World Bank
- Executive Committee: seven developed and seven Article 5 members
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Part of the London Amendment's Article 10 (✅ owner 22; Wikipedia for agencies and committee).
**Links:** ENV-4046, ENV-4048, ENV-4050
**Trap:** Thinking the fund is paid to all Parties. It serves eligible Article 5 Parties for agreed incremental costs.
**Mains hook:** A finance mechanism matched to a bounded schedule is one of the reasons Montreal outperformed CBD and Stockholm.
**Back:** *"Developing countries get the incremental cost, through four agencies."*

### Flashcard ENV-4048 — Montreal amendment timeline: London to Kigali
**Front:** What is the Montreal Protocol amendment sequence and where was each agreed?
**Definition (learn):** The Protocol has been amended and adjusted repeatedly. The main amendments are London (1990), Copenhagen (1992), Montreal (1997), Beijing (1999) and Kigali (2016, in force 2019).
**Breakdown:**
- London 1990 → Annex B, financial mechanism and Multilateral Fund
- Copenhagen 1992 → further ODS tightening
- Montreal 1997 → adjustment (Montreal venue, 1997)
- Beijing 1999 → adjustment
- Kigali → agreed October 2016 at the 28th Meeting of the Parties; in force 1 January 2019
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** London adopted 29 June 1990, in force 10 August 1992 (✅ owner 22); Copenhagen 1992, Montreal 1997, Beijing 1999, Kigali agreed October 2016 and in force 1 January 2019 (✅ Wikipedia). Years only for most of the rest.
**Links:** ENV-4046, ENV-4049, ENV-4047
**Trap:** Treating "Kigali" as an ODS phase-out amendment. It is an HFC phase-down added under the Montreal framework.
**Mains hook:** Successive amendments show adaptive design: the same treaty architecture absorbed new substances and finance rules.
**Back:** *"London ninety, Copenhagen ninety-two, Montreal ninety-seven, Beijing ninety-nine, Kigali sixteen."*

### Flashcard ENV-4049 — Kigali Amendment (2016): HFCs and phase-down
**Front:** What does the Kigali Amendment control and how?
**Definition (learn):** The Kigali Amendment added Annex F Group I HFCs and Group II HFC-23 and set differentiated phase-down schedules for production and consumption, expressed in CO₂ equivalents. Article 2J separately addresses HFC-23 by-product emissions.
**Breakdown:**
- Annex F Group I → HFCs (potent greenhouse gases)
- Annex F Group II → HFC-23 (by-product)
- Phase-down, not phase-out
- Developing countries → differentiated schedules; India in the later of two developing-country groups (owner 22)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Adopted October 2016, in force 1 January 2019 (✅ Wikipedia). India's exact baseline and step-down years are ⚠️ not verified; check the Ozone Secretariat or MoEFCC Ozone Cell before quoting.
**Links:** ENV-4048, ENV-4045, ENV-4043, ENV-4050
**Trap:** "HFCs are ozone-depleting, so Kigali regulates them for ozone." HFCs are not ODS; they are regulated for their climate impact.
**Mains hook:** Kigali is a model of using a working ozone treaty to address a climate problem; it avoids negotiating a wholly new instrument.
**Back:** *"HFCs are not ozone-killers, they are heat-trappers: Kigali phases them down."*

### Flashcard ENV-4050 — Montreal's success factors, disaggregated
**Front:** What design features explain Montreal's relative success?
**Definition (learn):** Montreal's success is attributed to a combination of design features rather than political will alone: binding, time-bound control schedules; a dedicated Multilateral Fund for developing-country compliance; a technically bounded set of substances and universal ratification of the Protocol; and built-in adjustment and amendment flexibility.
**Breakdown:**
- Binding, time-bound ODS schedules (baselines, freezes, reductions, phase-outs)
- Multilateral Fund (London Article 10) supports incremental costs
- Bounded problem: identifiable chemicals, feasible substitutes
- Adjustment and amendment flexibility (London, Kigali)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (⚠️ analytical synthesis in owner 22, Advanced Section 2)
**Links:** ENV-4047, ENV-4048, ENV-4051
**Trap:** "Montreal succeeded solely because of global political will." Its finance, binding timelines and bounded scope are the design factors.
**Mains hook:** Use Montreal's disaggregated success factors as the benchmark for judging other MEAs.
**Back:** *"Binding dates, a fund, a bounded list, and room to amend: Montreal's four pillars."*

### Flashcard ENV-4051 — Bounded versus diffuse problems: why treaties differ in effectiveness
**Front:** Why are narrowly bounded problems easier to bind through treaties than diffuse ones?
**Definition (learn):** A narrow, technically defined problem (ozone-depleting substances) can be handled with a list, schedule and finance. Diffuse, cross-sectoral problems (biodiversity loss, hazardous-waste streams) need complementary domestic and sectoral policy beyond any single treaty.
**Breakdown:**
- Bounded example → Montreal (ODS list)
- Diffuse example → CBD (biodiversity), Basel (vast waste streams)
- Consent-based (Basel) vs outright prohibition (Ban Amendment) tension
- Chemical-by-chemical listing (Stockholm) vs anticipating substitution (ENV-4043)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (⚠️ analytical, owner 22 Advanced Section 7)
**Links:** ENV-4043, ENV-4050, ENV-4036
**Trap:** Treating a bounded treaty's success as a template for diffuse problems without adapting design.
**Mains hook:** Match the mechanism to the problem: target-setting for CBD, PIC for Basel, listing for Stockholm, phase-out scheduling for Montreal.
**Back:** *"Bounded problems bind; diffuse problems need domestic policy too."*

### Flashcard ENV-4052 — Stockholm listing versus Rotterdam information: contrasting mechanisms
**Front:** How do Stockholm and Rotterdam differ in what they do once a chemical is listed?
**Definition (learn):** Stockholm creates POP-control obligations (elimination, restriction, release reduction) on listing. Rotterdam primarily enables informed trade decisions through Annex III import responses.
**Breakdown:**
- Stockholm → binding POP-control obligations under annexes A, B, C
- Rotterdam → Annex III import response circulated; trade information and decisions
- Both use scientific review bodies, but POPRC proceeds from Annex D/E/F and CRC from notifications
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 22, Advanced Section 4)
**Links:** ENV-4038, ENV-4042, ENV-4039
**Trap:** Describing Rotterdam as a production ban. It is a trade-information procedure.
**Mains hook:** Use the difference to show that different chemical problems need different instruments: control versus informed consent.
**Back:** *"Stockholm bans or limits, Rotterdam asks the importer before trade."*

### Flashcard ENV-4053 — PYQ routes for the conventions cluster
**Front:** What are the PYQ routes for the multilateral conventions topic?
**Definition (learn):** The owner records a 2026 GS-III Mains question on KMGBF, a 2018 GS-III 15-mark question on Indian biodiversity and the BDA 2002, and 2023 Prelims items on HFCs, mercury, and Nagoya/Biodiversity Management Committees. Prelims keys are not held locally for the 2023 items, so no answers are inferred.
**Breakdown:**
- 2026 GS-III Q8 (10 marks, 150 words): KMGBF aim, goals and India's commitments with examples
- 2018 GS-III Q17 (15 marks, 250 words): biodiversity variation in India and Biological Diversity Act 2002
- 2023 Prelims GS-I Q20: HFC uses (aerosols, foams, fire retardants, lubricants); key unavailable locally
- 2023 Prelims GS-I Q59: mercury pollution sources; key unavailable locally
- 2023 Prelims GS-I Q79: Biodiversity Management Committees and Nagoya ABS; key unavailable locally
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 22 PYQ integration blocks)
**Links:** ENV-4031, ENV-4033, ENV-4049, ENV-4030, ENV-4054
**Trap:** Answering the 2023 Prelims items from memory when the official key is not held locally; state the statement-level distinction instead.
**Mains hook:** Use the KMGBF framework for the 2026 question and the BDA 2002 plus NBA for the 2018 question.
**Back:** *"Twenty-twenty-six asks KMGBF; twenty-eighteen asks Biodiversity Act; twenty-twenty-three asks HFCs and Nagoya."*

### Flashcard ENV-4054 — 10-mark KMGBF answer spine (2026 GS-III Q8)
**Front:** How should a 10-mark answer on the KMGBF be structured in about 150 words?
**Definition (learn):** Aim, then the four goals, then the 23 targets (headlined by Target 3 and Targets 18–19), then India's commitments with named outcomes and tools.
**Breakdown:**
- Aim → global roadmap to halt and reverse biodiversity loss by 2030 and live in harmony with nature by 2050 (1 line)
- Goals → A ecosystem integrity; B sustainable use; C fair benefit-sharing; D means of implementation (2 lines)
- Targets → 23 action targets for 2030; Target 3 (30x30), Targets 18–19 (harmful incentives; USD 200 billion mobilisation) (2–3 lines)
- India → NBSAP 2024–2030; NR-7 with 142 indicators mapped to 23 National Biodiversity Targets; 98 Ramsar sites; 58 Tiger Reserves; 3,682 tigers; 25.17% forest and tree cover; PARIVESH and Mission LiFE (4–5 lines)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 22, Section 14)
**Links:** ENV-4031, ENV-4032, ENV-4033, ENV-4053
**Trap:** Listing only 30x30, or treating NBSAP as the KMGBF itself.
**Mains hook:** Conclude that global targets only matter when translated into national budgets, indicators and enforcement.
**Back:** *"Aim, four goals, twenty-three targets, India's plan and proof."*

### GROUP — Desertification UNCCD and Land Degradation

### Flashcard ENV-4055 — Desertification vs land degradation vs drought
**Front:** Distinguish desertification, land degradation (treaty and wider usage) and drought.
**Definition (learn):** Under UNCCD Article 1, land degradation is the reduction or loss of productivity and complexity in arid, semi-arid and dry sub-humid areas. Desertification is land degradation specifically in drylands, resulting from various factors including climatic variations and human activities. Drought is a naturally occurring, significant precipitation deficit that can last for years but is not automatically desertification.
**Breakdown:**
- Land degradation (treaty) → drylands productivity and complexity loss (erosion, soil deterioration, long-term vegetation loss)
- Land degradation (wider usage) → declining land condition in any climate; state explicitly when used
- Desertification → land degradation within drylands only
- Drought → precipitation-deficit hazard causing hydrological imbalance
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 23, Basic Section 2 and UNCCD Article 1)
**Links:** ENV-4056, ENV-4057, ENV-4058, ENV-4073
**Trap:** Desertification as the physical spread of deserts. It refers to degradation processes within existing drylands.
**Mains hook:** Define the treaty term first, then narrow to desertification, and treat drought as a hazard rather than proof of permanent degradation.
**Back:** *"Land degrades; drylands desert; drought dries but may not stay."*

### Flashcard ENV-4056 — Drylands and the aridity index (0.05–0.65)
**Front:** What is the UNCCD dryland aridity-index range and which lands are excluded?
**Definition (learn):** Drylands are areas other than polar and sub-polar regions where the ratio of annual precipitation to potential evapotranspiration is from 0.05 to 0.65: arid 0.05 to less than 0.20, semi-arid 0.20 to less than 0.50, and dry sub-humid 0.50 to 0.65.
**Breakdown:**
- Arid → 0.05 to <0.20
- Semi-arid → 0.20 to <0.50
- Dry sub-humid → 0.50 to 0.65
- Excluded → polar and sub-polar regions; hyper-arid land below 0.05 is outside the treaty range
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 23, Basic Section 1 and 2)
**Links:** ENV-4055, ENV-4057
**Trap:** Including hyper-arid land (below 0.05) in the Convention's dryland definition.
**Mains hook:** The index boundaries explain why hyper-arid deserts are not the treaty's desertification subject, but semi-arid margins often are.
**Back:** *"Point oh-five to point six-five, arid to dry sub-humid: the UNCCD dryland band."*

### Flashcard ENV-4057 — Drought vs desertification: hazard vs land condition
**Front:** Why is a drought episode alone not proof of desertification?
**Definition (learn):** Drought is a naturally occurring, significant below-normal precipitation that causes serious hydrological imbalance. It can persist for years, but desertification requires land degradation in drylands, which must be shown by trend analysis, not a single dry episode.
**Breakdown:**
- Drought → hazard, event-based
- Desertification → land condition, trend-based
- Multi-year trend and field evidence separate them
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 23, Basic Section 2; advanced Section 3 on multi-year trend analysis)
**Links:** ENV-4055, ENV-4056, ENV-4075
**Trap:** "Drought and desertification are the same phenomenon." They are different, though linked.
**Mains hook:** Drought is a risk signal; recovery or persistence is the land-condition question.
**Back:** *"Drought is a dry season; desertification is a dry trend."*

### Flashcard ENV-4058 — UNCCD (1994): objective, origin and seat
**Front:** What is the UNCCD's objective, when was it adopted and where is its secretariat?
**Definition (learn):** The UN Convention to Combat Desertification is a binding treaty whose objective is to combat desertification and mitigate drought effects in countries experiencing serious drought and/or desertification, especially in Africa.
**Breakdown:**
- Adopted in Paris, France, on 17 June 1994 (✅ Wikipedia)
- In force in December 1996 (✅ Wikipedia; infobox 26 December 1996)
- Secretariat in Bonn, Germany, since January 1999 (✅ Wikipedia)
- Parties: 197 at fetch time; as-of date not stated, so ⚠️ do not quote as current
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 17 June 1994, Paris; December 1996; Bonn (✅ Wikipedia; owner 23 Section 4 names the Bonn Secretariat).
**Links:** ENV-4059, ENV-4063, ENV-4064
**Trap:** Thinking the UNCCD is a non-binding soft-law instrument. It is a binding treaty, but its national LDN targets are voluntary.
**Mains hook:** The UNCCD is binding in structure but leaves LDN target-setting to national choice, which is a key design trade-off.
**Back:** *"Paris in ninety-four, Bonn for the seat, binding treaty, voluntary LDN targets."*

### Flashcard ENV-4059 — The three Rio Conventions: UNFCCC, CBD and UNCCD
**Front:** Which three conventions originated from the 1992 Rio Earth Summit, and how is the UNCCD distinct?
**Definition (learn):** The UNFCCC, CBD and UNCCD are the three Rio Conventions. The UNCCD (1994) specifically addresses dryland land degradation through national action programmes and international cooperation, with historical attention to Africa's Sahel.
**Breakdown:**
- UNFCCC → climate
- CBD → biodiversity (ENV-4029)
- UNCCD → desertification and land degradation in drylands
- Only Rio Convention with region-specific annexes (owner 23): Africa, Asia, Latin America and the Caribbean, Northern Mediterranean, Central and Eastern Europe
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Rio Earth Summit 1992; UNCCD 1994 (✅ owner 23). Annex list named by owner; the Wikipedia page did not list annexes (⚠️ confirm against UNCCD text).
**Links:** ENV-4029, ENV-4058, ENV-4028
**Trap:** "The UNCCD is not a Rio Convention." It is one of the three.
**Mains hook:** The UNCCD brings a land lens that links climate and biodiversity outcomes, since land degradation undermines carbon sinks and habitats.
**Back:** *"Climate, biodiversity, land: three Rio children from one 1992 summit."*

### Flashcard ENV-4060 — LDN: avoid, reduce, reverse; baseline as minimum
**Front:** What is Land Degradation Neutrality and what is its response hierarchy?
**Definition (learn):** LDN is a state in which the amount and quality of land resources needed for ecosystem functions, services and food security remain stable or increase against a baseline within specified scales. Its response hierarchy is avoid new degradation, reduce ongoing degradation, then reverse past degradation. The baseline is the minimum target.
**Breakdown:**
- Avoid → prevent new degradation (priority)
- Reduce → cut ongoing degradation
- Reverse → restore past degradation
- Target = baseline (minimum); countries may aim for net gain
- National targets are voluntary, measurable, time-bound and geographically explicit
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 23, Basic Sections 1, 2, 3; advanced Section 2)
**Links:** ENV-4061, ENV-4062, ENV-4074
**Trap:** LDN as a restoration-first framework. It is prevention-first.
**Mains hook:** LDN is credible only when baseline, scale, timeframe, land type, restoration quality and livelihood effects are monitored.
**Back:** *"Avoid first, reduce next, reverse last; the baseline is the floor, not the goal."*

### Flashcard ENV-4061 — LDN counterbalancing: a constrained safeguard, not an offset market
**Front:** When and how may losses be counterbalanced under LDN?
**Definition (learn):** Counterbalancing is a constrained land-use planning safeguard used only after the avoid-reduce-reverse hierarchy. Anticipated losses and planned gains should share a timeframe and planning scale and, where possible, be like for like within the same ecosystem-based land type.
**Breakdown:**
- Applied after avoid and reduce
- Same timeframe and planning scale
- Like for like within ecosystem-based land type
- Prospective, not retrospective
- Not a tradable-offset market or permission to cause avoidable damage
- Analytical parallel (⚠️): compensatory afforestation (CAMPA) faces an ecological-commensurability risk; LDN is not the same instrument
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 23, Basic Section 3.4; advanced Section 2.3–2.4)
**Links:** ENV-4060, ENV-4062, ENV-4074
**Trap:** "LDN permits degradation wherever restoration is financed elsewhere." Avoidance and reduction have priority.
**Mains hook:** Equal area does not equal equal soil, water, biodiversity or livelihood function; this is the ecological-commensurability risk to challenge.
**Back:** *"Count like for like, same land, same time, never a licence to degrade."*

### Flashcard ENV-4062 — SDG 15.3.1 and the one-out-all-out rule
**Front:** How is SDG indicator 15.3.1 calculated and what are its reporting periods?
**Definition (learn):** SDG 15.3.1 is the proportion of land that is degraded over total land area, built from three sub-indicators: land-cover change, land-productivity dynamics and soil-organic-carbon stock. Under one-out-all-out, significant negative change in any one classifies a unit as degraded. A gain needs at least one significant positive change and no significant negative change.
**Breakdown:**
- Sub-indicator 1 → land-cover change
- Sub-indicator 2 → land-productivity dynamics
- Sub-indicator 3 → soil organic carbon stock
- One-out-all-out → any significant negative change = degraded
- SDG reporting baseline: 2000–2015
- 2026 PRAIS cycle: assessment period 2016–2023, with baseline and 2019 estimates recalculated where needed
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 23, Basic Section 6; advanced Section 3)
**Links:** ENV-4060, ENV-4061, ENV-4063
**Trap:** Treating the LDN conceptual baseline and the SDG reporting baseline as the same. The reporting period is 2000–2015.
**Mains hook:** Cite the indicator method when quoting land-degradation figures, since national interpretation and thresholds still matter.
**Back:** *"Cover, productivity, carbon: any single fall flags the land."*

### Flashcard ENV-4063 — UNCCD 2018–2030 Strategic Framework: five objectives
**Front:** What are the five strategic objectives of the UNCCD 2018–2030 Strategic Framework?
**Definition (learn):** Adopted through decision 7/COP.13, the framework sets five objectives: improve affected ecosystems and contribute to LDN; improve living conditions of affected populations; mitigate, adapt to and manage drought; generate global environmental benefits; and mobilise substantial additional resources through partnerships.
**Breakdown:**
- SO1 → affected ecosystems, SLM and LDN
- SO2 → living conditions of affected populations
- SO3 → drought mitigation, adaptation and management
- SO4 → global environmental benefits
- SO5 → mobilise financial and non-financial resources
- Three core components: policy and planning; actions on the ground; financial and non-financial resources
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Decision 7/COP.13 (✅ owner 23, Section 4). Framework runs to 2030 and remains current (✅ owner 23, COP17 decision 6).
**Links:** ENV-4062, ENV-4064, ENV-4069
**Trap:** Confusing the Strategic Framework's five objectives with the LDN response hierarchy. They are different frameworks.
**Mains hook:** Place India's watershed and restoration policies under SO1 and SO5; judge them by ecosystem outcomes, not spend alone.
**Back:** *"Ecosystems, people, drought, global benefits, finance: five strategic objectives to 2030."*

### Flashcard ENV-4064 — UNCCD institutions: COP, CST, CRIC, Global Mechanism and NAPs
**Front:** What are the main UNCCD institutions and instruments?
**Definition (learn):** The COP is the supreme decision-making body; the Committee on Science and Technology (CST) advises on science, supported by the Science-Policy Interface; the Committee for the Review of the Implementation of the Convention (CRIC) reviews national reporting; the Secretariat in Bonn administers the process; the Global Mechanism supports resource mobilisation; National Action Programmes (Article 10) are affected-country instruments.
**Breakdown:**
- COP → supreme body
- CST → science and technology advice
- CRIC → implementation and national-report review
- Secretariat (Bonn) → services and administration
- Global Mechanism → resource mobilisation, investment, partnership (not an automatic grant fund)
- National Action Programmes → Article 10
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 23, Section 4)
**Links:** ENV-4058, ENV-4063, ENV-4065
**Trap:** Treating the Global Mechanism as a project-grant fund. It supports resource mobilisation and partnerships.
**Mains hook:** Institutional design determines whether reporting becomes accountability; CRIC is the review channel.
**Back:** *"COP decides, CST advises, CRIC checks, Bonn runs, GM raises money, NAPs act."*

### Flashcard ENV-4065 — India's UNCCD focal machinery: MoEFCC Desertification Cell, ISRO-SAC and DoLR
**Front:** Which Indian bodies handle UNCCD reporting, mapping and implementation?
**Definition (learn):** The MoEFCC Desertification Cell is India's nodal cell for UNCCD reporting and inter-ministerial coordination. ISRO's Space Applications Centre (SAC) leads the national remote-sensing desertification and land-degradation atlas. The Department of Land Resources (DoLR) is a major implementation partner for watershed and land-resource programmes but is not the UNCCD national focal cell. ICFRE supports forestry and restoration knowledge.
**Breakdown:**
- MoEFCC Desertification Cell → nodal reporting and coordination
- ISRO-SAC → Desertification and Land Degradation Atlas of India
- DoLR → watershed and land-resource programmes
- ICFRE → forestry and restoration planning
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** (✅ owner 23, Section 4)
**Links:** ENV-4064, ENV-4066, ENV-4067
**Trap:** Naming DoLR as the UNCCD focal cell. The focal cell is the MoEFCC Desertification Cell.
**Mains hook:** Map each domestic body to its role to show that reporting, measurement and implementation sit in different institutions.
**Back:** *"Environment cell reports, SAC maps, Land Resources implements watersheds."*

### Flashcard ENV-4066 — India's National Action Plans to Combat Desertification (2001, 2023)
**Front:** What are India's two national action plans to combat desertification and how do they differ?
**Definition (learn):** India prepared its original National Action Plan to Combat Desertification in 2001. The 2023 National Action Plan to Combat Desertification and Land Degradation through Forestry Interventions updates it, but signals that agriculture, water, rangeland, mining and tenure measures remain necessary beyond forestry.
**Breakdown:**
- 2001 → original NAP
- 2023 → updated NAP, forestry-framed
- Beyond forestry → agriculture, water, rangeland, mining, tenure (✅ owner 23)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2001; 2023 (✅ owner 23, source: UNCCD-hosted NAP India 2023 PDF cited in owner).
**Links:** ENV-4065, ENV-4067, ENV-4068
**Trap:** Treating the 2023 NAP as only a forest plan. It names non-forest measures too.
**Mains hook:** A forestry-framed plan can miss soil, water and rangeland governance; tie NAP to multi-sector measures.
**Back:** *"Two thousand one began it; twenty twenty-three forests it, but not only forests."*

### Flashcard ENV-4067 — ISRO-SAC Atlas 2021: 97.85 million hectares (2018–19)
**Front:** What does the ISRO-SAC Desertification and Land Degradation Atlas of India (2021) report, and for which year?
**Definition (learn):** The 2021 atlas maps land condition for 2018–19: 97.85 million hectares, or 29.77% of India's total geographical area, under desertification and land degradation, including 83.69 million hectares undergoing desertification within drylands.
**Breakdown:**
- Total → 97.85 million ha (29.77% of geographical area)
- Within drylands → 83.69 million ha undergoing desertification
- Reference period → 2018–19 (not 2021 or 2026)
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 97.85 Mha; 29.77%; 83.69 Mha; 2018–19 (✅ owner 23, ISRO-SAC source cited in owner; not fetched).
**Links:** ENV-4065, ENV-4068, ENV-4076
**Trap:** Quoting the atlas as a 2021 measurement or as a 2026 land-condition estimate.
**Mains hook:** Multi-year, ground-truthed atlases are the evidence base for India's dryland policy; cite edition and year.
**Back:** *"Ninety-seven point eight-five million hectares, as seen in 2018-19."*

### Flashcard ENV-4068 — India's restoration ambition: 26 Mha by 2030 and the 2026 PIB update
**Front:** What is India's COP14 restoration ambition and what did the 17 June 2026 PIB release report?
**Definition (learn):** India's COP14 commitment raised its restoration target from 21 million hectares to 26 million hectares by 2030, also framed through the Bonn Challenge.
